Doubly Robust Proxy Causal Learning with Neural Mean Embeddings
Bariscan Bozkurt, Alexandre Galashov, Dimitri Meunier, Zikai Shen, Arthur Gretton, Houssam Zenati University College London

Abstract
Unobserved confounding prevents standard covariate adjustment from identifying causal response functions in observational studies. Proxy causal learning addresses this problem through bridge equations involving treatment- and outcome-inducing proxies, avoiding direct recovery of the latent confounder. Existing doubly robust proxy estimators combine outcome and treatment bridges, but typically rely on fixed kernels, sieves, or low-dimensional semiparametric models; existing neural proxy methods are more flexible, but are largely single-bridge estimators. We develop a neural doubly robust framework for proxy causal learning with continuous and structured treatments. Our method introduces a neural mean-embedding estimator for the treatment bridge, combines it with a neural outcome bridge, and estimates the doubly robust correction through a final regression stage. The framework covers population, heterogeneous, and conditional dose-response functions, yielding full response-curve estimators rather than binary-treatment effects. The algorithms use two stages for each bridge and history-aware updates of the final linear layers to stabilize stochastic multi-stage training. We prove consistency of the algorithms showing that the doubly robust error is controlled by the final averaging and regression errors together with the smaller of the outcome- and treatment-side weak-norm bridge errors. Across synthetic and image-valued benchmarks, the proposed estimators outperform existing baselines and single-bridge neural estimators, showing the benefit of combining learned outcome and treatment bridges in a doubly robust construction. Our implementation is available at https://github.com/BariscanBozkurt/DRPCL-Neural-Mean-Embedding .



1
Introduction and related works

Causal inference from observational data is difficult when treatment assignment is confounded. Standard adjustment assumes that all relevant confounders are observed, yielding
Y^{(a)}\perp A\mid X
under consistency, no interference, and positivity. This condition underlies covariate adjustment and propensity-score methods
[
42
,
41
,
23
]
, outcome-regression methods
[
21
]
, and representation-learning estimators for treatment effects
[
24
,
56
]
. In practice, however, important determinants of treatment and outcome may be unrecorded, noisy, or only indirectly measured. Instrumental-variable methods offer one route beyond observed-confounder adjustment
[
39
,
47
,
37
]
, but require exclusion and independence assumptions that are often restrictive.

Proxy causal learning (PCL) uses a different source of information: proxy variables that are informative about the unobserved confounding
[
30
,
36
,
49
]
. In the standard PCL setting, observed variables are partitioned into covariates
X
, treatment proxies
Z
, and outcome proxies
W
. As illustrated in Figure
1
, the treatment
A
and outcome
Y
are confounded by observed covariates
X
and latent variables
U
, while
Z
and
W
provide treatment-side and outcome-side information about the same latent confounding. Under conditional independence and completeness assumptions, causal effects can be identified without recovering
U
directly.
Identification is instead expressed through bridge functions: functions of the observed proxies, treatment, and covariates whose conditional expectations reproduce the relevant regression or weighting equations. Learning these bridges replaces latent-confounder inference by the solution of Fredholm integral equations of the first kind.

Existing PCL estimators mainly follow outcome-bridge or treatment-bridge routes. Outcome-bridge methods learn a function
h(A,X,W)
satisfying

\mathbb{E}\{Y-h(A,X,W)\mid A,X,Z\}=0,

and then recover response curves by averaging or conditioning the learned bridge.
Linear and semiparametric bridge estimators were developed in proximal causal inference
[
49
,
15
]
; sieve and proxy-control estimators were studied by
16
; and RKHS-based methods provide flexible nonparametric bridge estimators
[
33
,
45
]
. Neural outcome-bridge estimators replace fixed features with learned representations, improving scalability to nonlinear and high-dimensional settings
[
55
,
29
]
. These methods estimate the regression-type bridge
h(A,X,W)
, but leave open the neural estimation of the complementary weighting-type bridge based on
Z
.

U
Z
X
W
A
Y

Figure 1
:
Causal graph for PCL setting.

Treatment bridges provide the complementary weighting route. For a given causal estimand, a treatment bridge
\varphi(A,X,Z)
is characterized by a moment equation of the form

\mathbb{E}\{r_{0}(A,X,W)-\varphi(A,X,Z)\mid A,X,W\}=0,

where
r_{0}
denotes the estimand specific density ratio; for the population dose-response curve,
r_{0}(a,x,w)=p_{A}(a)/p_{A\mid X,W}(a\mid x,w)
. This makes treatment bridges analogous to inverse-weighting functions
[
41
,
40
]
.
Semiparametric
[
15
]
, minimax
[
25
]
, and kernel-based treatment-bridge estimators
[
7
]
have been developed for proxy causal inference, including extensions to continuous treatments. Recent neural treatment-bridge work moves toward adaptive treatment-side estimation
[
58
]
, but is developed for binary treatment effects rather than full response functions with continuous or structured treatments.

Doubly robust PCL combines the outcome- and treatment-bridge routes, mirroring classical doubly robust causal inference, where validity can be retained when one of two complementary components is correctly specified
[
3
,
13
,
27
]
. This is especially useful in PCL because the two bridges use the proxies differently, and it is rarely known in advance whether
W
or
Z
is more informative about the latent confounding. Semiparametric and kernel doubly robust proxy estimators have been developed
[
15
,
51
,
8
]
, but they are either formulated for discrete-treatment contrasts or rely on fixed kernel representations for continuous treatments. This leaves open a neural doubly robust PCL framework that learns both outcome- and treatment-side representations for continuous and structured response functions.

We develop a neural doubly robust PCL framework for estimating causal response functions, including population, heterogeneous, and conditional dose-response targets. On the outcome side, we build on two-stage neural bridge regression. On the treatment side, we introduce a neural mean-embedding estimator that learns conditional embeddings of the treatment proxy using adaptive features. We then combine the two learned bridges through a final regression stage that estimates the doubly robust correction. The resulting framework covers population dose-response, heterogeneous dose-response, and conditional dose-response targets, and is designed for continuous and structured treatments. To stabilize stochastic multi-stage training, we use history-aware updates of the final linear heads, inspired by
18
.

Our contributions are:

•

We extend doubly robust bridge identification beyond population dose-response curves to heterogeneous and conditional response functions.

•

We introduce a neural mean-embedding treatment bridge and combine it with a neural outcome bridge to obtain a doubly robust PCL estimator for continuous and structured treatments.

•

We propose DRPCLNET-V1 and DRPCLNET-V2, two stable multi-stage training algorithms with history-aware closed-form linear-head updates and show significant practical gains over single-bridge neural estimators and existing proxy baselines on synthetic and image-valued benchmarks.

•

We prove the consistency of our proposed estimators showing that the doubly robust error decomposes into final averaging/regression errors and the smaller of the outcome- and treatment-side weak-norm bridge errors.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. Section
2
introduces the causal targets. Section
3
gives outcome-bridge, treatment-bridge, and doubly robust identification results. Section
4
presents the neural mean embedding estimators and the resulting doubly robust algorithms. Section
6
reports the numerical experiments, and Section
7
concludes.

2
Problem setup and target causal functions

This section introduces the causal targets studied in the paper within the proxy causal learning framework shown in Figure
1
.

Notation.

Uppercase letters denote random variables and calligraphic letters denote their state spaces; lowercase letters denote realizations. We observe
O=(Y,A,X,Z,W)\sim\mathbb{P}
, where
A\in{\mathcal{A}}
is the treatment,
Y\in{\mathcal{Y}}
is the outcome,
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
denotes observed covariates,
Z\in{\mathcal{Z}}
is a treatment proxy, and
W\in{\mathcal{W}}
is an outcome proxy. We write
{\mathbb{P}}_{R}
and
{\mathbb{P}}_{R\mid T}
for marginal probability laws and conditional laws, and
\mathbb{E}[\cdot]
for expectation. When densities or probability mass functions exist, we write them with lowercase
p
, for example
p_{A}(a)
and
p_{A\mid X,W}(a\mid x,w)
.

Let
A\in{\mathcal{A}}
denote the treatment, which may be continuous and high dimensional, and let
Y\in{\mathcal{Y}}
denote the observed outcome. Let
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
denote the vector of observed covariates, and let
U\in{\mathcal{U}}
denote an unobserved confounder.
Write
X=(S,V)
, where
V\in{\mathcal{V}}
is the pre-specified part of the observed covariates used to define groups, and
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
contains the remaining observed covariates. For example, in a clinical study,
V
could include age, sex, or baseline severity. A value
V=v
defines the group whose response curve we want to estimate. Our goal is to identify and estimate causal functions describing how the outcome changes under interventions on
A
, both at the population level and within such
V=v
groups. When this decomposition is not needed, we write
X
for the full observed covariate vector. We now define the causal targets studied in the paper.

Definition 2.1

(Target causal parameters)
.

Let
Y^{(a)}
denote the potential outcome under the intervention
A=a
. We consider:

(i)

Dose-response curve:

f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a):=\mathbb{E}[Y^{(a)}]
.

(ii)

Heterogeneous dose-response:

f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v):=\mathbb{E}[Y^{(a)}\mid V=v]
.

(iii)

Conditional dose-response:

f_{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime}):=\mathbb{E}[Y^{(a)}\mid A=a^{\prime}]
.

The subscripts follow standard discrete-treatment terminology: ATE, CATE, and ATT refer to average, conditional average, and average-on-the-treated treatment effects. Here, they index the corresponding response functions rather than discrete-treatment contrasts. Under consistency,
Y^{(a)}=Y
whenever
A=a
, and latent exchangeability,
Y^{(a)}\perp A\mid X,U
, these targets admit the latent-variable representations: (i)
f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[Y\mid A=a,X,U]]
, (ii)
f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v)=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[Y\mid A=a,S,V=v,U]\mid V=v]
, and (iii)
f_{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[Y\mid A=a,X,U]\mid A=a^{\prime}]
. These representations show that the causal functions would be identified by adjustment if the latent confounder
U
were observed. Proxy causal learning instead identifies the same functions without observing or estimating
U
, by using the proxies
Z
and
W
to learn bridge functions.

Intuitively,
Z
is informative about the latent confounding through treatment assignment, while
W
is informative about the same latent confounding through the outcome. We formalize these proxy roles through the following conditional independence assumptions, and then impose completeness to ensure that the proxies carry enough information for bridge identification.

Assumption 2.2

(Proxy conditional independencies)
.

Assume that the data are generated by a structural causal model compatible with Figure
1
, and that the following conditional independencies hold: (i)
Y\perp Z\mid U,A,X
, (ii)
W\perp Z\mid U,A,X,
and
W\perp A\mid U,X.

Assumption
2.2
formalizes the roles of
Z
and
W
as treatment and outcome proxies, respectively. In addition, identification requires that these proxies be sufficiently informative about the latent confounder
U
conditional on
(A,X)

[
36
,
45
]
. This is captured by the following completeness assumption.

Assumption 2.3

(Completeness)
.

For
{\mathbb{P}}_{A,X}
-almost all
(a,x)\in{\mathcal{A}}\times{\mathcal{X}}
, and for every square-integrable function
\ell\in L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{U\mid A=a,X=x})
: (i)
\mathbb{E}[\ell(U)\mid A=a,X=x,Z]=0\kern 5.0pt{\mathbb{P}}_{Z\mid A=a,X=x}\text{-a.e.}\iff\ell(U)=0\kern 5.0pt{\mathbb{P}}_{U\mid A=a,X=x}\text{-a.e.},
(ii)
\mathbb{E}[\ell(U)\mid A=a,X=x,W]=0\kern 5.0pt{\mathbb{P}}_{W\mid A=a,X=x}\text{-a.e.}\iff\ell(U)=0\kern 5.0pt{\mathbb{P}}_{U\mid A=a,X=x}\text{-a.e.}.

In the next section, we show how these assumptions lead to outcome-bridge, treatment-bridge, and doubly robust identification formulas for the target causal functions.

3
Identification of causal functions

This section gives identification formulas for the causal functions in Definition
2.1
. We first review the outcome-bridge route, then introduce treatment bridges as inverse-weighting analogues, and finally combine both routes to obtain doubly robust formulas.

3.1
Outcome bridge identification

Outcome-bridge identification is the standard route in PCL
[
36
,
33
,
55
,
29
]
: it seeks a function
h(A,X,W)
such that
\mathbb{E}[h(A,X,W)\mid A,X,Z]=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid A,X,Z]
. Once such a bridge exists, response functions are obtained by averaging or conditioning
h
over the relevant observed distribution.

Theorem 3.1

(Identification via the outcome bridge)
.

Suppose Assumptions
2.2
and
2.3
hold. Furthermore, suppose that for
{\mathbb{P}}_{A,X}
-almost all
(a,x)\in{\mathcal{A}}\times{\mathcal{X}}
, there exists an outcome bridge function
h_{0}(a,x,\cdot)\in L^{2}\!\left({\mathbb{P}}_{W\mid A=a,X=x}\right)

such that

\mathbb{E}[Y\mid A=a,X=x,Z]=\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,x,W)\mid A=a,X=x,Z],\qquad{\mathbb{P}}_{Z\mid A=a,X=x}\text{-a.e.}

(1)

Then, the causal functions are identified by (i)
f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)]
, (ii)
f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v)=\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)\mid V=v]
, (iii)
f_{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)\mid A=a^{\prime}]
.

The dose-response formula is the standard proxy outcome-bridge result of
36
. The heterogeneous and conditional formulas follow by conditioning the same bridge representation on
V=v
or
A=a^{\prime}
, as in
45
.

3.2
Treatment bridge identification

We next turn to treatment bridge identification, which complements the outcome-bridge view and is closer in spirit to inverse propensity weighting
[
41
,
40
]
. This perspective has been developed in semiparametric, minimax, and kernel forms, including recent extensions to continuous treatments and density-ratio-free formulations
[
15
,
25
,
51
,
7
,
8
]
. It is also the identification route that will motivate our treatment-side estimator.

Theorem 3.2

(Identification via treatment bridge)
.

Suppose Assumptions
2.2
and
2.3
hold. Assume that for
{\mathbb{P}}_{A,X}
-almost all
(a,x)\in{\mathcal{A}}\times{\mathcal{X}}
, there exist treatment bridge functions
\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{ATE}}(a,x,\cdot)\in L^{2}\!\left({\mathbb{P}}_{Z\mid A=a,X=x}\right)
and, for any
a^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{A}}
,
\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime},x,\cdot)\in L^{2}\!\left({\mathbb{P}}_{Z\mid A=a,X=x}\right)
. In addition, assume that for
{\mathbb{P}}_{A,S,V}
-almost all
(a,s,v)\in{\mathcal{A}}\times{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{V}}
, there exists
\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v,s,\cdot)\in L^{2}\!\left({\mathbb{P}}_{Z\mid A=a,S=s,V=v}\right)
. Suppose these bridge functions satisfy, almost everywhere with respect to the corresponding conditional law of
W
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\varphi^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a,X,W]=p_{A}(a)/p_{A\mid X,W}(a\mid X,W),

(2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\varphi^{\mathrm{CATE}}_{0}(a,v,S,Z)\mid A=a,S,V=v,W]=p_{A\mid V}(a\mid V=v)/p_{A\mid S,V,W}(a\mid S,V=v,W),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\varphi^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)\mid A=a,X,W]=p_{X,W\mid A}(X,W\mid a^{\prime})/p_{X,W\mid A}(X,W\mid a).

Then, the causal functions are identified by (i)
f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a]
, (ii)
f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v)=\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi^{\mathrm{CATE}}_{0}(a,v,S,Z)\mid A=a,V=v]
, (iii)
f_{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)\mid A=a].

The ATE and ATT statements follow from the treatment-bridge identification results of
7
. We prove the CATE extension in Appendix
A
.

3.3
Doubly robust identification

Combining outcome and treatment bridges yields doubly robust formulas that remain valid when either bridge is correctly specified and underlie semiparametric and kernel DR proxy methods
[
15
,
51
,
8
]
. The population dose-response formula recovers existing results; the heterogeneous and conditional formulas are new extensions developed here.

The doubly robust identification theorem below extends the bridge structure that appears in the efficient influence functions derived in Appendix
C
for discrete treatments. That appendix treats
A
as discrete, and
V
as discrete for the heterogeneous target. Here, we state the corresponding bridge identities directly as identification formulas for general treatments and covariates, including continuous
A
and
V
.

Theorem 3.3

(Doubly robust identification)
.

Suppose Assumptions
2.2
and
2.3
hold, and suppose that the outcome bridge
h_{0}
from Theorem
3.1
and the treatment bridge functions from Theorem
3.2
exist. Then the target causal functions admit the doubly robust representations

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR)}}(a;h_{0},\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{ATE}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{ATE}}(a,X,Z)\{Y-h_{0}(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a]+\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)],

(3)

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR)}}(a,v;h_{0},\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{CATE}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v,S,Z)\{Y-h_{0}(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a,V=v]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)\mid V=v],

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{\mathrm{(DR)}}(a,a^{\prime};h_{0},\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{ATT}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)\{Y-h_{0}(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)\mid A=a^{\prime}].

Moreover, each formula identifies the corresponding causal function if either
h_{0}
is a valid outcome bridge or the corresponding treatment bridge function is valid.

The ATE representation in Eq.
3
is the covariate-adjusted analogue of the doubly robust dose-response formula in
8
. The CATE and ATT representations are new extensions; their derivations are given in Appendix
B
.

4
Neural mean embedding approach for dose-response estimation

We introduce neural estimators for dose-response estimation that retain the staged bridge-learning structure of proxy identification while replacing the fixed kernel and sieve feature maps used in existing estimators
[
33
,
45
,
7
]
with adaptive trainable representations; fixed-feature baselines are reviewed in Appendix
D
. The framework consists of an outcome-bridge network, a treatment-bridge network, and a final doubly robust regression stage. We give the core parameterizations and objectives here, and defer stochastic training details and update rules to Appendices
E.1
,
E.2
, and
E.3
.

4.0.0.0.1
Setup and notation.

Both bridge estimators use a two-stage regression structure followed by a final third stage. We use superscript
(h)
for the outcome bridge and
(\varphi)
for the treatment bridge. Given a dataset
\mathcal{D}=\{(a_{i},x_{i},z_{i},w_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, we form two disjoint folds for each bridge. For the outcome bridge, we split it into
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(h)}=\{(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{x}_{i},\bar{z}_{i},\bar{w}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{h}}
, in which the outcome proxy
W
is observed, and
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(h)}=\{(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i},\tilde{y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m_{h}}
, in which the outcome
Y
is observed. For the treatment bridge, we split it into
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(\varphi)}=\{(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{x}_{i},\bar{z}_{i},\bar{w}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\varphi}}
, where similarly
W
is observed, and
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(\varphi)}=\{(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i},\hat{r}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m_{\varphi}}
which additionally includes pre-computed estimates
\hat{r}_{i}=\hat{r}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i})
of the density ratio
r(a,x,w)=p_{A}(a)/p_{A\mid X,W}(a\mid x,w)
, see below for more details.

4.0.0.0.2
Outcome bridge network.

OutcomeNet targets the outcome bridge (Eq.
1
) and follows the two-stage neural bridge structure of
53
,
55
. We add history-centered proximal penalties on the final linear heads to stabilize mini-batch training. We parameterize the bridge – where
\phi_{AX,2}^{(h)}(a,x):=\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(a)\otimes\phi_{X,2}^{(h)}(x)
– and the first-stage conditional embedding as

\displaystyle h(a,x,w)

\displaystyle={\bm{h}}^{\top}\left(\phi_{AX,2}^{(h)}(a,x)\otimes\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(w)\right),

\displaystyle\mu_{W}^{(h)}(a,x,z)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}[\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(W)\mid A=a,X=x,Z=z]=\left({\bm{V}}^{(h)}\right)^{\top}\phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(a,x,z).

Here, the subscript
1
denotes first-stage embedding features, while the subscript
2
denotes second-stage bridge features. The first stage learns the (outcome) proxy embedding by minimizing over
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(h)}
:

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{h,1}(\theta_{1}^{(h)},{\bm{V}}^{(h)})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{h}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{h}}\left\|\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(\bar{w}_{i})-({\bm{V}}^{(h)})^{\top}\phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{x}_{i},\bar{z}_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{1}^{(h)}\left\|{\bm{V}}^{(h)}-\hat{{\bm{V}}}_{t}^{(h)}\right\|_{F}^{2}.

Here,
\theta_{1}^{(h)}
denotes the parameters of the first-stage neural network
\phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}
. Minimizing it yields an estimate
\hat{\mu}_{W}^{(h)}(a,x,z)
. Then, the second stage fits the outcome bridge over
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(h)}
, replacing the unobserved
\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(w)
with this estimate:

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{h,2}(\theta_{2}^{(h)},{\bm{h}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m_{h}}\sum_{i=1}^{m_{h}}\ell_{h,2}\!\left(\tilde{y}_{i},\,{\bm{h}}^{\top}\left[\phi_{AX,2}^{(h)}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i})\otimes\hat{\mu}_{W}^{(h)}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i})\right]\right)+\lambda_{2}^{(h)}\left\|{\bm{h}}-\hat{{\bm{h}}}_{t}\right\|_{2}^{2},

where
\theta_{2}^{(h)}
collects the parameters of the second-stage networks
\phi_{AX,2}^{(h)}
and
\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}
. The history-centered penalties keep the current linear heads close to their previous iterates and stabilize stochastic mini-batch training. Once
\hat{h}
is learned, we estimate the outcome-bridge dose-response by the empirical average over an evaluation sample

\mathcal{D}_{3}^{(h)}=\{(x_{i}^{\circ},w_{i}^{\circ})\}_{i=1}^{t_{h}}
:

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{(h)}(a)=\frac{1}{t_{h}}\sum_{i=1}^{t_{h}}\hat{h}(a,x_{i}^{\circ},w_{i}^{\circ}),

(4)

4.0.0.0.3
Treatment bridge network.

TreatmentNet targets the treatment bridge (Eq.
2
).
We parameterize the treatment bridge and first-stage conditional embedding as

\displaystyle\varphi(a,x,z)

\displaystyle=\bm{\varphi}^{\top}\left(\phi_{AX,2}^{(\varphi)}(a,x)\otimes\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(z)\right),

\displaystyle\mu_{Z}^{(\varphi)}(a,x,w)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}[\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(Z)\mid A=a,X=x,W=w]=\left({\bm{V}}^{(\varphi)}\right)^{\top}\phi_{AXW,1}^{(\varphi)}(a,x,w).

For the population dose-response curve, the treatment-bridge target is
r(a,x,w)=p_{A}(a)/p_{A\mid X,W}(a\mid x,w)
and we denote by
\hat{r}
its estimate (see above). The first stage learns the (treatment) proxy embedding by minimizing over
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(\varphi)}
:

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\varphi,1}(\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)},{\bm{V}}^{(\varphi)})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{\varphi}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\varphi}}\left\|\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(\bar{z}_{i})-({\bm{V}}^{(\varphi)})^{\top}\phi_{AXW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{x}_{i},\bar{w}_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{1}^{(\varphi)}\left\|{\bm{V}}^{(\varphi)}-\hat{{\bm{V}}}_{t}^{(\varphi)}\right\|_{F}^{2}.

Minimizing it yields an estimate
\hat{\mu}_{Z}^{(\varphi)}
. Then, the second stage fits the treatment bridge over
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(\varphi)}
, regressing
\hat{r}
onto the learned proxy embedding:

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\varphi,2}(\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)},\bm{\varphi})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m_{\varphi}}\sum_{i=1}^{m_{\varphi}}\ell_{\varphi,2}\!\left(\hat{r}_{i},\,\bm{\varphi}^{\top}\left[\phi_{AX,2}^{(\varphi)}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i})\otimes\hat{\mu}_{Z}^{(\varphi)}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i})\right]\right)+\lambda_{2}^{(\varphi)}\left\|\bm{\varphi}-\hat{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

After learning
\hat{\varphi}
, the treatment-bridge dose-response is obtained by a third-stage regression: we form pseudo-outcomes
\{y_{i}^{(\varphi)}\}_{i=1}^{t_{\varphi}}=\{y_{i}\hat{\varphi}(a_{i},x_{i},z_{i})\}_{i=1}^{t_{\varphi}}
and fit
f^{(\varphi)}(\cdot;\theta_{3}^{(\varphi)})
so that

f^{(\varphi)}(a;\theta_{3}^{(\varphi)})\approx\mathbb{E}[Y\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a].

4.0.0.0.4
Training schedule.

Both bridge networks are trained by alternating between updating the featurizers by gradient descent and updating the linear heads by their closed-form history-centered ridge solutions. Importantly, the proxy-side second-stage featurizer,
\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}
for OutcomeNet or
\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}
for TreatmentNet, affects the first-stage regression target. Therefore, during the second-stage feature update, gradients are propagated through the closed-form first-stage solution for
{\bm{V}}
, following the deep feature learning strategy of
55
,
52
. For squared losses, the second-stage linear head has a closed-form history-centered ridge update. For differentiable robust losses such as Huber or log-cosh
[
22
]
, the closed-form ridge update for the linear head is replaced by a small number of L-BFGS steps
[
31
]
. This training schedule preserves the two-stage bridge structure while allowing flexible neural representations and robust second-stage objectives.

4.0.0.0.5
Doubly robust unification.

We combine OutcomeNet and TreatmentNet via Eq.
3
via two novel neural implementations, unlike
8
who use fixed kernel features.

Our first implementation, that we call
Doubly Robust PCL Network-V1
(DRPCLNET-V1), directly learns the bridge-weighted residual correction. Given fitted bridges
\hat{h}
and
\hat{\varphi}
, we construct pseudo-outcomes

y_{i}^{(\kappa,1)}=\hat{\varphi}(a_{i},x_{i},z_{i})\{y_{i}-\hat{h}(a_{i},x_{i},w_{i})\},

and fit a regression network
k^{(\kappa,1)}(\cdot;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa)})
such that

\displaystyle k^{(\kappa,1)}(a;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa)})\approx\mathbb{E}[\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\{Y-\hat{h}(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a].

(5)

The resulting estimator is

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR1)}}(a)=\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{(h)}(a)+k^{(\kappa,1)}(a;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa)}).

Our second implementation,
DRPCLNET-V2
, uses the decomposition

f_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR)}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)]+\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a]-\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)h_{0}(a,X,W)\mid A=a].

OutcomeNet estimates the first term and TreatmentNet estimates the second. We learn only the interaction term by constructing pseudo-outcomes

y_{i}^{(\kappa,2)}=\hat{\varphi}(a_{i},x_{i},z_{i})\hat{h}(a_{i},x_{i},w_{i}),

and fitting
k^{(\kappa,2)}(\cdot;\theta_{2}^{(\kappa)})
to approximate

k^{(\kappa,2)}(a;\theta_{2}^{(\kappa)})\approx\mathbb{E}[\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\hat{h}(a,X,W)\mid A=a].

The resulting estimator is

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR2)}}(a)=\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{(h)}(a)+f^{(\varphi)}(a;\theta_{3}^{(\varphi)})-k^{(\kappa,2)}(a;\theta_{2}^{(\kappa)}).

Full algorithmic details for both variants are given in Appendix
E.3
.

The CATE and ATT estimators follow the same multi-stage construction with target-specific treatment bridges and conditional response regressions. Details are deferred to Appendices
F
and
G
.

5
Consistency results

We present the consistency result for population dose-response estimation, focusing on DRPCLNET-V1. Similarly, DRPCLNET-V2 follows from the same decomposition after replacing the residual correction by the interaction-regression term. Full assumptions, empirical-process definitions, and proofs are deferred to Appendix
H.1
, Appendix
H.3
, Appendix
H.4
, and Appendix
H.5
. The analysis treats the neural estimators as exact minimizers of the sample-split empirical squared-loss objectives defined in the appendix, with first-stage solutions plugged into the second-stage objectives. It establishes consistency in weak bridge norms
[
5
]
; optimization guarantees for the nonconvex stochastic training procedure, along the lines of
12
, are left for future work.

Let
m_{0}(a,x,z):=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid A=a,X=x,Z=z]
and
r_{0}(a,x,w):=p_{A}(a)/p_{A\mid X,W}(a\mid x,w)
. Define the conditional expectation operators
T_{h}h(a,x,z):=\mathbb{E}[h(a,x,W)\mid A=a,X=x,Z=z]
and
T_{\varphi}\varphi(a,x,w):=\mathbb{E}[\varphi(a,x,Z)\mid A=a,X=x,W=w]
.
We measure bridge errors in the weak norms induced by
T_{h}
and
T_{\varphi}
:
\mathcal{R}_{h}^{\mathrm{weak}}:=\|T_{h}\hat{h}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,Z})}^{2}
, which equals
\|T_{h}(\hat{h}-h_{0})\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,Z})}^{2}
when
T_{h}h_{0}=m_{0}
, and
\mathcal{R}_{\varphi}^{\mathrm{weak},\hat{r}}:=\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-\hat{r}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,W})}^{2}
, where
\hat{r}
is the fitted density-ratio target used to train TreatmentNet. We write
\mathcal{E}_{r}:=\|\hat{r}-r_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,W})}^{2}
for the density-ratio estimation error.

Let
\nu_{h}=(n_{h},m_{h})
and
\nu_{\varphi}=(n_{\varphi},m_{\varphi})
denote the stage-wise sample sizes for outcome and treatment networks.
Up to fixed bounded constants, define

\rho_{h,\nu_{h}}:=\kappa_{h,2,\nu_{h}}+\kappa_{h,1,\nu_{h}}+\Delta_{h,1,\nu_{h}}+\Delta_{h,2,\nu_{h}},\qquad\rho_{\varphi,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}:=\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}+\kappa_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}+\Delta_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}+\Delta_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}.

Here
\kappa_{h,1,\nu_{h}}
and
\kappa_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}
are first-stage approximation errors for the neural conditional mean embeddings of
W\mid A,X,Z
and
Z\mid A,X,W
, respectively;
\kappa_{h,2,\nu_{h}}
is the second-stage approximation error for
m_{0}
, and
\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}
is the second-stage approximation error for the fitted ratio target
\hat{r}
. The
\Delta
-terms are empirical-process errors controlled by Rademacher complexities of the corresponding first- and second-stage loss classes, as defined in the appendix; see also
4
and
17
.
By Theorem
H.2
and Theorem
H.11
,
\mathcal{R}_{h}^{\mathrm{weak}}=O_{p}(\rho_{h,\nu_{h}})
and
\mathcal{R}_{\varphi}^{\mathrm{weak},\hat{r}}=O_{p}(\rho_{\varphi,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}})
. Moreover, the oracle treatment-side projected residual satisfies

\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-r_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,W})}^{2}\leq 2\mathcal{R}_{\varphi}^{\mathrm{weak},\hat{r}}+2\mathcal{E}_{r}.

The term
\mathcal{E}_{r}
is the error of the precomputed density-ratio estimator
\hat{r}
; its rate depends on the chosen density-ratio method and is not analyzed in the TreatmentNet bridge-learning bound.

Theorem 5.1

(Dose-response consistency of OutcomeNet, TreatmentNet, and DRPCLNET)
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
H.2
,
Theorem
H.11
,
Theorem
H.21
, and
Theorem
H.27
hold. Let

\rho_{\varphi,3}:=\kappa_{\varphi,3,t_{h}}+\Delta_{\varphi,3,t_{h}}

denote the third-stage regression error for estimating

a\mapsto\mathbb{E}[Y\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a]
in TreatmentNet. Let

\rho_{\mathrm{DR},1}:=\kappa_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}}+\Delta_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}}

denote the final DRPCLNET-V1 regression error for estimating the residual correction in
Eq.
5
. Finally, let
t_{h}
be the
evaluation-sample size in Eq.
4
, so that the
empirical averaging error of OutcomeNet satisfies

\mathcal{E}_{\mu,h}=O_{p}\!\left(t_{h}^{-1}\right).

Then:

(i)

OutcomeNet satisfies

\|\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{(h)}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}=O_{p}\!\left(\rho_{h,\nu_{h}}+t_{h}^{-1}\right).

(ii)

TreatmentNet satisfies

\|\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{(\varphi)}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}=O_{p}\!\left(\rho_{\varphi,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}+\mathcal{E}_{r}+\rho_{\varphi,3}\right).

(iii)

DRPCLNET-V1 satisfies

\|\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}=O_{p}\!\left(t_{h}^{-1}+\rho_{\mathrm{DR},1}+\min\left\{\rho_{h,\nu_{h}},\rho_{\varphi,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}+\mathcal{E}_{r}\right\}\right).

Theorem
5.1
gives double robustness in weak-norm form. The DRPCLNET-V1 estimator is consistent when the empirical outcome-bridge averaging in Eq.
4
and the DR stage-3 residual regression in Eq.
5
are consistent, and either the outcome-side weak-norm error
\mathcal{R}_{h}^{\mathrm{weak}}
vanishes or the oracle treatment-side weak-norm error
\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-r_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,W})}^{2}
vanishes. Equivalently, on the treatment side it suffices that
\mathcal{R}_{\varphi}^{\mathrm{weak},\hat{r}}+\mathcal{E}_{r}\to 0
, since
\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-r_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,W})}^{2}\leq 2\mathcal{R}_{\varphi}^{\mathrm{weak},\hat{r}}+2\mathcal{E}_{r}
. Importantly, our result does not require controlling strong
L^{2}
-norm convergence of either
\widehat{h}
to
h_{0}
or
\widehat{\varphi}
to
\varphi_{0}
. Since the bridge functions
h_{0}
and
\varphi_{0}
are solutions to ill-posed inverse problems, controlling
L^{2}
-norms would require restrictive assumptions on measure of ill-posedness
[
6
,
9
]
. Our result builds on weak norm rates given in Theorem
H.2
, Theorem
H.11
, and Theorem
H.27
. We achieve this by building on the doubly robust identification formula in
8
and
16
.

The corresponding consistency guarantees for heterogeneous and conditional dose-response estimation follow by the same weak-norm arguments and are given in Appendix
H.6
and
H.7
, respectively.

6
Numerical experiments

We evaluate the proposed neural doubly robust proxy estimators against OutcomeNet, TreatmentNet, and applicable proxy baselines, including DRKPV
[
8
]
, PKDR
[
51
]
, and KPV
[
33
,
45
]
. Performance is measured by causal MSE between estimated and ground-truth response curves over the evaluation grid. Baselines are included only when compatible with the benchmark: DRKPV is omitted in the high-dimensional synthetic setting with observed
X
, PKDR is omitted for image-valued dSprites treatments, and CATE comparisons use KPV since doubly robust proxy baselines are not available for that target. Implementation details, architectures, hyperparameters, and density-ratio estimation procedures are given in Appendix
I
.

Low-dimensional continuous dose-response.

We first consider the low-dimensional continuous-treatment benchmark of
51
. This setting contains nonlinear latent confounding and noisy proxy measurements. The latent variables are
U_{1}\sim\mathcal{U}[-1,2]
and
U_{2}\sim\mathcal{U}[0,1]-\mathbf{1}\{0\leq U_{1}\leq 1\}
. The outcome and proxies are generated as
W=(U_{2}+\mathcal{U}[-1,1],\,U_{1}+\mathcal{N}(0,1))^{\top}
,
Z=(U_{2}+\mathcal{N}(0,1),\,U_{1}+\mathcal{U}[-1,1])^{\top}
,
A=U_{1}+\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, and
Y=3\cos\{2(0.3U_{2}+0.3U_{1}+0.2)+1.5A\}+\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

High-dimensional synthetic dose-response.

We next evaluate performance in the high-dimensional synthetic benchmark adapted from
45
. In this setting, the observed covariates
X
, treatment proxy
Z
, and outcome proxy
W
are high-dimensional vectors, with unobserved confounding induced by latent noise components shared across the proxy, treatment, and outcome mechanisms. The continuous treatment assignment depends nonlinearly on summaries of
X
and
Z
, together with a latent confounding term. The outcome depends on the structural dose-response component
f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)=a^{2}+1.2a
, summaries of
X
and
W
, a treatment-covariate interaction, and latent noise. Appendix
I.2
gives the full data-generating process.

Image-valued dSprites dose-response.

We also consider a structured high-dimensional treatment benchmark based on dSprites
[
34
,
55
]
. Here, each treatment
A\in\mathbb{R}^{4096}
is a flattened
64\times 64
image, and the target response is determined by the image functional
f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(A)=\{(\mathrm{vec}(B)^{\top}A)^{2}-3000\}/500
, where
B_{ij}=|32-j|/32
. The outcome is confounded by the latent vertical position
\mathrm{posY}
, with
Y=12(\mathrm{posY}-0.5)^{2}f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(A)+\mathcal{N}(0,0.5^{2})
. The treatment proxy
Z
consists of the latent factors
(\mathrm{scale},\mathrm{rotation},\mathrm{posX})
, while the outcome proxy
W
is a separate image sharing the same
\mathrm{posY}
.

Synthetic heterogeneous dose-response.

To evaluate CATE estimation, we adapt the binary-treatment benchmark of
1
to the proxy causal learning setting. We use
V=\epsilon_{0}
as the covariate indexing heterogeneity, and define the latent confounders as
U_{1}=1+2V+\epsilon_{1}
,
U_{2}=1+2V+\epsilon_{2}
, and
U_{3}=(V-1)^{2}+\epsilon_{3}
, where
\epsilon_{i}\sim\mathcal{U}[-0.5,0.5]
. The observed outcome is
Y=VU_{1}U_{2}U_{3}+\nu
when
A=1
and
Y=0
when
A=0
, with
\nu\sim\mathcal{N}(0,0.25^{2})
. The ground-truth heterogeneous response at
A=1
is
f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(1,v)=v(1+2v)^{2}(v-1)^{2}
. Proxies are noisy measurements of the latent confounders:
W_{1}=U_{1}+\mathcal{U}[-b,b]
,
W_{2}=U_{2}+\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
,
W_{3}=U_{3}+\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
,
Z_{1}=U_{1}+\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
,
Z_{2}=U_{2}+\mathcal{U}[-b,b]
, and
Z_{3}=U_{3}+\mathcal{U}[-b,b]
, with
b=\sigma=0.1
.

(a)

Low-dimensional dose-response

(b)

High-dimensional dose-response

(c)

Image-valued dSprites dose-response

(d)

Synthetic heterogeneous response

Figure 2
:
Estimator comparison across the main benchmark settings. Each panel reports causal MSE on a logarithmic scale as the sample size increases. DRKPV (Nystrom) denotes the low-rank approximation used for dSprites experiment as exact DRKPV exceeded memory limits for large sample sizes.

Across the four benchmarks, DRPCLNET-V1 and DRPCLNET-V2 are consistently among the strongest performers, often improving over single-bridge neural estimators and kernel baselines as sample size increases. These results suggest combining learned outcome and treatment bridges stabilizes causal function estimation across low-dimensional and structured/high-dimensional settings. Additional ATT experiments, ablations, and proxy-misspecification analyses appear in Appendix
I.4
.

7
Conclusion

Summary.

We introduced a neural doubly robust framework for proxy causal learning with continuous and structured treatments. The method combines a neural outcome bridge with a new neural mean-embedding treatment bridge, and uses final regression stages to estimate population, heterogeneous, and conditional response functions. We also established weak-norm error bounds showing that the doubly robust error is controlled by the final averaging/regression errors and the smaller of the outcome- and treatment-side bridge errors. Empirically, the proposed estimators perform favorably against single-bridge neural estimators and existing proxy baselines across synthetic and image-valued benchmarks. As a broader impact, the method may support better causal analysis from imperfect observational data, but incorrect conclusions may affect downstream decisions if the proxy variables do not satisfy the assumptions needed for bridge identification.

Limitations and future work.

The neural estimators require more tuning than kernel or semiparametric baselines, and TreatmentNet depends on a precomputed density-ratio estimate whose error enters our bounds. Reducing hyperparameter sensitivity and analyzing the nonconvex stochastic optimization remain important future directions.

Appendix Contents

Appendix A
Treatment bridge identification of the heterogeneous dose-response

In this appendix, we prove the treatment-bridge identification result for the heterogeneous dose-response
f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v)
stated in Theorem
3.2
. The corresponding ATE and ATT statements follow from
7
. To match the notation of the main text, we write
X=(S,V)
and use
p
to denote densities or probability mass functions whenever they exist.

Proof of the CATE statement in Theorem
3.2
.

Fix
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
and
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
, and write
\varphi_{0}=\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{CATE}}
for brevity. By assumption,
\varphi_{0}
satisfies

\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,v,S,Z)\mid A=a,S,V=v,W]=\frac{p_{A\mid V}(a\mid v)}{p_{A\mid S,V,W}(a\mid S,v,W)}\qquad{\mathbb{P}}_{W\mid A=a,S,V=v}\text{-a.e.}

(6)

Define

r_{a}(v,S,U)\coloneqq\frac{p_{A\mid V}(a\mid v)}{p_{A\mid S,V,U}(a\mid S,v,U)}.

Using
W\perp A\mid U,X
from Assumption
2.2
, with
X=(S,V)
, Bayes’ rule gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[r_{a}(v,S,U)\mid A=a,S,V=v,W]

\displaystyle=\int\frac{p_{A\mid V}(a\mid v)}{p_{A\mid S,V,U}(a\mid S,v,u)}\,p_{U\mid A,S,V,W}(u\mid a,S,v,W)\,du

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{A\mid V}(a\mid v)}{p_{A\mid S,V,W}(a\mid S,v,W)}

{\mathbb{P}}_{W\mid A=a,S,V=v}
-almost everywhere. Comparing this identity with Equation
6
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,v,S,Z)\mid A=a,S,V=v,U]-r_{a}(v,S,U)\;\middle|\;A=a,S,V=v,W\right]=0.

By Assumption
2.3
, applied conditionally on
A=a
and
X=(S,V)
, it follows that

\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,v,S,Z)\mid A=a,S,V=v,U]=\frac{p_{A\mid V}(a\mid v)}{p_{A\mid S,V,U}(a\mid S,v,U)}\qquad{\mathbb{P}}_{U\mid A=a,S,V=v}\text{-a.e.}

(7)

Now define

m_{a}(S,U,V)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[Y\mid A=a,S,V,U].

Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi_{0}(a,v,S,Z)\mid A=a,V=v]

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi_{0}(a,v,S,Z)\mid A=a,S,V=v,U]\;\middle|\;A=a,V=v\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathbb{E}[Y\mid A=a,S,V=v,U]\,\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,v,S,Z)\mid A=a,S,V=v,U]\;\middle|\;A=a,V=v\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}\!\left[m_{a}(S,U,v)\,\frac{p_{A\mid V}(a\mid v)}{p_{A\mid S,V,U}(a\mid S,v,U)}\;\middle|\;A=a,V=v\right],

where the second equality uses
Y\perp Z\mid U,A,X
from Assumption
2.2
, with
X=(S,V)
, and the third equality uses Equation
7
.

Expanding the last conditional expectation gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[m_{a}(S,U,v)\,\frac{p_{A\mid V}(a\mid v)}{p_{A\mid S,V,U}(a\mid S,v,U)}\;\middle|\;A=a,V=v\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\int m_{a}(s,u,v)\,\frac{p_{A\mid V}(a\mid v)}{p_{A\mid S,V,U}(a\mid s,v,u)}\,p_{S,U\mid A,V}(s,u\mid a,v)\,ds\,du

\displaystyle\quad=\int m_{a}(s,u,v)\,p_{S,U\mid V}(s,u\mid v)\,ds\,du

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}[m_{a}(S,U,v)\mid V=v].

Finally, by consistency and latent exchangeability conditional on
(X,U)=(S,V,U)
,

\mathbb{E}[m_{a}(S,U,v)\mid V=v]=\mathbb{E}[Y^{(a)}\mid V=v]=f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v).

Therefore,

f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v)=\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v,S,Z)\mid A=a,V=v].

This proves the claim.
∎

Appendix B
Doubly robust identification of causal functions: dose, heterogeneous, and conditional response curves

In this section, we prove Theorem
3.3
. The key point is that each doubly robust formula remains valid if either the outcome bridge or the corresponding treatment bridge is correctly specified.

Proof.

We prove the result separately for the ATE, CATE, and ATT. Throughout, we use the notation
X=(S,V)
, and we write
h_{0}(a,X,W)
for the outcome bridge.

The ATE formula is the covariate adjusted version of
8
[Theorem 2.7]. So, below, we prove CATE and ATT extensions.

B.0.0.0.1
CATE.

Consider

f_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR)}}(a,v;h,\varphi)=\mathbb{E}[\varphi(a,v,S,Z)\{Y-h(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a,V=v]+\mathbb{E}[h(a,X,W)\mid V=v].

If
h=h_{0}
, then

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR)}}(a,v;h_{0},\varphi)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\varphi(a,v,S,Z)\,\mathbb{E}[Y-h_{0}(a,X,W)\mid A=a,S,V=v,Z]\middle|A=a,V=v\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)\mid V=v]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)\mid V=v]=f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v),

where the second equality again follows from Equation
1
, since
X=(S,V)
, and the last equality follows from Theorem
3.1
.

If
\varphi=\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{CATE}}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v,S,Z)h(a,X,W)\mid A=a,V=v]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v,S,Z)\mid A=a,S,V=v,W]\,h(a,X,W)\middle|A=a,V=v\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\frac{p_{A\mid V}(a\mid v)}{p_{A\mid S,V,W}(a\mid S,v,W)}\,h(a,X,W)\middle|A=a,V=v\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\int h(a,s,v,w)\,\frac{p_{A\mid V}(a\mid v)}{p_{A\mid S,V,W}(a\mid s,v,w)}\,p_{S,W\mid A,V}(s,w\mid a,v)\,ds\,dw

\displaystyle\qquad=\int h(a,s,v,w)\,p_{S,W\mid V}(s,w\mid v)\,ds\,dw=\mathbb{E}[h(a,X,W)\mid V=v].

Hence,

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR)}}(a,v;h,\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{CATE}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v,S,Z)\mid A=a,V=v]

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v,S,Z)h(a,X,W)\mid A=a,V=v]+\mathbb{E}[h(a,X,W)\mid V=v]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v,S,Z)\mid A=a,V=v]=f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v),

where the last equality follows from Theorem
3.2
.

B.0.0.0.2
ATT.

Consider

f_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{\mathrm{(DR)}}(a,a^{\prime};h,\varphi)=\mathbb{E}[\varphi(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)\{Y-h(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a]+\mathbb{E}[h(a,X,W)\mid A=a^{\prime}].

If
h=h_{0}
, then

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{\mathrm{(DR)}}(a,a^{\prime};h_{0},\varphi)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\varphi(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)\,\mathbb{E}[Y-h_{0}(a,X,W)\mid A=a,X,Z]\middle|A=a\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)\mid A=a^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)\mid A=a^{\prime}]=f_{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime}),

by Equation
1
and Theorem
3.1
.

If
\varphi=\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{ATT}}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)h(a,X,W)\mid A=a]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)\mid A=a,X,W]\,h(a,X,W)\middle|A=a\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\frac{p_{X,W\mid A}(X,W\mid a^{\prime})}{p_{X,W\mid A}(X,W\mid a)}\,h(a,X,W)\middle|A=a\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\int h(a,x,w)\,p_{X,W\mid A}(x,w\mid a^{\prime})\,dx\,dw=\mathbb{E}[h(a,X,W)\mid A=a^{\prime}].

Therefore,

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{\mathrm{(DR)}}(a,a^{\prime};h,\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{ATT}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)\mid A=a]

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)h(a,X,W)\mid A=a]+\mathbb{E}[h(a,X,W)\mid A=a^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi_{0}^{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)\mid A=a]=f_{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime}),

where the last equality follows from Theorem
3.2
.

This proves the double robustness of all three identification formulas.
∎

Appendix C
Semiparametric efficiency theory and influence functions in the discrete setting

In this section, we derive and formally establish the Efficient Influence Functions (EIFs) for the bridge-based target causal functions. We focus specifically on the discrete treatment setting, where
A
takes values in a finite set
\mathcal{A}
. The restriction to discrete
A

is important: pointwise dose-response functionals are pathwise differentiable in
the usual semiparametric sense for atoms
A=a
, whereas continuous-treatment
pointwise effects require a different local or smoothed efficiency theory
[
26
,
14
,
51
,
57
]
. For
the CATE result below we also take
V
to be discrete, so that

\mathbb{P}(V=v)>0
.

Throughout this section,
O=(Y,A,W,Z,X)
, and for CATE we write

X=(S,V)
and use
h_{0}(a,S,V,W)
as shorthand for
h_{0}(a,X,W)
. Define

\pi_{a}:=\mathbb{P}(A=a),\qquad\pi_{v}:=\mathbb{P}(V=v),\qquad\pi_{a\mid v}:=\mathbb{P}(A=a\mid V=v).

All expectations are taken under the true observed law unless otherwise stated.

C.0.0.0.1
Semiparametric regularity.

In addition to the bridge existence, uniqueness, square-integrability, and
positivity conditions stated below, we use the same tangent-space regularity
condition as in
[
15
]
and

[
8
]
. Namely, the relevant
conditional expectation operators are assumed to have dense range; the stronger
surjectivity condition on the operators and their adjoints used by

[
15
]
is sufficient. For the ATE and ATT formulas,
one may take

Tg:=\mathbb{E}[g(W,A,X)\mid Z,A,X],\qquad T^{\ast}r:=\mathbb{E}[r(Z,A,X)\mid W,A,X],

with
T:L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{W,A,X})\to L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Z,A,X})
. For the CATE formula, with

X=(S,V)
, take

T_{\mathrm{C}}g:=\mathbb{E}[g(W,A,S,V)\mid Z,A,S,V],\qquad T_{\mathrm{C}}^{\ast}r:=\mathbb{E}[r(Z,A,S,V)\mid W,A,S,V].

This condition is used only to conclude that the influence functions derived
below are canonical gradients, hence efficient.

Theorem C.1

(Efficient influence functions for discrete targets)
.

Let Assumptions
2.2

and
2.3
hold. Suppose that there exists a unique outcome
bridge
h_{0}
satisfying Eq.
1
, and unique treatment
bridges
\varphi^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0},\varphi^{\mathrm{CATE}}_{0},\varphi^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}
satisfying the treatment bridge equations

2
. Assume the
corresponding positivity and support-overlap conditions hold. In particular,

\pi_{a}>0
,
\pi_{a^{\prime}}>0
,
\pi_{v}>0
, and
\pi_{a\mid v}>0
whenever the
corresponding target is considered. Assume also the semiparametric regularity
condition above.

Then
f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)
,
f_{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime})
, and, for discrete

V
,
f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v)
are pathwise differentiable. Their EIFs are as
follows.

(i) ATE.
For any
a\in\mathcal{A}
,

\displaystyle\psi^{\mathrm{EIF}}_{\mathrm{ATE}}(O;a)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathds{1}\{A=a\}}{\pi_{a}}\,\varphi^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)\bigl\{Y-h_{0}(A,X,W)\bigr\}+h_{0}(a,X,W)-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a).

(8)

(ii) ATT / conditional dose-response.
For any

a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
,

\displaystyle\psi^{\mathrm{EIF}}_{\mathrm{ATT}}(O;a,a^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathds{1}\{A=a\}}{\pi_{a}}\,\varphi^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)\bigl\{Y-h_{0}(A,X,W)\bigr\}

(9)

\displaystyle+\frac{\mathds{1}\{A=a^{\prime}\}}{\pi_{a^{\prime}}}\bigl\{h_{0}(a,X,W)-f_{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime})\bigr\}.

(10)

(iii) CATE, with discrete
V
.
For any
a\in\mathcal{A}

and any
v
with
\pi_{v}>0
,

\displaystyle\psi^{\mathrm{EIF}}_{\mathrm{CATE}}(O;a,v)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathds{1}\{V=v\}}{\pi_{v}}\Bigg[\frac{\mathds{1}\{A=a\}}{\pi_{a\mid v}}\,\varphi^{\mathrm{CATE}}_{0}(a,v,S,Z)\bigl\{Y-h_{0}(A,S,V,W)\bigr\}

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt+h_{0}(a,S,v,W)-f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v)\Bigg].

(11)

Proof.

The ATE formula is the covariate-adjusted version of

8
. It is also
obtained from the EIF of
15

after replacing the classical treatment bridge
q_{0}
, which satisfies

\mathbb{E}[q_{0}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a,X,W]=1/p(a\mid X,W)
, by the normalized bridge

\varphi^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)=\pi_{a}q_{0}(a,X,Z)
. We give the derivations
for ATT and CATE because the conditioning events change the derivative and the
normalization.

Let
\{P_{t}:t\in(-\delta,\delta)\}
be a regular parametric submodel through
the true law
P_{0}=P
, and let

\dot{\ell}(O):=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log p_{t}(O)\right|_{t=0}

be its score. Let
h_{t}
denote a differentiable path of outcome bridges along
the submodel, and write

\dot{h}:=\left.\partial h_{t}/\partial t\right|_{t=0}
.

C.0.0.0.2
ATT.

Fix
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
and write

\psi:=f_{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)\mid A=a^{\prime}].

Define

N_{t}:=\mathbb{E}_{t}[\mathds{1}\{A=a^{\prime}\}h_{t}(a,X,W)],\qquad D_{t}:=P_{t}(A=a^{\prime}),

so that
\psi_{t}=N_{t}/D_{t}
. Differentiating the ratio at
t=0
gives

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial\psi_{t}}{\partial t}\right|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi_{a^{\prime}}}\left[\left.\frac{\partial N_{t}}{\partial t}\right|_{t=0}-\psi\left.\frac{\partial D_{t}}{\partial t}\right|_{t=0}\right].

(12)

The denominator derivative is

\left.\frac{\partial D_{t}}{\partial t}\right|_{t=0}=\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}\{A=a^{\prime}\}\dot{\ell}(O)].

For the numerator,

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial N_{t}}{\partial t}\right|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}\{A=a^{\prime}\}h_{0}(a,X,W)\dot{\ell}(O)]+\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}\{A=a^{\prime}\}\dot{h}(a,X,W)].

(13)

It remains to express the second term in Eq.
13
as an
inner product with the score. The bridge equation along the submodel is

\mathbb{E}_{t}[Y-h_{t}(A,X,W)\mid A,X,Z]=0.

Differentiating this equation and using the conditional mean-zero bridge
residual gives, on the event
\{A=a\}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\dot{h}(a,X,W)\mid A=a,X,Z]=\mathbb{E}[\{Y-h_{0}(a,X,W)\}\dot{\ell}(O)\mid A=a,X,Z].

(14)

The ATT treatment bridge satisfies

\mathbb{E}[\varphi^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)\mid A=a,X,W]=\frac{p(X,W\mid A=a^{\prime})}{p(X,W\mid A=a)}.

Therefore, for any square-integrable
g(X,W)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}\{A=a\}\varphi^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)g(X,W)]

\displaystyle=\pi_{a}\mathbb{E}[\varphi^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)g(X,W)\mid A=a]

\displaystyle=\pi_{a}\int g(x,w)\frac{p(x,w\mid A=a^{\prime})}{p(x,w\mid A=a)}p(x,w\mid A=a)\,dx\,dw

\displaystyle=\pi_{a}\mathbb{E}[g(X,W)\mid A=a^{\prime}].

(15)

Taking
g=\dot{h}(a,\cdot,\cdot)
in Eq.
15
yields

\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}\{A=a^{\prime}\}\dot{h}(a,X,W)]=\frac{\pi_{a^{\prime}}}{\pi_{a}}\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}\{A=a\}\varphi^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)\dot{h}(a,X,W)].

Combining this identity with Eq.
14
gives

\frac{1}{\pi_{a^{\prime}}}\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}\{A=a^{\prime}\}\dot{h}(a,X,W)]=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\frac{\mathds{1}\{A=a\}}{\pi_{a}}\varphi^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)\{Y-h_{0}(a,X,W)\}\dot{\ell}(O)\right].

Substituting this display and Eq.
13
into
Eq.
12
gives

\left.\frac{\partial\psi_{t}}{\partial t}\right|_{t=0}=\mathbb{E}[\psi^{\mathrm{EIF}}_{\mathrm{ATT}}(O;a,a^{\prime})\dot{\ell}(O)].

The candidate in Eq.
10
is mean zero because the first term has
conditional mean zero given
(A=a,X,Z)
, and the second term is centered
conditional on
A=a^{\prime}
.

It remains to check efficiency. Let
\mathcal{F}=(Z,A,X)
and

\varepsilon:=Y-h_{0}(A,X,W)
. Under the bridge-restricted semiparametric model,
the tangent space can be written as
\Lambda=\Lambda_{1}+\Lambda_{2}
, where

\Lambda_{1}=\{s_{1}(\mathcal{F})\in L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}):\mathbb{E}[s_{1}]=0\},

and

\Lambda_{2}=\left\{s_{2}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}):\mathbb{E}[s_{2}\mid\mathcal{F}]=0,\ \mathbb{E}[\varepsilon s_{2}\mid\mathcal{F}]\in\overline{\mathcal{R}(T)}\right\}.

Set
H_{\mathrm{ATT}}(X,W):=h_{0}(a,X,W)-\psi
, and decompose

G_{\mathrm{ATT}}:=\psi^{\mathrm{EIF}}_{\mathrm{ATT}}
as

G_{\mathrm{ATT},1}:=\mathbb{E}[G_{\mathrm{ATT}}\mid\mathcal{F}]=\frac{\mathds{1}\{A=a^{\prime}\}}{\pi_{a^{\prime}}}\mathbb{E}[H_{\mathrm{ATT}}(X,W)\mid\mathcal{F}],\qquad G_{\mathrm{ATT},2}:=G_{\mathrm{ATT}}-G_{\mathrm{ATT},1}.

Then
G_{\mathrm{ATT},1}\in\Lambda_{1}
. Also

\mathbb{E}[G_{\mathrm{ATT},2}\mid\mathcal{F}]=0
. Moreover

\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon G_{\mathrm{ATT},2}\mid\mathcal{F}]
is a square-integrable

\mathcal{F}
-measurable function; by the dense-range condition for
T
, it
belongs to
\overline{\mathcal{R}(T)}
. Hence

G_{\mathrm{ATT},2}\in\Lambda_{2}
, so

G_{\mathrm{ATT}}\in\Lambda
. Since it represents the pathwise derivative and
belongs to the tangent space, it is the canonical gradient.

C.0.0.0.3
CATE.

Fix
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
v
with
\pi_{v}>0
. Write

\psi:=f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v)=\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,S,v,W)\mid V=v].

Define

N_{t}:=\mathbb{E}_{t}[\mathds{1}\{V=v\}h_{t}(a,S,v,W)],\qquad D_{t}:=P_{t}(V=v),

so that
\psi_{t}=N_{t}/D_{t}
. The quotient rule gives

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial\psi_{t}}{\partial t}\right|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi_{v}}\left[\left.\frac{\partial N_{t}}{\partial t}\right|_{t=0}-\psi\left.\frac{\partial D_{t}}{\partial t}\right|_{t=0}\right],

(16)

with

\left.\frac{\partial D_{t}}{\partial t}\right|_{t=0}=\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}\{V=v\}\dot{\ell}(O)]

and

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial N_{t}}{\partial t}\right|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}\{V=v\}h_{0}(a,S,v,W)\dot{\ell}(O)]+\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}\{V=v\}\dot{h}(a,S,v,W)].

(17)

The bridge equation along the submodel is

\mathbb{E}_{t}[Y-h_{t}(A,S,V,W)\mid A,S,V,Z]=0.

Differentiating at
t=0
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\dot{h}(A,S,V,W)\mid A,S,V,Z]=\mathbb{E}[\{Y-h_{0}(A,S,V,W)\}\dot{\ell}(O)\mid A,S,V,Z].

(18)

The CATE treatment bridge satisfies

\mathbb{E}[\varphi^{\mathrm{CATE}}_{0}(a,v,S,Z)\mid A=a,S,V=v,W]=\frac{\pi_{a\mid v}}{p(A=a\mid S,V=v,W)}.

Consequently, for any square-integrable
g(S,W)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}\{V=v\}\mathds{1}\{A=a\}\varphi^{\mathrm{CATE}}_{0}(a,v,S,Z)g(S,W)]

\displaystyle\qquad=\pi_{v}\pi_{a\mid v}\int g(s,w)\frac{\pi_{a\mid v}}{p(A=a\mid s,v,w)}p(s,w\mid A=a,V=v)\,ds\,dw

\displaystyle\qquad=\pi_{v}\pi_{a\mid v}\int g(s,w)p(s,w\mid V=v)\,ds\,dw

\displaystyle\qquad=\pi_{a\mid v}\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}\{V=v\}g(S,W)].

(19)

Taking
g=\dot{h}(a,\cdot,v,\cdot)
in Eq.
19

gives

\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}\{V=v\}\dot{h}(a,S,v,W)]=\frac{1}{\pi_{a\mid v}}\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}\{V=v\}\mathds{1}\{A=a\}\varphi^{\mathrm{CATE}}_{0}(a,v,S,Z)\dot{h}(a,S,v,W)].

Combining this display with Eq.
18
yields

\frac{1}{\pi_{v}}\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}\{V=v\}\dot{h}(a,S,v,W)]=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\frac{\mathds{1}\{V=v\}}{\pi_{v}}\frac{\mathds{1}\{A=a\}}{\pi_{a\mid v}}\varphi^{\mathrm{CATE}}_{0}(a,v,S,Z)\{Y-h_{0}(A,S,V,W)\}\dot{\ell}(O)\right].

Substituting this identity and Eq.
17
into
Eq.
16
gives

\left.\frac{\partial\psi_{t}}{\partial t}\right|_{t=0}=\mathbb{E}[\psi^{\mathrm{EIF}}_{\mathrm{CATE}}(O;a,v)\dot{\ell}(O)].

Thus Eq.
11
is an influence function. It is mean zero by the
outcome bridge equation and by the centering of

h_{0}(a,S,v,W)-f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v)
conditional on
V=v
.

For efficiency, let
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{C}}:=(Z,A,S,V)
and

\varepsilon:=Y-h_{0}(A,S,V,W)
. The tangent space is

\Lambda_{\mathrm{C}}=\Lambda_{\mathrm{C},1}+\Lambda_{\mathrm{C},2}
, where

\Lambda_{\mathrm{C},1}=\{s_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{C}})\in L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}):\mathbb{E}[s_{1}]=0\},

and

\Lambda_{\mathrm{C},2}=\left\{s_{2}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}):\mathbb{E}[s_{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{C}}]=0,\ \mathbb{E}[\varepsilon s_{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{C}}]\in\overline{\mathcal{R}(T_{\mathrm{C}})}\right\}.

Set
H_{\mathrm{C}}(S,W):=h_{0}(a,S,v,W)-\psi
, and write

G_{\mathrm{C}}:=\psi^{\mathrm{EIF}}_{\mathrm{CATE}}(O;a,v)
. Then

G_{\mathrm{C},1}:=\mathbb{E}[G_{\mathrm{C}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{C}}]=\frac{\mathds{1}\{V=v\}}{\pi_{v}}\mathbb{E}[H_{\mathrm{C}}(S,W)\mid\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{C}}],\qquad G_{\mathrm{C},2}:=G_{\mathrm{C}}-G_{\mathrm{C},1}.

The first component belongs to
\Lambda_{\mathrm{C},1}
. The second satisfies

\mathbb{E}[G_{\mathrm{C},2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{C}}]=0
, and

\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon G_{\mathrm{C},2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{C}}]
is a
square-integrable
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{C}}
-measurable function. By the
dense-range condition for
T_{\mathrm{C}}
, this conditional moment belongs to

\overline{\mathcal{R}(T_{\mathrm{C}})}
. Hence

G_{\mathrm{C},2}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{C},2}
, so

G_{\mathrm{C}}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{C}}
. Since it represents the pathwise
derivative and lies in the tangent space, it is the canonical gradient. This
proves Eq.
11
and completes the proof.
∎

Remark C.2

(Relation to classical proximal ATE identification)
.

In the discrete-treatment case, our ATE representation is a normalized version of

15
. For a fixed treatment level

a
, their treatment bridge may be written as

\mathbb{E}[q^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a,X,W]=\frac{1}{p(A=a\mid X,W)}.

Their corresponding doubly robust representation of the counterfactual mean is

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR)}}(a;h_{0},q^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\big[\mathds{1}\{A=a\}q^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)\{Y-h_{0}(A,X,W)\}\big]+\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)].

(20)

Equivalently, its influence-function moment is

\psi^{\mathrm{EIF}}_{q^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}}(O;a)=\mathds{1}\{A=a\}q^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)\{Y-h_{0}(A,X,W)\}+h_{0}(a,X,W)-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a).

Our bridge in Eq.
2
satisfies

\mathbb{E}[\varphi^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a,X,W]=\frac{p(a)}{p(A=a\mid X,W)}.

Thus, when the bridge solution is unique,

\varphi^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)=p(a)q^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)
. Substituting this into
Eq.
20
gives

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\varphi^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)\{Y-h_{0}(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a\right]+\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)],

which is exactly Eq.
3
. Therefore the
normalized bridge does not change the discrete EIF; it rewrites the same moment
as a conditional expectation given
A=a
. This form is the one used by our
neural mean-embedding estimator and avoids an explicit sparse indicator moment
when the target is indexed by a continuous or structured treatment value.

Remark C.3

(Relation to the classical proximal ATT EIF)
.

The ATT formula above is not obtained by multiplying the whole ATE EIF by

\mathds{1}\{A=a^{\prime}\}/\pi_{a^{\prime}}
. The bridge-residual term remains on the event

\{A=a\}
, while the centering term is evaluated on the event
\{A=a^{\prime}\}
.

Consider the binary case
a=0
,
a^{\prime}=1
, and define

\mu_{0}:=f_{\mathrm{ATT}}(0,1)=\mathbb{E}[Y^{(0)}\mid A=1]
. Cui et al.’s ATT bridge
is restricted to the control arm and satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[h^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(X,W)\mid A=0,X,Z]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid A=0,X,Z],

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[q^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(X,Z)\mid A=0,X,W]

\displaystyle=\frac{p(A=1\mid X,W)}{p(A=0\mid X,W)}.

Their proof of the ATT EIF first derives the influence function for

\mu_{0}
, namely

\displaystyle\psi^{\mathrm{EIF}}_{\mu_{0}}(O)=\frac{\mathds{1}\{A=0\}}{\pi_{1}}q^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(X,Z)\{Y-h^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(X,W)\}+\frac{\mathds{1}\{A=1\}}{\pi_{1}}\{h^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(X,W)-\mu_{0}\}.

(21)

Equivalently,

\displaystyle\mu_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\frac{\mathds{1}\{A=0\}}{\pi_{1}}q^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(X,Z)\{Y-h^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(X,W)\}+\frac{\mathds{1}\{A=1\}}{\pi_{1}}h^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(X,W)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\frac{\mathds{1}\{A=0\}}{\pi_{1}}q^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(X,Z)\{Y-h^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(X,W)\}\right]+\mathbb{E}[h^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(X,W)\mid A=1].

(22)

Our normalized ATT bridge satisfies

\mathbb{E}[\varphi^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(0,1,X,Z)\mid A=0,X,W]=\frac{p(X,W\mid A=1)}{p(X,W\mid A=0)}=\frac{\pi_{0}}{\pi_{1}}\frac{p(A=1\mid X,W)}{p(A=0\mid X,W)}.

Thus, under uniqueness,

\varphi^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(0,1,X,Z)=(\pi_{0}/\pi_{1})q^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(X,Z)
, and
Eq.
10
reduces to Eq.
21
with

h^{\mathrm{ATT}}_{0}(X,W)=h_{0}(0,X,W)
.

The usual binary ATT contrast is

\tau_{\mathrm{ATT}}:=\mathbb{E}[Y^{(1)}-Y^{(0)}\mid A=1]=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid A=1]-f_{\mathrm{ATT}}(0,1).

Its EIF is therefore

\frac{\mathds{1}\{A=1\}}{\pi_{1}}\{Y-\mathbb{E}[Y\mid A=1]\}-\psi^{\mathrm{EIF}}_{\mathrm{ATT}}(O;0,1).

Hence
\psi^{\mathrm{EIF}}_{\mathrm{ATT}}(O;a,a^{\prime})
in
Theorem
C.1
is the EIF for the conditional
counterfactual mean
f_{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime})
; the EIF for an ATT contrast is
obtained by adding the observed-arm component and taking the difference.

Remark C.4

(Continuous and structured treatments)
.

The EIFs in Theorem
C.1
are discrete-treatment EIFs.
They justify the corresponding discrete doubly robust moment equations. The
identification formulas in Theorem
3.3

are still stated for continuous or structured
A
, provided the bridge
equations are interpreted through regular conditional laws and the required
positivity conditions hold. However, the pointwise semiparametric efficiency
theory for continuous treatments is not obtained by replacing

\mathds{1}\{A=a\}/p(a)
with a density ratio. Existing continuous-treatment
DR theory typically works with localized or smoothed target functionals

[
26
,
14
,
51
,
57
]
.
Our contribution in the continuous and high-dimensional setting is therefore an
identification and estimation construction based on normalized treatment
bridges and neural mean embeddings, not a closed-form pointwise continuous-treatment EIF.

Remark C.5

(Heterogeneous dose-response identification)
.

Equation
3
extends the doubly robust
proximal identification formula from population dose-response curves to the
heterogeneous response function

f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v)=\mathbb{E}[Y^{(a)}\mid V=v]
. We are not aware of a previous
proximal doubly robust identification formula for the full curve

(a,v)\mapsto f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v)
. This is distinct from the binary ATT
case: the conditioning event is an observed effect modifier
V=v
, not the
realized treatment arm.

Appendix D
Review of existing multi-stage kernel algorithms with fixed feature maps

We briefly review the kernel-based multi-stage estimators that serve as the main fixed-feature baselines for our method. In particular, the doubly robust kernel estimator DRKPV of
8
combines two components: the outcome-bridge estimator KPV of
33
and the treatment-bridge estimator KAP of
7
. Both methods are built in RKHSs with feature maps fixed by the choice of kernels. This makes them a natural starting point for our neural construction, where these fixed feature maps are replaced by adaptive learned representations.

D.1
Kernel Proxy Variable: outcome-bridge method

The Kernel Proxy Variable (KPV) method of
33
estimates the outcome bridge function
h_{0}:{\mathcal{A}}\times{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{W}}\to\mathbb{R}
by solving Equation
1
. In the RKHS formulation, one assumes that
h_{0}\in{\mathcal{H}}
, where
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{W}}}
. Then the dose-response curve admits the representation

f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)]=\left\langle h_{0},\;\phi_{{\mathcal{A}}}(a)\otimes\mu_{XW}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}},\qquad\mu_{XW}:=\mathbb{E}[\phi_{{\mathcal{X}}}(X)\otimes\phi_{{\mathcal{W}}}(W)].

Replacing
h_{0}
and
\mu_{XW}
by their empirical estimators yields

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)=\left\langle\hat{h},\;\phi_{{\mathcal{A}}}(a)\otimes\hat{\mu}_{XW}\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}},\qquad\hat{\mu}_{XW}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{{\mathcal{X}}}(x_{i})\otimes\phi_{{\mathcal{W}}}(w_{i}).

To estimate
\hat{h}
, KPV uses two stages. The first stage estimates the conditional mean embedding

\mu_{W\mid A,X,Z}(a,x,z):=\mathbb{E}[\phi_{{\mathcal{W}}}(W)\mid A=a,X=x,Z=z].

Under standard CME regularity conditions, there exists an operator
V_{W\mid A,X,Z}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{X}}{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{W}}})
such that
[
46
,
19
,
38
,
33
]

\mu_{W\mid A,X,Z}(a,x,z)=V_{W\mid A,X,Z}\bigl(\phi_{{\mathcal{A}}}(a)\otimes\phi_{{\mathcal{X}}}(x)\otimes\phi_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(z)\bigr)

Given first-stage samples
\{(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{x}_{i},\bar{z}_{i},\bar{w}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{h}}
, KPV estimates this operator by vector-valued ridge regression:

\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{h,1}(V)=\frac{1}{n_{h}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{h}}\left\|\phi_{{\mathcal{W}}}(\bar{w}_{i})-V\!\left(\phi_{{\mathcal{A}}}(\bar{a}_{i})\otimes\phi_{{\mathcal{X}}}(\bar{x}_{i})\otimes\phi_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(\bar{z}_{i})\right)\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{W}}}}^{2}+\lambda_{h,1}\|V\|_{{\mathcal{S}}_{2}}^{2}.

(23)

The corresponding CME estimator has the closed form

\hat{\mu}_{W\mid A,X,Z}(a,x,z)=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{h}}\beta_{i}(a,x,z)\,\phi_{{\mathcal{W}}}(\bar{w}_{i}),

where

\bm{\beta}(a,x,z)=\left({\bm{K}}_{\bar{A}\bar{A}}\odot{\bm{K}}_{\bar{X}\bar{X}}\odot{\bm{K}}_{\bar{Z}\bar{Z}}+n_{h}\lambda_{h,1}{\bm{I}}\right)^{-1}\left({\bm{K}}_{\bar{A}a}\odot{\bm{K}}_{\bar{X}x}\odot{\bm{K}}_{\bar{Z}z}\right).

In the second stage, KPV substitutes
\hat{\mu}_{W\mid A,X,Z}
into the empirical bridge loss. Given second-stage samples
\{(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i},\tilde{y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m_{h}}
, it solves

\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{h,2}(h)=\frac{1}{m_{h}}\sum_{i=1}^{m_{h}}\left(\tilde{y}_{i}-\left\langle h,\,\phi_{{\mathcal{A}}}(\tilde{a}_{i})\otimes\phi_{{\mathcal{X}}}(\tilde{x}_{i})\otimes\hat{\mu}_{W\mid A,X,Z}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i})\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}\right)^{2}+\lambda_{h,2}\|h\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{2}.

(24)

Its minimizer admits the closed form

\hat{h}(a,x,w)=\bm{\alpha}^{\top}\left({\bm{K}}_{\tilde{A}a}\odot{\bm{K}}_{\tilde{X}x}\odot{\bm{B}}^{\top}{\bm{K}}_{\bar{W}w}\right),

where

{\bm{B}}=\left({\bm{K}}_{\bar{A}\bar{A}}\odot{\bm{K}}_{\bar{X}\bar{X}}\odot{\bm{K}}_{\bar{Z}\bar{Z}}+n_{h}\lambda_{h,1}{\bm{I}}\right)^{-1}\left({\bm{K}}_{\bar{A}\tilde{A}}\odot{\bm{K}}_{\bar{X}\tilde{X}}\odot{\bm{K}}_{\bar{Z}\tilde{Z}}\right),

and

{\bm{M}}={\bm{K}}_{\tilde{A}\tilde{A}}\odot{\bm{K}}_{\tilde{X}\tilde{X}}\odot\left({\bm{B}}^{\top}{\bm{K}}_{\bar{W}\bar{W}}{\bm{B}}\right),\qquad\bm{\alpha}=\left({\bm{M}}+m_{h}\lambda_{h,2}{\bm{I}}\right)^{-1}\tilde{{\bm{Y}}}.

The key limitation for our purposes is that the feature maps
\phi_{{\mathcal{A}}},\phi_{{\mathcal{X}}},\phi_{{\mathcal{Z}}},\phi_{{\mathcal{W}}}
are fixed once the kernels are chosen. The neural estimator introduced later keep the same multi-stage structure, but replace these fixed RKHS representations with adaptive feature maps learned from data
[
55
]
. We also note that
45
provides a kernel implementation for heterogeneous and conditional treatment effects; in our experiments, we compare against this baseline using the implementation style of
33
together with the numerically more stable formulation of
54
, which is computationally easier to handle than the original variants.

D.2
Kernel Alternative Proxy: treatment bridge-based method

Complementing the outcome-bridge framework,
7
introduced the Kernel Alternative Proxy (KAP) algorithm for treatment-bridge estimation. Since the original closed-form derivation is developed in the setting without additional observed covariates, we review that version here. Our neural treatment-bridge formulation in Section
E.2
will later extend the same idea to the case with observed covariates
X
.

In the original KAP formulation, the treatment bridge
\varphi_{0}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}
is defined by

\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,Z)\mid A=a,W]=\frac{p_{W}(W)p_{A}(a)}{p_{W,A}(W,a)}.

Given such a bridge, the dose-response is identified by

f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi_{0}(a,Z)\mid A=a],

as stated in Theorem
3.2
in the no-
X
setting.

To estimate
\varphi_{0}
, KAP considers the regularized squared loss

{\mathcal{L}}_{\varphi,2}(\varphi)=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left(r(W,A)-\mathbb{E}[\varphi(A,Z)\mid W,A]\right)^{2}\right]+\lambda_{\varphi,2}\|\varphi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}}^{2},

where
r(W,A):=p_{W}(W)p_{A}(A)/p_{W,A}(W,A)
. A key observation is that the cross-term can be rewritten using the identity
r(w,a)p_{W,A}(w,a)=p_{W}(w)p_{A}(a)
, leading to the equivalent objective

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\varphi,2}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathbb{E}[\varphi(A,Z)\mid W,A]^{2}\right]-2\,\mathbb{E}_{W}\mathbb{E}_{A}\!\left[\mathbb{E}[\varphi(A,Z)\mid W,A]\right]+\lambda_{\varphi,2}\|\varphi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}}^{2}+\mathrm{const.},

(25)

where
\mathbb{E}_{W}\mathbb{E}_{A}[\cdot]
denotes expectation under the product distribution
p_{W}(w)p_{A}(a)
.

As in KPV, the multi-stage structure arises because the conditional expectation is not directly tractable. Using the reproducing property,

\mathbb{E}[\varphi(A,Z)\mid W,A]=\left\langle\varphi,\,\phi_{{\mathcal{A}}}(A)\otimes\mu_{Z\mid A,W}(A,W)\right\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}},

where
\mu_{Z\mid A,W}(a,w):=\mathbb{E}[\phi_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(Z)\mid A=a,W=w]
is the conditional mean embedding of the treatment proxy. Under standard CME regularity conditions, there exists an operator
V_{Z\mid A,W}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{2}({\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{W}}},{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}})
such that
[
46
,
19
,
38
,
7
]

\mu_{Z\mid A,W}(a,w)=V_{Z\mid A,W}\bigl(\phi_{{\mathcal{A}}}(a)\otimes\phi_{{\mathcal{W}}}(w)\bigr).

Given first-stage samples
\{(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{w}_{i},\bar{z}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\varphi}}
, KAP estimates this operator by vector-valued ridge regression:

\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\varphi,1}(V)=\frac{1}{n_{\varphi}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\varphi}}\left\|\phi_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(\bar{z}_{i})-V\bigl(\phi_{{\mathcal{A}}}(\bar{a}_{i})\otimes\phi_{{\mathcal{W}}}(\bar{w}_{i})\bigr)\right\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}}^{2}+\lambda_{\varphi,1}\|V\|_{{\mathcal{S}}_{2}}^{2}.

The corresponding CME estimator has the form

\hat{\mu}_{Z\mid A,W}(a,w)=\hat{V}_{Z\mid A,W}\bigl(\phi_{{\mathcal{A}}}(a)\otimes\phi_{{\mathcal{W}}}(w)\bigr)=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\varphi}}\beta_{i}(a,w)\phi_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(\bar{z}_{i}),

with

\bm{\beta}(a,w)=\left({\bm{K}}_{\bar{A}\bar{A}}\odot{\bm{K}}_{\bar{W}\bar{W}}+n_{\varphi}\lambda_{\varphi,1}{\bm{I}}\right)^{-1}\left({\bm{K}}_{\bar{A}a}\odot{\bm{K}}_{\bar{W}w}\right).

In the second stage, the CME estimate is substituted into Equation
25
. Given second-stage samples
\{(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m_{\varphi}}
, the empirical objective becomes

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\varphi,2}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m_{\varphi}}\sum_{i=1}^{m_{\varphi}}\left\langle\varphi,\,\phi_{{\mathcal{A}}}(\tilde{a}_{i})\otimes\hat{\mu}_{Z\mid A,W}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i})\right\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{2}{m_{\varphi}(m_{\varphi}-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}\left\langle\varphi,\,\phi_{{\mathcal{A}}}(\tilde{a}_{i})\otimes\hat{\mu}_{Z\mid A,W}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{w}_{j})\right\rangle+\lambda_{\varphi,2}\|\varphi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}}^{2}.

(26)

By the generalized representer theorem
[
43
]
, the minimizer
\hat{\varphi}
lies in the span of the corresponding data-dependent features and admits a closed-form solution.

Finally, KAP estimates the dose-response through a third-stage regression. Indeed,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y\hat{\varphi}(a,Z)\mid A=a]

\displaystyle=\left\langle\hat{\varphi},\,\phi_{{\mathcal{A}}}(a)\otimes\mathbb{E}[Y\phi_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(Z)\mid A=a]\right\rangle.

(27)

The term
\mu_{YZ\mid A}(a):=\mathbb{E}[Y\phi_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(Z)\mid A=a]
is estimated by vector-valued ridge regression, leading to a fully closed-form estimator. Using the notation of
7
, the final estimator can be written as
\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)=\bm{\alpha}^{\top}{\bm{E}}(a)
, where
{\bm{E}}(a)
is assembled from the first- and third-stage kernel matrices; the explicit expression is given in Algorithm 4.1 of
7
.

As in KPV, the main limitation for our purposes is that all feature maps are fixed once the kernels are chosen. Our neural treatment-bridge estimator keeps the same multi-stage logic, but replaces these fixed RKHS representations by adaptive learned features.

D.3
Doubly Robust Kernel Proxy Variable

The Doubly Robust Kernel Proxy Variable (DRKPV) estimator of
8
combines the kernel outcome-bridge estimator KPV with the kernel treatment-bridge estimator KAP. In the notation of the previous two subsections, it targets the doubly robust representation

f_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR)}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,Z)\{Y-h_{0}(a,W)\}\mid A=a]+\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,W)].

Thus, the estimator consists of three parts: an outcome-bridge term
\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,W)]
, a treatment-bridge term
\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi_{0}(a,Z)\mid A=a]
, and a correction term
\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,Z)h_{0}(a,W)\mid A=a]
. The first two are obtained from KPV and KAP, respectively.

The only additional ingredient is therefore the cross term
\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,Z)h_{0}(a,W)\mid A=a]
. Following
8
, this quantity is approximated by plugging in the learned bridge functions and writing

\mathbb{E}[\hat{\varphi}(a,Z)\hat{h}(a,W)\mid A=a]=\left\langle\hat{\varphi}\otimes\hat{h},\,\mu_{ZW\mid A}(a)\otimes\phi_{{\mathcal{A}}}(a)\otimes\phi_{{\mathcal{A}}}(a)\right\rangle,

where
\mu_{ZW\mid A}(a):=\mathbb{E}[\phi_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(Z)\otimes\phi_{{\mathcal{W}}}(W)\mid A=a]
. This conditional mean embedding is estimated by a further vector-valued ridge regression on a third split of the data. If
\xi_{i}(a)=\bigl[({\bm{K}}_{AA}+t\lambda_{\mathrm{DR}}{\bm{I}})^{-1}{\bm{K}}_{Aa}\bigr]_{i}
, then the resulting closed-form approximation of the cross term is

{\mathbb{E}[\hat{\varphi}(a,Z)\hat{h}(a,W)\mid A=a]}\approx\sum_{i=1}^{t}\xi_{i}(a)\,\hat{\varphi}(a,z_{i})\hat{h}(a,w_{i}).

Putting the pieces together, DRKPV takes the form

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR)}}(a)=\hat{f}^{(h)}_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)+\hat{f}^{(\varphi)}_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)-\hat{f}^{(\mathrm{cross})}_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a),

where
\hat{f}^{(h)}_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)
is the KPV estimate,
\hat{f}^{(\varphi)}_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)
is the KAP estimate, and
\hat{f}^{(\mathrm{cross})}_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)
is the conditional mean embedding approximation above. By construction, this estimator inherits the doubly robust identification property: it remains valid if either the outcome bridge or the treatment bridge is correctly specified. As with KPV and KAP, however, all feature maps are fixed by the choice of kernels, which is precisely the limitation addressed by our neural extension.

Appendix E
Neural mean embedding-based proxy causal learning for dose-response

In this sextion, we elaborate the algorithmic structure of the Neural Mean Embedding (NME) framework for dose-response estimation. The guiding idea is to preserve the multi-stage bridge-based structure induced by the identification strategy, while replacing fixed RKHS feature maps by trainable neural representations. This allows the representation itself to adapt to the data and makes the resulting estimators suitable for stochastic optimization in high-dimensional and structured settings.

Our construction consists of three components:

1.

Outcome bridge network.
We begin with the Deep Feature Proxy Causal Learning (DFPCL) architecture of
55
, which provides a neural parameterization of the outcome bridge estimator. We refine this component by incorporating proximal closed-form updates for the final linear layer and by introducing a hybrid optimization scheme for the second-stage head. This latter modification allows the use of general robust regression losses, such as the Huber loss, rather than restricting the training objective to mean squared error.

2.

Treatment bridge network.
We next introduce a neural estimator of the treatment bridge function in Section
E.2
. This construction follows the same principles as the outcome-side network: adaptive feature learning, proximal closed-form updates for the last linear layer, and hybrid optimization in the second stage.

3.

Neural doubly robust unification.
Finally, in Section
E.3
, we combine the outcome- and treatment-bridge components into a fully neural doubly robust estimator of the dose-response curve. This final stage is designed to leverage the complementary strengths of both bridge functions.

E.1
Dose-response curve estimation: outcome bridge-based approach

We first describe the outcome-bridge component, which builds on the DFPCL architecture of
55
. We refer to this component as
OutcomeNet
. To distinguish it from the treatment-side construction introduced later in Section
E.2
, we attach the superscript
(h)
to all quantities associated with the outcome bridge.

Let
\mathcal{D}=\{(a_{i},y_{i},w_{i},z_{i},x_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N}
be an observational dataset of size
N
