. As in the fixed-feature multi-stage estimators, the learning procedure is split into two stages. The first stage learns a neural conditional mean embedding of the outcome proxy, while the second stage learns the outcome bridge itself. To reflect this structure, we partition
\mathcal{D}
into two subsets,

\mathcal{D}^{(h)}_{1}=\{(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{y}_{i},\bar{w}_{i},\bar{z}_{i},\bar{x}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{h}},\qquad\mathcal{D}^{(h)}_{2}=\{(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{y}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m_{h}},

where
\mathcal{D}^{(h)}_{1}
is used in the first stage and
\mathcal{D}^{(h)}_{2}
in the second stage. Depending on the implementation, these two subsets may be disjoint or partially overlapping.

The neural parameterization mirrors the two-stage bridge structure reviewed in the kernel setting. The outcome bridge function is modeled as

\displaystyle h(a,x,w)

\displaystyle={\bm{h}}^{\top}\left(\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(a)\otimes\phi_{X,2}^{(h)}(x)\otimes\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(w)\right),

(28)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(W)\mid A=a,X=x,Z=z\right]

\displaystyle=\left({\bm{V}}^{(h)}\right)^{\top}\phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(a,x,z).

(29)

Equation
29
is the first-stage neural conditional mean embedding: it predicts the second-stage proxy features
\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(W)
from the variables
(A,X,Z)
. Equation
28
then uses these features to evaluate the bridge function
h(a,x,w)
. This is the neural mean embedding analogue of the two-stage KPV construction reviewed earlier, but with learnable feature maps replacing fixed RKHS features.

More specifically:

•

\phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}:{\mathcal{A}}\times{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Z}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{h,1}}
denotes the first-stage feature extractor, parameterized by
\theta^{(h)}_{1}
.

•

\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}
,
\phi_{X,2}^{(h)}
, and
\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}
denote the second-stage feature extractors for
A
,
X
, and
W
, jointly parameterized by
\theta^{(h)}_{2}
.

•

{\bm{V}}^{(h)}
is the first-stage linear operator mapping the learned features of
(A,X,Z)
to predicted proxy features in the representation space of
W
.

•

{\bm{h}}
is the second-stage linear head mapping the tensorized representation of
(A,X,W)
to the scalar bridge value
h(a,x,w)
.

This parameterization separates the two roles played by the neural model. The first stage learns how the information contained in
(A,X,Z)
predicts the proxy representation of
W
. The second stage then uses this learned proxy representation to fit the outcome bridge. In this way, the architecture preserves the logic of proximal bridge estimation while allowing the representation spaces themselves to be adapted during training.

OutcomeNet is trained in two stages. The first stage learns a neural conditional mean embedding of the outcome proxy, and the second stage uses this learned representation to estimate the outcome bridge function. As in the fixed-feature setting, this two-stage structure is dictated by the bridge equation itself. The difference is that, in the neural setting, both the feature maps and the final linear layers are learned from data.

The first stage estimates the neural conditional mean embedding

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(W)\mid A=a,X=x,Z=z\right]=\left({\bm{V}}^{(h)}\right)^{\top}\phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(a,x,z)

by minimizing the regularized least-squares objective

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{h,1}(\theta_{1}^{(h)},{\bm{V}}^{(h)})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{h}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{h}}\left\|\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(\bar{w}_{i})-\left({\bm{V}}^{(h)}\right)^{\top}\phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{x}_{i},\bar{z}_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda^{(h)}_{1}\|{\bm{V}}^{(h)}\|_{F}^{2},

(30)

where
\lambda^{(h)}_{1}
is the regularization parameter for the first-stage linear layer and
\|\cdot\|_{F}
denotes the Frobenius norm. Importantly, although Equation
30
depends on the second-stage proxy featurizer
\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}
, the parameters
\theta_{W,2}^{(h)}
are not updated in this stage. Instead,
\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(W)
is treated as the target representation to be predicted from
(A,X,Z)
.

In the second stage, the learned first-stage operator
\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}
is used to construct predicted proxy features on
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(h)}
. The outcome bridge is then estimated by minimizing

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{h,2}(\theta_{2}^{(h)},{\bm{h}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m_{h}}\sum_{i=1}^{m_{h}}\Big(\tilde{y}_{i}-{\bm{h}}^{\top}\big(\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(\tilde{a}_{i})\otimes\phi_{X,2}^{(h)}(\tilde{x}_{i})\otimes\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}(\theta_{W,2}^{(h)})^{\top}\phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i})\big)\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\lambda^{(h)}_{2}\|{\bm{h}}\|_{2}^{2},

(31)

where
\theta_{2}^{(h)}=\{\theta_{A,2}^{(h)},\theta_{X,2}^{(h)},\theta_{W,2}^{(h)}\}
collects the parameters of the second-stage featurizers.

A key difficulty is that Equation
31
depends on
\theta_{W,2}^{(h)}
only implicitly, through the first-stage optimum
\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}(\theta_{W,2}^{(h)})
. Indeed, changing
\theta_{W,2}^{(h)}
changes the target representation in the first stage, and therefore also changes the first-stage optimum itself. Following
55
,
52
, this dependence is handled approximately by differentiating through the closed-form solution for
\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}(\theta_{W,2}^{(h)})
, while treating the first-stage feature representation
\phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}
as fixed. This yields a tractable approximation to the full bi-level gradient.

The resulting learning procedure alternates between gradient-based updates of the neural feature extractors and analytical updates of the final linear layers.

E.1.0.0.1
Neural parameter update (Stage 1).

The first-stage feature extractor is updated by a gradient step on Equation
30
with learning rate
\eta_{1}^{(h)}
:

\theta_{1}^{(h)}\leftarrow\theta_{1}^{(h)}-\eta_{1}^{(h)}\frac{\partial\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{h,1}}{\partial\theta_{1}^{(h)}}.

E.1.0.0.2
Closed-form update of the first-stage linear layer.

Fixing the feature extractors, the first-stage operator
{\bm{V}}^{(h)}
is updated analytically as

\displaystyle\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}(\theta_{W,2}^{(h)})=\left(\Phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}\Phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)\top}+n_{h}\lambda^{(h)}_{1}{\bm{I}}\right)^{-1}\Phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}\Phi_{W,2}^{(h)\top},

(32)

where the feature matrices are formed from
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(h)}
as

\displaystyle\Phi_{W,2}^{(h)}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(\bar{w}_{1})&\cdots&\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(\bar{w}_{n_{h}})\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{W,2}^{(h)}\times n_{h}},

\displaystyle\Phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(\bar{a}_{1},\bar{x}_{1},\bar{z}_{1})&\cdots&\phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(\bar{a}_{n_{h}},\bar{x}_{n_{h}},\bar{z}_{n_{h}})\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{h,1}\times n_{h}}.

Here
d_{W,2}^{(h)}
is the output dimension of
\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}
, and
d_{h,1}
is the output dimension of
\phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}
.

E.1.0.0.3
Neural parameter update (Stage 2).

The second-stage featurizers are updated by a gradient step on Equation
31
with learning rate
\eta_{2}^{(h)}
:

\theta_{2}^{(h)}\leftarrow\theta_{2}^{(h)}-\eta_{2}^{(h)}\frac{\partial\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{h,2}}{\partial\theta_{2}^{(h)}}.

For
\theta_{W,2}^{(h)}
, the gradient is propagated through the closed-form expression in Equation
32
.

E.1.0.0.4
Closed-form update of the second-stage linear layer.

Fixing the second-stage featurizers, the final linear head
{\bm{h}}
is updated analytically as

\displaystyle\hat{{\bm{h}}}=\left(\Psi_{2}^{(h)}\Psi_{2}^{(h)\top}+m_{h}\lambda^{(h)}_{2}{\bm{I}}\right)^{-1}\Psi_{2}^{(h)}{\bm{Y}}_{2}^{\top},

(33)

where the
i
-th column of
\Psi_{2}^{(h)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{h,2}\times m_{h}}
is

\displaystyle\Psi_{2,i}^{(h)}

\displaystyle=\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(\tilde{a}_{i})\otimes\phi_{X,2}^{(h)}(\tilde{x}_{i})\otimes\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)\top}\phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i}),\qquad i=1,\ldots,m_{h},

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}^{(h)}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\Psi_{2,1}^{(h)}&\cdots&\Psi_{2,m_{h}}^{(h)}\end{bmatrix},\kern 5.0pt\text{and}\quad{\bm{Y}}_{2}=\begin{bmatrix}\tilde{y}_{1}&\cdots&\tilde{y}_{m_{h}}\end{bmatrix}.

Here
d_{h,2}=d_{A,2}^{(h)}d_{X,2}^{(h)}d_{W,2}^{(h)}
.

Following
55
,
52
, it is helpful to perform several first-stage updates before each second-stage update. In practice, we carry out
T_{h,1}
first-stage updates for every
T_{h,2}
second-stage updates, typically with
T_{h,1}\geq T_{h,2}
, so that the neural conditional mean embedding remains sufficiently accurate throughout training.

E.1.0.0.5
Proximal closed-form updates.

The analytical updates in Equations
32
and
33
are exact minimizers of the corresponding quadratic objectives on a fixed dataset. In stochastic mini-batch training, however, directly replacing the last-layer parameters by these batchwise optima can lead to unstable trajectories, since each batch induces a different local optimum. Following
18
, we therefore replace the zero-centered ridge penalties by proximal penalties centered at the previous iterates. This keeps the batchwise closed-form updates close to the current parameter state and substantially stabilizes training.

In the present two-stage setting, there is one additional subtlety. The stage-2 loss depends on the first-stage optimum through the predicted proxy features. Consequently, in addition to the persistent first-stage iterate
\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}_{t}
, we introduce an auxiliary first-stage solution that is recomputed
on the fly
on the current second-stage batch. This auxiliary operator is not stored as a separate model parameter. Its only role is to evaluate the second-stage loss with the current proxy representation
\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}
. To define it, note that
\mathcal{D}^{(h)}_{2}
must also retain the proxy observations
\tilde{w}_{i}
, even though they do not appear explicitly in the quadratic bridge loss.

Let
\mathcal{B}^{(h)}_{1}\subset\mathcal{D}^{(h)}_{1}
and
\mathcal{B}^{(h)}_{2}\subset\mathcal{D}^{(h)}_{2}
denote mini-batches sampled at iteration
t
. We define the proximal batch losses

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{h,1}^{\mathrm{prox}}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(h)},{\bm{V}}^{(h)};\theta_{2}^{(h)},\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)}}\left\|\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(\bar{w}_{i})-\left({\bm{V}}^{(h)}\right)^{\top}\phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{x}_{i},\bar{z}_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{1}^{(h)}\left\|{\bm{V}}^{(h)}-\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}_{t}\right\|_{F}^{2},

(34)

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{h,2}^{\mathrm{prox}}\!\left(\theta_{2}^{(h)},{\bm{h}};\theta_{1}^{(h)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}}\Big(\tilde{y}_{i}-{\bm{h}}^{\top}\big(\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(\tilde{a}_{i})\otimes\phi_{X,2}^{(h)}(\tilde{x}_{i})\otimes\check{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)\top}_{t}\phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i})\big)\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\lambda_{2}^{(h)}\left\|{\bm{h}}-\hat{{\bm{h}}}_{t}\right\|_{2}^{2},

(35)

where
\lambda_{1}^{(h)}
and
\lambda_{2}^{(h)}
are proximal regularization parameters,
\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}_{t}
and
\hat{{\bm{h}}}_{t}
are the previous iterates, and
\check{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}_{t}
denotes the auxiliary on-the-fly first-stage solution computed from the current second-stage batch.

More precisely, we define the persistent first-stage update by

\displaystyle\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}_{t+1}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(h)};\theta_{2}^{(h)},\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)}\right)=\argmin_{{\bm{V}}^{(h)}}\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{h,1}^{\mathrm{prox}}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(h)},{\bm{V}}^{(h)};\theta_{2}^{(h)},\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)}\right),

(36)

and the auxiliary on-the-fly first-stage operator by

\displaystyle\check{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}_{t}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(h)};\theta_{2}^{(h)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}\right)=\argmin_{{\bm{V}}^{(h)}}\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{h,1}^{\mathrm{prox}}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(h)},{\bm{V}}^{(h)};\theta_{2}^{(h)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}\right).

(37)

The first quantity is the actual stage-1 parameter update, while the second is an auxiliary batchwise solve used only inside the stage-2 objective.

Given
\check{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}_{t}
, the second-stage proximal update is

\displaystyle\hat{{\bm{h}}}_{t+1}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(h)};\theta_{2}^{(h)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}\right)=\argmin_{{\bm{h}}}\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{h,2}^{\mathrm{prox}}\!\left(\theta_{2}^{(h)},{\bm{h}};\theta_{1}^{(h)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}\right).

(38)

Expanding these minimizers yields the proximal closed-form updates. For any batch
\mathcal{B}
, let

\Phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B})=\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(a_{i},x_{i},z_{i})\end{bmatrix}_{i\in\mathcal{B}},\qquad\Phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B})=\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(w_{i})\end{bmatrix}_{i\in\mathcal{B}},

where the tuples
(a_{i},x_{i},z_{i},w_{i})
are read from the corresponding batch. Then

\displaystyle\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}_{t+1}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(h)};\theta_{2}^{(h)},\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\Phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)})\Phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)}|\lambda_{1}^{(h)}{\bm{I}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(\Phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)})\Phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)}|\lambda_{1}^{(h)}\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}_{t}\right),

(39)

\displaystyle\check{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}_{t}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(h)};\theta_{2}^{(h)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\Phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)})\Phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}|\lambda_{1}^{(h)}{\bm{I}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(\Phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)})\Phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}|\lambda_{1}^{(h)}\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}_{t}\right).

(40)

The corresponding second-stage feature matrix is built from
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}
columnwise as

\Psi_{2,i}^{(h)}=\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(\tilde{a}_{i})\otimes\phi_{X,2}^{(h)}(\tilde{x}_{i})\otimes\check{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)\top}_{t}\phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i}),\quad i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)},

and we write

\Psi_{2}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)})=\begin{bmatrix}\Psi_{2,i}^{(h)}\end{bmatrix}_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}},\quad{\bm{Y}}_{2}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)})=\begin{bmatrix}\tilde{y}_{i}\end{bmatrix}_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}}.

Then the second-stage proximal update is

\displaystyle\hat{{\bm{h}}}_{t+1}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(h)};\theta_{2}^{(h)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\Psi_{2}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)})\Psi_{2}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}|\lambda_{2}^{(h)}{\bm{I}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(\Psi_{2}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}){\bm{Y}}_{2}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}|\lambda_{2}^{(h)}\hat{{\bm{h}}}_{t}\right).

(41)

Equivalently, the batch-size factors may be absorbed into
\lambda_{1}^{(h)}
and
\lambda_{2}^{(h)}
; we write them explicitly here only to match the averaged losses in Equations
34
and
35
.

Finally, note that the stage-2 loss depends on
\theta_{W,2}^{(h)}
through the auxiliary operator
\check{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}_{t}
. In practice, gradients with respect to
\theta_{W,2}^{(h)}
are therefore propagated through the closed-form mapping in Equation
40
, while the first-stage representation
\phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}
is treated as fixed, exactly as in the non-proximal DFPCL update.

E.1.0.0.6
Flexible second-stage optimization with robust loss functions.

The proximal update in Equation
41
is available only for a quadratic second-stage loss. To allow greater flexibility, we replace the squared loss in the second stage by a general differentiable regression loss
\ell_{h,2}(\cdot,\cdot)
. In particular, this allows the use of robust losses such as the Huber loss
[
22
]
, which can be less sensitive to outliers than mean squared error.

To define the resulting objective, recall that the stage-2 prediction uses the auxiliary first-stage operator computed on the current second-stage batch. The generalized second-stage batch loss is then

\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{h,2}^{\mathrm{gen}}\!\left(\theta_{2}^{(h)},{\bm{h}};\theta_{1}^{(h)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}\right)=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}}\ell_{h,2}\!\left(\tilde{y}_{i},{\bm{h}}^{\top}\Psi_{2,i}^{(h)}\right)+\lambda_{2}^{(h)}\|{\bm{h}}-\hat{{\bm{h}}}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}.

(42)

Once the loss is no longer quadratic, the exact proximal closed-form update for
{\bm{h}}
is no longer available. We therefore use a two-step strategy inside each stage-2 iteration.

1.

Featurizer update.

We first update the second-stage neural parameters
\theta_{2}^{(h)}
by a gradient step on Equation
42
, using the current estimate of the head and the current auxiliary first-stage operator
\check{{\bm{V}}}_{t}^{(h)}
.

2.

Head refinement.

Holding the feature extractors fixed, we then refine the second-stage linear head
{\bm{h}}
by approximately minimizing Equation
42
with respect to
{\bm{h}}
. In our implementation, this inner optimization is performed by
K_{h}
steps of L-BFGS
[
31
]
, using the
PyTorch
implementation
[
2
]
.

This numerical refinement plays the same role as the closed-form update in the quadratic case: it keeps the final linear layer close to the minimizer of the current second-stage objective. When
\ell_{h,2}
is chosen to be the squared loss and the inner optimization is run to convergence, the resulting update recovers the proximal quadratic solution in Equation
41
. The advantage of Equation
42
is therefore not conceptual but practical: it preserves the same multi-stage bridge-learning structure while allowing robust second-stage losses.

The complete OutcomeNet training procedure is summarized in Algorithm
1
.

Input:
Datasets
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(h)}=\{(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{w}_{i},\bar{z}_{i},\bar{x}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{h}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(h)}=\{(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{y}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m_{h}}
.

Parameters:
Neural parameters
\theta_{1}^{(h)},\theta_{2}^{(h)}
; linear layers
{\bm{V}}^{(h)},{\bm{h}}
.

Design choices:
Second-stage loss
\ell_{h,2}
.

Hyperparameters:
Learning rates
\eta_{1}^{(h)},\eta_{2}^{(h)}
; proximal coefficients
\lambda_{1}^{(h)},\lambda_{2}^{(h)}
; update counts
T_{h,1},T_{h,2}
; inner optimization count
K_{h}
.

Output:
Optimized OutcomeNet parameters
\{\theta_{1}^{(h)},\theta_{2}^{(h)},\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)},\hat{{\bm{h}}}\}
.

1:

repeat

2:

Sample mini-batches
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)}\subset\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(h)}
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}\subset\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(h)}
.

3:

for

t_{1}=1
to
T_{h,1}

do

4:

Update
\theta_{1}^{(h)}
by one gradient step on Equation
34
using
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)}
.

5:

Update the persistent first-stage operator
\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}
with Equation
39
using
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)}
.

6:

end

for

7:

for

t_{2}=1
to
T_{h,2}

do

8:

Compute the auxiliary first-stage operator
\check{{\bm{V}}}_{t}^{(h)}
on
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}
with Equation
40
.

9:

Update
\theta_{2}^{(h)}
by one gradient step on Equation
42
using
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}
.

10:

Recompute
\check{{\bm{V}}}_{t}^{(h)}
on
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}
with the updated
\theta_{2}^{(h)}
.

11:

Update
{\bm{h}}
by running
K_{h}
steps of L-BFGS on Equation
42
, holding
\theta_{2}^{(h)}
fixed.

12:

end

for

13:

until
convergence

Algorithm 1

Outcome bridge network (OutcomeNet)

E.1.0.0.7
Dose-response estimation.

Once the outcome bridge
\hat{h}
has been learned, the dose-response curve is obtained by plugging
\hat{h}
into the identifying representation
f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)]
. Concretely, given an evaluation sample
\mathcal{D}_{3}^{(h)}=\{(x_{i}^{\circ},w_{i}^{\circ})\}_{i=1}^{t_{h}}
drawn from the observed marginal law of
(X,W)
, we estimate the dose-response by the empirical average

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{(h)}(a)=\frac{1}{t_{h}}\sum_{i=1}^{t_{h}}\hat{h}(a,x_{i}^{\circ},w_{i}^{\circ}).

(43)

Using Equation
28
, this estimator can be written equivalently as

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{(h)}(a)=\hat{{\bm{h}}}^{\top}\left(\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(a)\otimes\hat{\mu}_{XW}^{(h)}\right),\qquad\hat{\mu}_{XW}^{(h)}:=\frac{1}{t_{h}}\sum_{i=1}^{t_{h}}\phi_{X,2}^{(h)}(x_{i}^{\circ})\otimes\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(w_{i}^{\circ}).

Thus, after learning the bridge function, dose-response estimation reduces to averaging the learned bridge over the empirical distribution of the observed covariates and outcome proxies. In practice,
\mathcal{D}_{3}^{(h)}
may be chosen as a held-out sample or as one of the previously used splits. In our implementation, we use the second-stage sample
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(h)}
for this empirical averaging step.

E.2
Dose-response curve estimation: treatment bridge-based approach

We now introduce the treatment bridge-based component of our neural mean embedding framework, which we refer to as
TreatmentNet
. In contrast to the kernel KAP derivation reviewed earlier, the present neural formulation explicitly incorporates the observed covariates
X
. The target of this component is the treatment bridge
\varphi_{0}(a,x,z)
, which satisfies the conditional moment relation

\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a,X,W]=r(a,X,W),\qquad r(a,x,w):=\frac{p_{A}(a)\,p_{X,W}(x,w)}{p_{A,X,W}(a,x,w)}.

Accordingly, we view treatment-bridge learning as a regression problem in which the unknown conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}[\varphi(A,X,Z)\mid A,X,W]
is fitted to the density-ratio target
r(A,X,W)
.

In practice, the ratio
r(A,X,W)
is not known and is replaced by a pre-computed estimator
\hat{r}(A,X,W)
; see Appendix
I.1
for details. Let
\mathcal{D}=\{(a_{i},y_{i},w_{i},z_{i},x_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N}
be the full dataset, and split it into

\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(\varphi)}=\{(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{x}_{i},\bar{w}_{i},\bar{z}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\varphi}},\qquad\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(\varphi)}=\{(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i},\tilde{\hat{r}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m_{\varphi}},

where
\tilde{\hat{r}}_{i}:=\hat{r}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i})
. The first split is used to learn the conditional mean embedding of the treatment proxy, and the second split is used to learn the treatment bridge.

The treatment bridge and the first-stage neural conditional mean embedding are parameterized as

\displaystyle\varphi(a,x,z)

\displaystyle=\bm{\varphi}^{\top}\left(\phi_{AX,2}^{(\varphi)}(a,x)\otimes\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(z)\right),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(Z)\mid A=a,X=x,W=w]

\displaystyle=\left({\bm{V}}^{(\varphi)}\right)^{\top}\phi_{AXW,1}^{(\varphi)}(a,x,w).

Here:

•

\phi_{AXW,1}^{(\varphi)}:{\mathcal{A}}\times{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{W}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\varphi,1}}
is the first-stage feature extractor, parameterized by
\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)}
.

•

\phi_{AX,2}^{(\varphi)}
and
\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}
are the second-stage feature extractors, jointly parameterized by
\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)}
.

•

{\bm{V}}^{(\varphi)}
is the first-stage linear operator mapping features of
(A,X,W)
to predicted proxy features.

•

\bm{\varphi}
is the second-stage linear head mapping the tensorized representation of
(A,X,Z)
to the bridge value
\varphi(a,x,z)
.

As on the outcome side, TreatmentNet is trained by a bi-level multi-stage procedure. The first stage learns the conditional mean embedding of the proxy representation
\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(Z)
, and the second stage regresses the density-ratio target
\hat{r}
onto the resulting learned representation.

The first-stage batch loss, for a mini-batch
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)}\subset\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(\varphi)}
, is

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\varphi,1}^{\mathrm{prox}}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)},{\bm{V}}^{(\varphi)};\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)},\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)}}\left\|\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(\bar{z}_{i})-\left({\bm{V}}^{(\varphi)}\right)^{\top}\phi_{AXW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{x}_{i},\bar{w}_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{1}^{(\varphi)}\left\|{\bm{V}}^{(\varphi)}-\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(\varphi)}_{t}\right\|_{F}^{2}.

(44)

As in OutcomeNet, the targets
\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(Z)
depend on the second-stage proxy featurizer, but
\theta_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}
is not updated in this stage; the feature map acts only as the target representation.

For fixed
\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)}
and
\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)}
, the proximal first-stage minimizer is

\displaystyle\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(\varphi)}_{t+1}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)};\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)},\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\Phi_{AXW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)})\Phi_{AXW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)}|\lambda_{1}^{(\varphi)}{\bm{I}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\Phi_{AXW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)})\Phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)}|\lambda_{1}^{(\varphi)}\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(\varphi)}_{t}\right),

(45)

where

\Phi_{AXW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B})=\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{AXW,1}^{(\varphi)}(a_{i},x_{i},w_{i})\end{bmatrix}_{i\in\mathcal{B}},\quad\Phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B})=\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(z_{i})\end{bmatrix}_{i\in\mathcal{B}}.

The second stage is trained on a mini-batch
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}\subset\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(\varphi)}
. As on the outcome side, the second-stage prediction depends on the current first-stage optimum. We therefore distinguish between:

•

the
persistent
first-stage operator
\hat{{\bm{V}}}_{t}^{(\varphi)}
, which is updated on batches from
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(\varphi)}
, and

•

an
auxiliary on-the-fly
operator
\check{{\bm{V}}}_{t}^{(\varphi)}
, recomputed on the current second-stage batch
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}
.

The auxiliary operator is not stored as a separate model parameter. Its only role is to evaluate the second-stage loss using the current proxy feature representation
\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}
, exactly as in the outcome-bridge formulation.

Formally,
\check{{\bm{V}}}_{t}^{(\varphi)}
is defined as the minimizer of the first-stage proximal objective evaluated on
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}
:

\displaystyle\check{{\bm{V}}}^{(\varphi)}_{t}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)};\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\Phi_{AXW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)})\Phi_{AXW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}|\lambda_{1}^{(\varphi)}{\bm{I}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\Phi_{AXW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)})\Phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}|\lambda_{1}^{(\varphi)}\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(\varphi)}_{t}\right).

(46)

Using this auxiliary operator, the second-stage feature vector for sample
i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}
is

\Psi_{2,i}^{(\varphi)}=\phi_{AX,2}^{(\varphi)}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i})\otimes\check{{\bm{V}}}_{t}^{(\varphi)\top}\phi_{AXW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i}).

The quadratic second-stage proximal loss is therefore

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\varphi,2}^{\mathrm{prox}}\!\left(\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)},\bm{\varphi};\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}}\Big(\tilde{\hat{r}}_{i}-\bm{\varphi}^{\top}\Psi_{2,i}^{(\varphi)}\Big)^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{(\varphi)}\left\|\bm{\varphi}-\hat{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(47)

A difficulty, analogous to the one encountered in OutcomeNet, is that Equation
47
depends on
\theta_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}
only implicitly, through the first-stage optimum
\check{{\bm{V}}}_{t}^{(\varphi)}
. Following
55
,
52
, we handle this dependence by differentiating through the closed-form mapping in Equation
46
, while treating the first-stage feature matrix
\Phi_{AXW,1}^{(\varphi)}
as fixed when computing gradients with respect to
\theta_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}
.

For fixed features, the corresponding proximal second-stage minimizer is

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\varphi}}_{t+1}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)};\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\Psi_{2}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)})\Psi_{2}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}|\lambda_{2}^{(\varphi)}{\bm{I}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\Psi_{2}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}){\bm{R}}_{2}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}|\lambda_{2}^{(\varphi)}\hat{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}\right),

(48)

where

\Psi_{2}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)})=\begin{bmatrix}\Psi_{2,i}^{(\varphi)}\end{bmatrix}_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}},\quad{\bm{R}}_{2}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)})=\begin{bmatrix}\tilde{\hat{r}}_{i}\end{bmatrix}_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}}.

As on the outcome side, the closed-form update above is available only for a quadratic second-stage loss. To allow greater flexibility, we replace the squared loss in Equation
47
by a general differentiable regression loss
\ell^{(\varphi)}_{2}(\cdot,\cdot)
, leading to

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\varphi,2}^{\mathrm{gen}}\!\left(\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)},\bm{\varphi};\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}}\ell^{(\varphi)}_{2}\!\left(\tilde{\hat{r}}_{i},\,\bm{\varphi}^{\top}\Psi_{2,i}^{(\varphi)}\right)+\lambda_{2}^{(\varphi)}\left\|\bm{\varphi}-\hat{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(49)

As in OutcomeNet, we optimize Equation
49
by a two-step stage-2 procedure: first updating the neural feature extractors
\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)}
by gradient descent, and then approximately minimizing the resulting objective with respect to
\bm{\varphi}
by running
K_{\varphi}
steps of L-BFGS
[
31
]
, using the
PyTorch
implementation
[
2
]
.

The complete training procedure is summarized in Algorithm
2
.

Input:
Datasets
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(\varphi)}=\{(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{x}_{i},\bar{w}_{i},\bar{z}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\varphi}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(\varphi)}=\{(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i},\tilde{\hat{r}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m_{\varphi}}
, where
\tilde{\hat{r}}_{i}
denotes a pre-computed estimate of
r(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i})
(see Appendix
I.1
).

Parameters:
Neural parameters
\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)},\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)}
; linear layers
{\bm{V}}^{(\varphi)},\bm{\varphi}
.

Design choice:
Second-stage loss
\ell^{(\varphi)}_{2}
.

Hyperparameters:
Learning rates
\eta_{1}^{(\varphi)},\eta_{2}^{(\varphi)}
; proximal coefficients
\lambda_{1}^{(\varphi)},\lambda_{2}^{(\varphi)}
; update counts
T_{\varphi,1},T_{\varphi,2}
; inner optimization count
K_{\varphi}
.

Output:
Optimized TreatmentNet parameters
\{\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)},\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)},\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(\varphi)},\hat{\bm{\varphi}}\}
.

1:

repeat

2:

Sample mini-batches
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)}\subset\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(\varphi)}
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}\subset\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(\varphi)}
.

3:

for

t_{1}=1
to
T_{\varphi,1}

do

4:

Update
\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)}
by one gradient step on Equation
44
using
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)}
.

5:

Update the persistent first-stage operator
\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(\varphi)}
with Equation
45
using
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)}
.

6:

end

for

7:

for

t_{2}=1
to
T_{\varphi,2}

do

8:

Compute the auxiliary first-stage operator
\check{{\bm{V}}}_{t}^{(\varphi)}
on
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}
with Equation
46
.

9:

Update
\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)}
by one gradient step on Equation
49
using
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}
.

10:

Recompute
\check{{\bm{V}}}_{t}^{(\varphi)}
on
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}
with the updated
\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)}
.

11:

Update
\bm{\varphi}
by running
K_{\varphi}
steps of L-BFGS on Equation
49
, holding
\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)}
fixed.

12:

end

for

13:

until
convergence

Algorithm 2

Treatment bridge network (TreatmentNet)

E.2.0.0.1
Dose-response estimation with the treatment bridge.

The preceding two-stage procedure yields an estimator
\hat{\varphi}
of the treatment bridge satisfying the identifying relation in Equation
2
. To recover the final dose-response curve, we must still estimate the conditional mean

f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a].

Unlike the outcome-bridge formulation, this target is not obtained by a simple marginal average; it is itself a regression function in the treatment level
a
. We therefore introduce a third stage, in which the bridge-transformed outcome is regressed on the treatment.

To this end, we construct a pseudo-outcome dataset

\mathcal{D}_{3}^{(\varphi)}=\{(a_{k},y_{k}^{\mathrm{pseudo}})\}_{k=1}^{n_{\varphi,3}},

where

y_{k}^{\mathrm{pseudo}}=y_{k}\,\hat{\varphi}(a_{k},x_{k},z_{k}),\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\varphi}(a_{k},x_{k},z_{k})=\hat{\bm{\varphi}}^{\top}\left(\phi_{AX,2}^{(\varphi)}(a_{k},x_{k})\otimes\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(z_{k})\right).

The third stage then fits a regression network
f^{(\varphi)}(\cdot;\theta_{3}^{(\varphi)})
to approximate

f^{(\varphi)}(a;\theta_{3}^{(\varphi)})\approx\mathbb{E}[Y\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a].

In practice,
f^{(\varphi)}(\cdot;\theta_{3}^{(\varphi)})
is trained by minimizing a regression loss over
\mathcal{D}_{3}^{(\varphi)}
,

\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\varphi,3}(\theta_{3}^{(\varphi)})=\frac{1}{n_{\varphi,3}}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{\varphi,3}}\ell^{(\varphi)}_{3}\!\left(y_{k}^{\mathrm{pseudo}},f^{(\varphi)}(a_{k};\theta_{3}^{(\varphi)})\right),

(50)

where
\ell^{(\varphi)}_{3}
may be any differentiable regression loss. Unless otherwise stated, we use the squared loss in our implementation. This final stage converts the bridge-transformed observations into an explicit estimate of the dose-response curve as a function of
a
. In our implementation,
\mathcal{D}_{3}^{(\varphi)}
is constructed from the second-stage split
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(\varphi)}
, although a separate third-stage split could also be used.

E.3
Dose-response curve estimation: doubly robust approach

We now combine the learned outcome and treatment bridges into a doubly robust estimator of the dose-response curve. Recall the identifying representation

f_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR)}}(a;h_{0},\varphi_{0})=\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)\{Y-h_{0}(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a]+\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)].

Given estimators
\hat{h}
from OutcomeNet and
\hat{\varphi}
from TreatmentNet, the only remaining task is to estimate the conditional expectation terms appearing in this formula. We describe two implementations of this final stage. The first one is the version used in our main experiments.

Let

\mathcal{D}^{(\kappa)}=\{(a_{i},y_{i},w_{i},z_{i},x_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}}

be a sample on which both
\hat{h}
and
\hat{\varphi}
can be evaluated. In our implementation, we reuse the second-stage split for this purpose, although a separate split could also be used.

E.3.0.0.1
Version 1: direct residual regression.

The most direct implementation is to regress the bridge-weighted residual

\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\{Y-\hat{h}(a,X,W)\}

on the treatment value
A
. We therefore construct the pseudo-outcome dataset

\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(\kappa)}=\{(a_{i},y_{i}^{(\kappa,1)})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}},\quad\text{where}\quad y_{i}^{(\kappa,1)}=\hat{\varphi}(a_{i},x_{i},z_{i})\bigl(y_{i}-\hat{h}(a_{i},x_{i},w_{i})\bigr).

We then fit a regression network
k^{(\kappa,1)}(\cdot;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa)})
to approximate

k^{(\kappa,1)}(a;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa)})\approx\mathbb{E}[\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\{Y-\hat{h}(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a].

More generally, this network can be trained with any differentiable regression loss. Writing
\ell^{(\kappa)}_{1}
for this loss, the third-stage objective is

\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\kappa,1}(\theta_{1}^{(\kappa)})=\frac{1}{n_{\kappa}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}}\ell^{(\kappa)}_{1}\!\left(y_{i}^{(\kappa,1)},k^{(\kappa,1)}(a_{i};\theta_{1}^{(\kappa)})\right).

(51)

Combining this residual regression with the outcome-bridge estimator yields

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR1)}}(a)

\displaystyle=\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{(h)}(a)+k^{(\kappa,1)}(a;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa)})=\frac{1}{n_{\kappa}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}}\hat{h}(a,x_{i},w_{i})+k^{(\kappa,1)}(a;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa)}).

(52)

This is the most direct implementation of the doubly robust formula, since the additional network learns the entire correction term in one step.We name this version DRPCLNET V1.

E.3.0.0.2
Version 2: decoupled decomposition.

An alternative implementation is obtained by expanding the doubly robust formula as

f_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR)}}(a;h_{0},\varphi_{0})=\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)]+\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a]-\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)h_{0}(a,X,W)\mid A=a].

The first two terms are already estimated by OutcomeNet and TreatmentNet, respectively. It therefore remains only to estimate the interaction term

\mathbb{E}[\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\hat{h}(a,X,W)\mid A=a].

For this purpose, we construct the pseudo-outcome dataset

\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(\kappa)}=\{(a_{i},y_{i}^{(\kappa,2)})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}},\qquad y_{i}^{(\kappa,2)}=\hat{\varphi}(a_{i},x_{i},z_{i})\hat{h}(a_{i},x_{i},w_{i}),

and fit a regression network
k^{(\kappa,2)}(\cdot;\theta_{2}^{(\kappa)})
such that

k^{(\kappa,2)}(a;\theta_{2}^{(\kappa)})\approx\mathbb{E}[\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\hat{h}(a,X,W)\mid A=a].

As above, this network may be trained with any differentiable regression loss. Denoting this loss by
\ell^{(\kappa)}_{2}
, we minimize

\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\kappa,2}(\theta_{2}^{(\kappa)})=\frac{1}{n_{\kappa}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}}\ell^{(\kappa)}_{2}\!\left(y_{i}^{(\kappa,2)},k^{(\kappa,2)}(a_{i};\theta_{2}^{(\kappa)})\right).

(53)

The resulting decoupled doubly robust estimator is

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR2)}}(a)

\displaystyle=\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{(h)}(a)+\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{(\varphi)}(a)-k^{(\kappa,2)}(a;\theta_{2}^{(\kappa)})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{\kappa}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}}\hat{h}(a,x_{i},w_{i})+f^{(\varphi)}(a;\theta_{3}^{(\varphi)})-k^{(\kappa,2)}(a;\theta_{2}^{(\kappa)}).

(54)

In particular, Version 2 reuses the treatment-bridge third-stage regression and only learns the interaction term. We name this version DRPCLNET V2.

The complete training procedures for DRPCLNET (V1) and (V2) are summarized in Algorithms
3
and
4

Input:

\mathcal{D}^{(\kappa)}=\{(a_{i},y_{i},w_{i},z_{i},x_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}}
on which both
\hat{h}
and
\hat{\varphi}
can be evaluated.

and

.

\ell^{(\kappa)}_{1}
.

and

, together with the optimization hyperparameters for the correction network
k^{(\kappa,1)}(\cdot;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa)})

.

to obtain

on

.

Appendix F
Neural mean embedding-based proxy causal learning for heterogeneous dose-response

In this section, we derive the doubly robust algorithm for heterogeneous dose-response curve. In particular, similar to dose-response counterpart, we firs derive the outcome bridge-based algorithm. Then, we derive the analogous treatment bridge algorithm, and then combine both into doubly robust algorithm.

F.1
Heterogeneous dose-response estimation: outcome bridge method

Throughout this subsection, we write
X=(S,V)
, where
V
denotes the conditioning variable and
S
collects the remaining observed covariates. Recall that the target is given by

f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v)=\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,v,S,W)\mid V=v].

According to Theorem
3.1
, the corresponding outcome bridge must satisfy

\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,v,S,W)\mid A=a,V=v,S,Z]=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid A=a,V=v,S,Z].

Thus, the overall learning procedure remains the same as in Appendix
E.1
: the first stage learns a neural conditional mean embedding of the outcome proxy, and the second stage learns the outcome bridge itself. The only structural change is that the heterogeneity variable
V
is now included explicitly in both stages.

Let

\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(h)}=\{(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{v}_{i},\bar{s}_{i},\bar{z}_{i},\bar{w}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{h}},\quad\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(h)}=\{(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{v}_{i},\tilde{s}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i},\tilde{y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m_{h}}

denote the first- and second-stage data splits. We parameterize the bridge and the first-stage neural conditional mean embedding as

\displaystyle h(a,v,s,w)

\displaystyle={\bm{h}}^{\top}\left(\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(a)\otimes\phi_{V,2}^{(h)}(v)\otimes\phi_{S,2}^{(h)}(s)\otimes\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(w)\right),

(55)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(W)\mid A=a,V=v,S=s,Z=z]

\displaystyle=\left({\bm{V}}^{(h)}\right)^{\top}\phi_{AVSZ,1}^{(h)}(a,v,s,z).

(56)

Here
\phi_{AVSZ,1}^{(h)}
is the first-stage feature extractor, parameterized by
\theta_{1}^{(h)}
, while
\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}
,
\phi_{V,2}^{(h)}
,
\phi_{S,2}^{(h)}
, and
\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}
are the second-stage feature extractors, jointly parameterized by
\theta_{2}^{(h)}
.

For a mini-batch
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)}\subset\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(h)}
, the first-stage proximal loss is

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{h,1}^{\mathrm{prox}}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(h)},{\bm{V}}^{(h)};\theta_{2}^{(h)},\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)}}\left\|\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(\bar{w}_{i})-\left({\bm{V}}^{(h)}\right)^{\top}\phi_{AVSZ,1}^{(h)}(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{v}_{i},\bar{s}_{i},\bar{z}_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{1}^{(h)}\left\|{\bm{V}}^{(h)}-\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}_{t}\right\|_{F}^{2}.

(57)

As in the population-level case, the proxy feature map
\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}
acts as the first-stage target and is not updated during this stage.

For fixed
\theta_{1}^{(h)}
and
\theta_{2}^{(h)}
, the corresponding proximal first-stage minimizer is

\displaystyle\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}_{t+1}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(h)};\theta_{2}^{(h)},\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\Phi_{AVSZ,1}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)})\Phi_{AVSZ,1}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)}|\lambda_{1}^{(h)}{\bm{I}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\Phi_{AVSZ,1}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)})\Phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(h)}|\lambda_{1}^{(h)}\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}_{t}\right),

(58)

where

\Phi_{AVSZ,1}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B})=\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{AVSZ,1}^{(h)}(a_{i},v_{i},s_{i},z_{i})\end{bmatrix}_{i\in\mathcal{B}},\quad\text{and}\quad\Phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B})=\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(w_{i})\end{bmatrix}_{i\in\mathcal{B}}.

The second stage again requires an auxiliary on-the-fly first-stage solve on the current second-stage batch. Specifically, for a mini-batch
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}\subset\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(h)}
, we define

\displaystyle\check{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}_{t}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(h)};\theta_{2}^{(h)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\Phi_{AVSZ,1}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)})\Phi_{AVSZ,1}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}|\lambda_{1}^{(h)}{\bm{I}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\Phi_{AVSZ,1}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)})\Phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}|\lambda_{1}^{(h)}\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}_{t}\right).

(59)

As before, this auxiliary operator is not a separate model parameter; it is recomputed only to evaluate the current second-stage loss with the updated proxy representation.

Using
\check{{\bm{V}}}^{(h)}_{t}
, the heterogeneous second-stage feature vector is

\Psi_{2,i}^{(h)}=\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(\tilde{a}_{i})\otimes\phi_{V,2}^{(h)}(\tilde{v}_{i})\otimes\phi_{S,2}^{(h)}(\tilde{s}_{i})\otimes\check{{\bm{V}}}_{t}^{(h)\top}\phi_{AVSZ,1}^{(h)}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{v}_{i},\tilde{s}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i}),\qquad i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}.

For a general differentiable regression loss
\ell_{h,2}
, the second-stage objective becomes

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{h,2}^{\mathrm{gen}}\!\left(\theta_{2}^{(h)},{\bm{h}};\theta_{1}^{(h)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}}\ell_{h,2}\!\left(\tilde{y}_{i},\,{\bm{h}}^{\top}\Psi_{2,i}^{(h)}\right)+\lambda_{2}^{(h)}\left\|{\bm{h}}-\hat{{\bm{h}}}_{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(60)

When
\ell_{h,2}
is the squared loss, the corresponding proximal closed-form update is

\displaystyle\hat{{\bm{h}}}_{t+1}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(h)};\theta_{2}^{(h)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\Psi_{2}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)})\Psi_{2}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}|\lambda_{2}^{(h)}{\bm{I}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\Psi_{2}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}){\bm{Y}}_{2}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}|\lambda_{2}^{(h)}\hat{{\bm{h}}}_{t}\right),

(61)

where

\Psi_{2}^{(h)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)})=\begin{bmatrix}\Psi_{2,i}^{(h)}\end{bmatrix}_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}},\quad\text{and}\quad{\bm{Y}}_{2}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)})=\begin{bmatrix}\tilde{y}_{i}\end{bmatrix}_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(h)}}.

For non-quadratic choices of
\ell_{h,2}
, we proceed exactly as in Appendix
E.1
: we first update the feature extractors by gradient descent, and then refine
{\bm{h}}
by a finite number of L-BFGS steps while holding the features fixed.

The bi-level optimization above yields an estimator
\hat{h}
of the heterogeneous outcome bridge. To recover the heterogeneous dose-response curve
f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v)
, we still need to integrate out the remaining variables
S
and
W
conditional on
V=v
. Using the identifying formula from Theorem
3.1
and the parameterization in Equation
55
, we obtain

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v)

\displaystyle\approx\mathbb{E}[\hat{h}(a,v,S,W)\mid V=v]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[{\bm{h}}^{\top}\left(\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(a)\otimes\phi_{V,2}^{(h)}(v)\otimes\phi_{S,2}^{(h)}(S)\otimes\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(W)\right)\middle|V=v\right]

\displaystyle={\bm{h}}^{\top}\left(\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(a)\otimes\phi_{V,2}^{(h)}(v)\otimes\mathbb{E}[\phi_{S,2}^{(h)}(S)\otimes\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(W)\mid V=v]\right).

(62)

Thus, once
\hat{h}
has been learned, the heterogeneous curve is determined by the conditional mean embedding of the joint second-stage features of
(S,W)
given
V=v
.

To estimate this quantity, we introduce a third-stage dataset

\mathcal{D}_{3}^{(h)}=\{(v_{i},{\bm{u}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{h,3}},\quad\text{where}\quad{\bm{u}}_{i}=\phi_{S,2}^{(h)}(s_{i})\otimes\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(w_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{S,2}^{(h)}d_{W,2}^{(h)}}.

We then train a regression network
f^{(h)}(\cdot;\theta_{3}^{(h)})
so that

f^{(h)}(v;\theta_{3}^{(h)})\approx\mathbb{E}[{\bm{u}}\mid V=v].

Finally, the heterogeneous dose-response estimator is

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{(h)}(a,v)={\bm{h}}^{\top}\left(\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(a)\otimes\phi_{V,2}^{(h)}(v)\otimes f^{(h)}(v;\theta_{3}^{(h)})\right).

(63)

The heterogeneous OutcomeNet construction can thus be viewed as a structured extension of the population-level outcome-bridge procedure. The first two stages remain unchanged at the methodological level: the first stage learns a neural conditional mean embedding of the outcome proxy, and the second stage estimates the bridge function itself, with the sole modification that the heterogeneity variable
V
is now incorporated explicitly into the feature representations. The additional ingredient specific to the heterogeneous setting is the third-stage regression
f^{(h)}(v;\theta_{3}^{(h)})
, which estimates the conditional mean embedding of the joint
(S,W)
-features given
V=v
. Combining this regression with the learned bridge head yields the estimator in Equation
63
. The resulting procedure is summarized in Algorithm
5
.

Input:
Datasets
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(h)}=\{(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{v}_{i},\bar{s}_{i},\bar{z}_{i},\bar{w}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{h}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(h)}=\{(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{v}_{i},\tilde{s}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i},\tilde{y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m_{h}}
.

Design choice:
Third-stage regression loss
\ell_{h,3}
.

Output:
Heterogeneous outcome-bridge estimator
\hat{f}_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{(h)}(a,v)
.

1:

Train the first two stages exactly as in Algorithm
1
, with the following replacements:

X=(V,S),\hskip 17.00024pt\phi_{AXZ,1}^{(h)}\text{ replaced by }\phi_{AVSZ,1}^{(h)},\hskip 17.00024pt\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}\otimes\phi_{X,2}^{(h)}\text{ replaced by }\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}\otimes\phi_{V,2}^{(h)}\otimes\phi_{S,2}^{(h)}.

2:

Construct the third-stage dataset

\mathcal{D}_{3}^{(h)}=\{(v_{i},\mathbf{c}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{h,3}},\hskip 17.00024pt\mathbf{c}_{i}=\phi_{S,2}^{(h)}(s_{i})\otimes\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(w_{i}).

3:

Train the regression network
f^{(h)}(\cdot;\theta_{3}^{(h)})
on
\mathcal{D}_{3}^{(h)}
to approximate
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{c}\mid V=v]
.

4:

Return

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{(h)}(a,v)={\bm{h}}^{\top}\left(\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(a)\otimes\phi_{V,2}^{(h)}(v)\otimes f^{(h)}(v;\theta_{3}^{(h)})\right).

Algorithm 5

Heterogeneous OutcomeNet

F.2
Heterogeneous dose-response estimation: treatment bridge method

We now extend TreatmentNet to the heterogeneous dose-response function. As in the outcome-bridge subsection, we write
X=(S,V)
, where
V
denotes the effect modifiers of interest and
S
collects the remaining observed covariates. Recall that, according to Theorem
3.2
, the heterogeneous dose-response is identified through the treatment bridge by

f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v)=\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi_{0}(a,v,S,Z)\mid A=a,V=v],

where the corresponding bridge function satisfies

\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,v,S,Z)\mid A=a,V=v,S,W]=\frac{p_{A\mid V}(a\mid v)}{p_{A\mid S,V,W}(a\mid s,v,w)}.

Thus, compared with the population-level treatment-bridge formulation, the only structural change is again the explicit inclusion of
V
in the bridge and in the neural feature maps.

In practice, the conditional density ratio is not known and is replaced by an estimator
\hat{r}(a,v,s,w)\approx p_{A\mid V}(a\mid v)/p_{A\mid S,V,W}(a\mid s,v,w)
; see Appendix
I.1
. Let

\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(\varphi)}=\{(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{v}_{i},\bar{s}_{i},\bar{w}_{i},\bar{z}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\varphi}},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(\varphi)}=\{(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{v}_{i},\tilde{s}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i},\tilde{\hat{r}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m_{\varphi}}

denote the first- and second-stage splits. We parameterize the heterogeneous treatment bridge and the first-stage neural conditional mean embedding as

\displaystyle\varphi(a,v,s,z)

\displaystyle=\bm{\varphi}^{\top}\left(\phi_{AVS,2}^{(\varphi)}(a,v,s)\otimes\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(z)\right),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(Z)\mid A=a,V=v,S=s,W=w]

\displaystyle=\left({\bm{V}}^{(\varphi)}\right)^{\top}\phi_{AVSW,1}^{(\varphi)}(a,v,s,w).

Here
\phi_{AVSW,1}^{(\varphi)}
is the first-stage feature extractor, parameterized by
\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)}
, while
\phi_{AVS,2}^{(\varphi)}
and
\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}
are the second-stage feature extractors, jointly parameterized by
\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)}
.

As in the population-level TreatmentNet, the estimation proceeds by a bi-level multi-stage procedure. The first stage learns a neural conditional mean embedding of the treatment proxy, and the second stage regresses the estimated density-ratio target onto the corresponding learned representation.

For a mini-batch
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)}\subset\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(\varphi)}
, the first-stage proximal loss is

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\varphi,1}^{\mathrm{prox}}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)},{\bm{V}}^{(\varphi)};\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)},\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)}}\left\|\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(\bar{z}_{i})-\left({\bm{V}}^{(\varphi)}\right)^{\top}\phi_{AVSW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{v}_{i},\bar{s}_{i},\bar{w}_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{1}^{(\varphi)}\left\|{\bm{V}}^{(\varphi)}-\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(\varphi)}_{t}\right\|_{F}^{2}.

(64)

As before, the target representation
\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(Z)
depends on the second-stage proxy featurizer, but
\theta_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}
is not updated in this stage. For fixed
\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)}
and
\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)}
, the corresponding proximal first-stage minimizer is

\displaystyle\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(\varphi)}_{t+1}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)};\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)},\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\Phi_{AVSW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)})\Phi_{AVSW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)}|\lambda_{1}^{(\varphi)}{\bm{I}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\Phi_{AVSW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)})\Phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{1}^{(\varphi)}|\lambda_{1}^{(\varphi)}\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(\varphi)}_{t}\right),

(65)

where

\Phi_{AVSW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B})=\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{AVSW,1}^{(\varphi)}(a_{i},v_{i},s_{i},w_{i})\end{bmatrix}_{i\in\mathcal{B}},\quad\text{and}\quad\Phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B})=\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(z_{i})\end{bmatrix}_{i\in\mathcal{B}}.

The second stage again requires an auxiliary on-the-fly first-stage solve on the current second-stage batch. Thus, for
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}\subset\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(\varphi)}
, we define

\displaystyle\check{{\bm{V}}}^{(\varphi)}_{t}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)};\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\Phi_{AVSW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)})\Phi_{AVSW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}|\lambda_{1}^{(\varphi)}{\bm{I}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\Phi_{AVSW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)})\Phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}|\lambda_{1}^{(\varphi)}\hat{{\bm{V}}}^{(\varphi)}_{t}\right).

(66)

As in the population-level case, this auxiliary operator is used only to evaluate the current second-stage loss with the current proxy representation.

Using
\check{{\bm{V}}}^{(\varphi)}_{t}
, the heterogeneous second-stage feature vector is

\Psi_{2,i}^{(\varphi)}=\phi_{AVS,2}^{(\varphi)}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{v}_{i},\tilde{s}_{i})\otimes\check{{\bm{V}}}_{t}^{(\varphi)\top}\phi_{AVSW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{v}_{i},\tilde{s}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i}),\qquad i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}.

The quadratic second-stage proximal loss is therefore

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\varphi,2}^{\mathrm{prox}}\!\left(\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)},\bm{\varphi};\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}}\Big(\tilde{\hat{r}}_{i}-\bm{\varphi}^{\top}\Psi_{2,i}^{(\varphi)}\Big)^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{(\varphi)}\left\|\bm{\varphi}-\hat{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(67)

For fixed features, the corresponding proximal second-stage minimizer is

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\varphi}}_{t+1}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)};\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\Psi_{2}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)})\Psi_{2}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}|\lambda_{2}^{(\varphi)}{\bm{I}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\Psi_{2}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}){\bm{R}}_{2}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}|\lambda_{2}^{(\varphi)}\hat{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}\right),

(68)

where

\Psi_{2}^{(\varphi)}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)})=\begin{bmatrix}\Psi_{2,i}^{(\varphi)}\end{bmatrix}_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}},\quad\text{and}\quad{\bm{R}}_{2}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)})=\begin{bmatrix}\tilde{\hat{r}}_{i}\end{bmatrix}_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}}.

As before, the quadratic loss can be replaced by a general differentiable regression loss
\ell^{(\varphi)}_{2}
, yielding

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\varphi,2}^{\mathrm{gen}}\!\left(\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)},\bm{\varphi};\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi)}}\ell^{(\varphi)}_{2}\!\left(\tilde{\hat{r}}_{i},\,\bm{\varphi}^{\top}\Psi_{2,i}^{(\varphi)}\right)+\lambda_{2}^{(\varphi)}\left\|\bm{\varphi}-\hat{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(69)

This extension is again practically useful, since the conditional density-ratio estimates
\hat{r}
may be noisy or heavy-tailed. In our implementation, we optimize Equation
69
by the same two-step stage-2 strategy used earlier: a gradient update for the feature extractors, followed by
K_{\varphi}
steps of L-BFGS
[
31
,
2
]
for the linear head.

The procedure above yields an estimator
\hat{\varphi}
of the heterogeneous treatment bridge. To recover the heterogeneous dose-response curve, we introduce a third regression stage targeting

f_{\mathrm{CATE}}(a,v)=\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi_{0}(a,v,S,Z)\mid A=a,V=v].

We therefore construct a pseudo-outcome dataset

\mathcal{D}_{3}^{(\varphi)}=\{((a_{i},v_{i}),y_{i}^{\mathrm{pseudo}})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\varphi,3}},\quad\text{where}\quad y_{i}^{\mathrm{pseudo}}=y_{i}\,\hat{\varphi}(a_{i},v_{i},s_{i},z_{i}),

and

\hat{\varphi}(a_{i},v_{i},s_{i},z_{i})=\hat{\bm{\varphi}}^{\top}\left(\phi_{AVS,2}^{(\varphi)}(a_{i},v_{i},s_{i})\otimes\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(z_{i})\right).

We then train a regression network
f^{(\varphi)}(\cdot,\cdot;\theta_{3}^{(\varphi)})
such that

f^{(\varphi)}(a,v;\theta_{3}^{(\varphi)})\approx\mathbb{E}[Y\hat{\varphi}(a,v,S,Z)\mid A=a,V=v].

More generally, this network may be trained with any differentiable regression loss
\ell^{(\varphi)}_{3}
. Writing the corresponding empirical objective as

\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\varphi,3}(\theta_{3}^{(\varphi)})=\frac{1}{n_{\varphi,3}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\varphi,3}}\ell^{(\varphi)}_{3}\!\left(y_{i}^{\mathrm{pseudo}},f^{(\varphi)}(a_{i},v_{i};\theta_{3}^{(\varphi)})\right).

This third-stage regression completes the heterogeneous TreatmentNet construction and summarized in Algorithm
6
.

Input:
Datasets
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(\varphi)}=\{(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{v}_{i},\bar{s}_{i},\bar{w}_{i},\bar{z}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\varphi}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(\varphi)}=\{(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{v}_{i},\tilde{s}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i},\tilde{\hat{r}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m_{\varphi}}
.

Design choices:
Second-stage loss
\ell^{(\varphi)}_{2}
; third-stage loss
\ell^{(\varphi)}_{3}
.

Output:
Heterogeneous treatment-bridge estimator
\hat{f}_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{(\varphi)}(a,v)
.

1:

Train the first two stages exactly as in Algorithm
2
, with the following replacements:

X=(V,S),\hskip 17.00024pt\phi_{AXW,1}^{(\varphi)}\text{ replaced by }\phi_{AVSW,1}^{(\varphi)},\hskip 17.00024pt\phi_{AX,2}^{(\varphi)}\text{ replaced by }\phi_{AVS,2}^{(\varphi)}.

2:

Construct the third-stage pseudo-outcome dataset

\mathcal{D}_{3}^{(\varphi)}=\{((a_{i},v_{i}),y_{i}^{\mathrm{pseudo}})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\varphi,3}},\hskip 17.00024pty_{i}^{\mathrm{pseudo}}=y_{i}\,\hat{\varphi}(a_{i},v_{i},s_{i},z_{i}).

3:

Train the regression network
f^{(\varphi)}(\cdot,\cdot;\theta_{3}^{(\varphi)})
on
\mathcal{D}_{3}^{(\varphi)}
to approximate
\mathbb{E}[Y\hat{\varphi}(a,v,S,Z)\mid A=a,V=v]
.

4:

Return
\hat{f}_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{(\varphi)}(a,v)=f^{(\varphi)}(a,v;\theta_{3}^{(\varphi)})
.

Algorithm 6

Heterogeneous TreatmentNet

F.3
Heterogeneous dose-response estimation: doubly robust method

Having obtained heterogeneous bridge estimators from Appendices
F.1
and
F.2
, we now combine them into a doubly robust estimator of the heterogeneous dose-response curve. Recall the identifying representation

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR)}}(a,v;h_{0},\varphi_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,v,S,Z)\{Y-h_{0}(a,v,S,W)\}\mid A=a,V=v]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,v,S,W)\mid V=v].

Given estimators
\hat{h}
and
\hat{\varphi}
, the remaining task is therefore to estimate the conditional expectation terms appearing in this expression over the joint space
(A,V)
. As in the population-level case, we describe two implementations of this final stage.
Let

\mathcal{D}^{(\kappa)}=\{(a_{i},v_{i},s_{i},w_{i},z_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}}

be a sample on which both
\hat{h}
and
\hat{\varphi}
can be evaluated. In our implementation, we reuse the second-stage split for this purpose, although a separate split may also be used.

F.3.0.0.1
Version 1: direct residual regression.

The most direct implementation is to regress the bridge-weighted residual

\hat{\varphi}(a,v,S,Z)\{Y-\hat{h}(a,v,S,W)\}

on the pair
(A,V)
. We therefore construct the pseudo-outcome dataset

\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(\kappa)}=\{((a_{i},v_{i}),y_{i}^{(\kappa,1)})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}},\qquad y_{i}^{(\kappa,1)}=\hat{\varphi}(a_{i},v_{i},s_{i},z_{i})\bigl(y_{i}-\hat{h}(a_{i},v_{i},s_{i},w_{i})\bigr).

We then fit a regression network
k^{(\kappa,1)}(\cdot,\cdot;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa)})
to approximate

k^{(\kappa,1)}(a,v;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa)})\approx\mathbb{E}[\hat{\varphi}(a,v,S,Z)\{Y-\hat{h}(a,v,S,W)\}\mid A=a,V=v].

More generally, this network may be trained with any differentiable regression loss. Writing
\ell^{(\kappa)}_{1}
for this loss, the third-stage objective is

\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\kappa,1}(\theta_{1}^{(\kappa)})=\frac{1}{n_{\kappa}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}}\ell^{(\kappa)}_{1}\!\left(y_{i}^{(\kappa,1)},k^{(\kappa,1)}(a_{i},v_{i};\theta_{1}^{(\kappa)})\right).

(70)

Combining this residual regression with the heterogeneous OutcomeNet estimator gives

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR1)}}(a,v)

\displaystyle=\hat{f}_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{(h)}(a,v)+k^{(\kappa,1)}(a,v;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa)})

\displaystyle={\bm{h}}^{\top}\left(\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(a)\otimes\phi_{V,2}^{(h)}(v)\otimes f^{(h)}(v;\theta_{3}^{(h)})\right)+k^{(\kappa,1)}(a,v;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa)}).

(71)

The complete procedure is summarized in Algorithm
7
.

F.3.0.0.2
Version 2: decoupled decomposition.

Alternatively, we may expand the doubly robust formula as

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR)}}(a,v;h_{0},\varphi_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,v,S,W)\mid V=v]+\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi_{0}(a,v,S,Z)\mid A=a,V=v]

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,v,S,Z)h_{0}(a,v,S,W)\mid A=a,V=v].

The first two terms are already estimated by the heterogeneous OutcomeNet and TreatmentNet procedures. It therefore remains only to estimate the interaction term

\mathbb{E}[\hat{\varphi}(a,v,S,Z)\hat{h}(a,v,S,W)\mid A=a,V=v].

For this purpose, we construct the pseudo-outcome dataset

\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(\kappa)}=\{((a_{i},v_{i}),y_{i}^{(\kappa,2)})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}},\quad\text{where}\quad y_{i}^{(\kappa,2)}=\hat{\varphi}(a_{i},v_{i},s_{i},z_{i})\hat{h}(a_{i},v_{i},s_{i},w_{i}).

We then fit a correction network
k^{(\kappa,2)}(\cdot,\cdot;\theta_{2}^{(\kappa)})
such that

k^{(\kappa,2)}(a,v;\theta_{2}^{(\kappa)})\approx\mathbb{E}[\hat{\varphi}(a,v,S,Z)\hat{h}(a,v,S,W)\mid A=a,V=v].

As above, this network may be trained with any differentiable regression loss. Denoting the loss by
\ell^{(\kappa)}_{2}
, we minimize

\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\kappa,2}(\theta_{2}^{(\kappa)})=\frac{1}{n_{\kappa}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}}\ell^{(\kappa)}_{2}\!\left(y_{i}^{(\kappa,2)},k^{(\kappa,2)}(a_{i},v_{i};\theta_{2}^{(\kappa)})\right).

(72)

The corresponding decoupled heterogeneous doubly robust estimator is

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR2)}}(a,v)=\hat{f}_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{(h)}(a,v)+\hat{f}_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{(\varphi)}(a,v)-k^{(\kappa,2)}(a,v;\theta_{2}^{(\kappa)})

\displaystyle={\bm{h}}^{\top}\left(\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(a)\otimes\phi_{V,2}^{(h)}(v)\otimes f^{(h)}(v;\theta_{3}^{(h)})\right)+f^{(\varphi)}(a,v;\theta_{3}^{(\varphi)})-k^{(\kappa,2)}(a,v;\theta_{2}^{(\kappa)}).

(73)

In comparison, Version 1 directly regresses the full bridge-weighted residual. Version 2 instead reuses the heterogeneous treatment-bridge regression and only learns the interaction term. Both constructions are compatible with the same learned bridge functions and differ only in how the final correction is parameterized. Both procedures for DRPCLNET (V1) and (V2) are summarized in Algorithms
7
and
8
.

Input:
A dataset
\mathcal{D}^{(\kappa)}=\{(a_{i},v_{i},s_{i},w_{i},z_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}}
on which both
\hat{h}
and
\hat{\varphi}
can be evaluated.

Subroutines:
Algorithms
5
and
6
.

Design choice:
Third-stage regression loss
\ell^{(\kappa)}_{1}
.

Hyperparameters:
Hyperparameters required by Algorithms
5
and
6
, together with the optimization hyperparameters for the correction network
k^{(\kappa,1)}(\cdot,\cdot;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa)})
.

Output:
Heterogeneous doubly robust estimator
\hat{f}_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR1)}}(a,v)
.

1:

Train the heterogeneous OutcomeNet via Algorithm
5
to obtain
\hat{h}(a,v,s,w)
and
\hat{f}_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{(h)}(a,v)
.

2:

Train the heterogeneous TreatmentNet via Algorithm
6
to obtain
\hat{\varphi}(a,v,s,z)
.

3:

Construct the pseudo-outcome dataset

\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(\kappa)}=\{((a_{i},v_{i}),y_{i}^{(\kappa,1)})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}},\hskip 17.00024pty_{i}^{(\kappa,1)}=\hat{\varphi}(a_{i},v_{i},s_{i},z_{i})\bigl(y_{i}-\hat{h}(a_{i},v_{i},s_{i},w_{i})\bigr).

4:

Train the correction network
k^{(\kappa,1)}(\cdot,\cdot;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa)})
on
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(\kappa)}
using the loss in Equation
70
.

5:

Define

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR1)}}(a,v)=\hat{f}_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{(h)}(a,v)+k^{(\kappa,1)}(a,v;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa)}).

6:

Return

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{\mathrm{(DR1)}}(a,v)
.

and

and

and

.

to obtain

and
\hat{f}_{\mathrm{CATE}}^{(h)}(a,v)
