Algorithm 8

Heterogeneous DRPCLNET, Version 2

Appendix G
Neural mean embedding-based proxy causal learning for conditional dose-response

We now extend the neural mean embedding framework to the conditional dose-response function
f_{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime})
. As in the previous sections, we proceed by developing corresponding outcome- and treatment-bridge estimators and then combining them into a doubly robust construction. We begin with the outcome-bridge formulation. In this case, the bridge-learning problem itself is unchanged relative to the population-level dose-response setting; the only modification appears in the final integration step, which now conditions on the observed treatment level
A=a^{\prime}
.

G.1
Conditional dose-response estimation: outcome bridge method

Recall that, according to Theorem
3.1
, the conditional dose-response is identified by

f_{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)\mid A=a^{\prime}].

The bridge equation remains exactly the same as in the population-level outcome-bridge formulation.
Therefore, the first two stages of the estimator are identical to those of OutcomeNet in Appendix
E.1
. In particular, we reuse the same neural parameterization of the bridge function
h(a,x,w)
and the same two-stage training procedure to obtain an estimator
\hat{h}
.

The only new ingredient is the final conditional integration step. Using Equation
28
, we have

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime})

\displaystyle\approx\mathbb{E}[\hat{h}(a,X,W)\mid A=a^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[{\bm{h}}^{\top}\left(\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(a)\otimes\phi_{X,2}^{(h)}(X)\otimes\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(W)\right)\middle|A=a^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle={\bm{h}}^{\top}\left(\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(a)\otimes\mathbb{E}[\phi_{X,2}^{(h)}(X)\otimes\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(W)\mid A=a^{\prime}]\right).

(74)

Thus, once
\hat{h}
has been learned, estimating
f_{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime})
reduces to estimating the conditional mean embedding of the joint second-stage features of
(X,W)
given
A=a^{\prime}
.

To estimate this quantity, we introduce a third-stage dataset

\mathcal{D}_{3}^{(h,\mathrm{ATT})}=\{(a_{i},\mathbf{c}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{h,3}},\quad\text{where}\quad\mathbf{c}_{i}=\phi_{X,2}^{(h)}(x_{i})\otimes\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(w_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{X,2}^{(h)}d_{W,2}^{(h)}}.

We then train a regression network
g^{(h)}(\cdot;\theta_{3}^{(h)})
so that

g^{(h)}(a^{\prime};\theta_{3}^{(h)})\approx\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{c}\mid A=a^{\prime}].

More generally, this network may be trained with any differentiable regression loss
\ell_{h,3}^{\mathrm{ATT}}
. Denoting the corresponding empirical objective by

\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{h,3}^{\mathrm{ATT}}(\theta_{3}^{(h)})=\frac{1}{n_{h,3}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{h,3}}\ell_{h,3}^{\mathrm{ATT}}\!\left(\mathbf{c}_{i},\,g^{(h)}(a_{i};\theta_{3}^{(h)})\right),

(75)

we use the squared loss in our implementation.

Finally, the conditional dose-response estimator is

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{(h)}(a,a^{\prime})={\bm{h}}^{\top}\left(\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(a)\otimes g^{(h)}(a^{\prime};\theta_{3}^{(h)})\right).

(76)

In our implementation, the third-stage dataset is constructed from the second-stage split, although one may alternatively use a separate third-stage split.

The resulting procedure is summarized in Algorithm
9
.

Input:
Datasets
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(h)}=\{(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{x}_{i},\bar{z}_{i},\bar{w}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{h}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(h)}=\{(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i},\tilde{y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m_{h}}
.

Design choice:
Third-stage regression loss
\ell_{h,3}^{\mathrm{ATT}}
.

Output:
Conditional outcome-bridge estimator
\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{(h)}(a,a^{\prime})
.

1:

Train the first two stages exactly as in Algorithm
1
.

2:

Construct the third-stage dataset

\mathcal{D}_{3}^{(h,\mathrm{ATT})}=\{(a_{i},\mathbf{c}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{h,3}},\hskip 17.00024pt\mathbf{c}_{i}=\phi_{X,2}^{(h)}(x_{i})\otimes\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(w_{i}).

3:

Train the regression network
g^{(h)}(\cdot;\theta_{3}^{(h)})
on
\mathcal{D}_{3}^{(h,\mathrm{ATT})}
to approximate
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{c}\mid A=a^{\prime}]
.

4:

Return

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{(h)}(a,a^{\prime})={\bm{h}}^{\top}\left(\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(a)\otimes g^{(h)}(a^{\prime};\theta_{3}^{(h)})\right).

Algorithm 9

Conditional OutcomeNet

G.2
Conditional dose-response estimation: treatment bridge method

We now extend TreatmentNet to the conditional dose-response function
f_{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime})
. For a fixed reference treatment level
a^{\prime}
, Theorem
3.2
gives the identifying representation

f_{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi_{0}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)\mid A=a],

where the corresponding treatment bridge satisfies

\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)\mid A=a,X,W]=\frac{p_{X,W\mid A}(X,W\mid a^{\prime})}{p_{X,W\mid A}(X,W\mid a)}.

Thus, in contrast to the population-level treatment-bridge setting, the bridge now depends on the additional reference level
a^{\prime}
. In the implementation considered here, we treat
a^{\prime}
as fixed and learn a separate treatment bridge for each reference level of interest.

An important simplification is that the first-stage conditional mean embedding of the treatment proxy does not depend on
a^{\prime}
. Indeed, the regression

\mathbb{E}[\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(Z)\mid A=a,X=x,W=w]=\left({\bm{V}}^{(\varphi)}\right)^{\top}\phi_{AXW,1}^{(\varphi)}(a,x,w)

is identical to the one used in Appendix
E.2
. Therefore, the first stage may be trained exactly as in the population-level TreatmentNet and, in principle, shared across multiple values of
a^{\prime}
. The dependence on
a^{\prime}
enters only through the second-stage target.

Let
\hat{r}^{(a^{\prime})}(a,x,w)
denote an estimator of the density ratio

\hat{r}^{(a^{\prime})}(a,x,w)\approx\frac{p_{X,W\mid A}(x,w\mid a^{\prime})}{p_{X,W\mid A}(x,w\mid a)},

and let

\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(\varphi)}=\{(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{x}_{i},\bar{w}_{i},\bar{z}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\varphi}},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}=\{(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i},\tilde{\hat{r}}_{i}^{(a^{\prime})})\}_{i=1}^{m_{\varphi}}

denote the first- and second-stage data splits. For fixed
a^{\prime}
, we parameterize the conditional treatment bridge as

\displaystyle\varphi_{a^{\prime}}(a,x,z)

\displaystyle=\bm{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})\top}\left(\phi_{AX,2}^{(\varphi)}(a,x)\otimes\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(z)\right),

(77)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(Z)\mid A=a,X=x,W=w]

\displaystyle=\left({\bm{V}}^{(\varphi)}\right)^{\top}\phi_{AXW,1}^{(\varphi)}(a,x,w).

(78)

Since the first stage is unchanged, the persistent and auxiliary first-stage operators are exactly those defined in Appendix
E.2
. In particular, for the current second-stage batch
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}\subset\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}
, we compute the auxiliary on-the-fly operator
\check{{\bm{V}}}_{t}^{(\varphi)}
exactly as in Equation
46
. Using this auxiliary operator, the conditional second-stage feature vector is

\Psi_{2,i}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}=\phi_{AX,2}^{(\varphi)}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i})\otimes\check{{\bm{V}}}_{t}^{(\varphi)\top}\phi_{AXW,1}^{(\varphi)}(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i}),\qquad i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}.

The corresponding quadratic second-stage proximal loss is

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\varphi,2}^{\mathrm{prox}}\!\left(\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)},\bm{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})};\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}}\Big(\tilde{\hat{r}}_{i}^{(a^{\prime})}-\bm{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})\top}\Psi_{2,i}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{2}^{(\varphi)}\left\|\bm{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})}-\hat{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}^{(a^{\prime})}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

For fixed features, the proximal closed-form update is

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\varphi}}_{t+1}^{(a^{\prime})}\!\left(\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)};\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\Psi_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})})\Psi_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}|\lambda_{2}^{(\varphi)}{\bm{I}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\Psi_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}){\bm{R}}_{2}^{(a^{\prime})}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})})^{\top}+|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}|\lambda_{2}^{(\varphi)}\hat{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}^{(a^{\prime})}\right),

where

\Psi_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})})=\begin{bmatrix}\Psi_{2,i}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}\end{bmatrix}_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}},\qquad{\bm{R}}_{2}^{(a^{\prime})}(\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})})=\begin{bmatrix}\tilde{\hat{r}}_{i}^{(a^{\prime})}\end{bmatrix}_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}}.

As in the population-level treatment-bridge estimator, we may replace the squared loss by a general differentiable regression loss
\ell^{(\varphi,\mathrm{ATT})}_{2}
, leading to

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\varphi,2}^{\mathrm{gen}}\!\left(\theta_{2}^{(\varphi)},\bm{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})};\theta_{1}^{(\varphi)},\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}}\ell^{(\varphi,\mathrm{ATT})}_{2}\!\left(\tilde{\hat{r}}_{i}^{(a^{\prime})},\,\bm{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})\top}\Psi_{2,i}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}\right)

\displaystyle+\lambda_{2}^{(\varphi)}\left\|\bm{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})}-\hat{\bm{\varphi}}_{t}^{(a^{\prime})}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

In practice, we optimize this objective exactly as in Appendix
E.2
: we first update the second-stage feature extractors by gradient descent, and then refine the linear head by
K_{\varphi}
steps of L-BFGS.

Once
\hat{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})}
has been learned, the remaining task is to estimate the conditional dose-response curve

f_{\mathrm{ATT}}(a,a^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi_{0}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)\mid A=a].

For fixed
a^{\prime}
, this is again a regression function in the treatment level
a
. We therefore introduce a third-stage pseudo-outcome dataset

\mathcal{D}_{3}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}=\{(a_{i},y_{i}^{\mathrm{pseudo},(a^{\prime})})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\varphi,3}},\quad\text{where}\quad y_{i}^{\mathrm{pseudo},(a^{\prime})}=y_{i}\,\hat{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})}(a_{i},x_{i},z_{i}),

where

\hat{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})}(a_{i},x_{i},z_{i})=\hat{\bm{\varphi}}^{(a^{\prime})\top}\left(\phi_{AX,2}^{(\varphi)}(a_{i},x_{i})\otimes\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(z_{i})\right).

We then train a regression network
f^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}(\cdot;\theta_{3}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})})
such that

f^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}(a;\theta_{3}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})})\approx\mathbb{E}[Y\hat{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a].

More generally, this network may be trained with any differentiable regression loss
\ell^{(\varphi,\mathrm{ATT})}_{3}
. Writing the corresponding empirical objective as

\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\varphi,3}^{\mathrm{ATT}}(\theta_{3}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})})=\frac{1}{n_{\varphi,3}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\varphi,3}}\ell^{(\varphi,\mathrm{ATT})}_{3}\!\left(y_{i}^{\mathrm{pseudo},(a^{\prime})},f^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}(a_{i};\theta_{3}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})})\right),

(79)

we use the squared loss in our implementation unless otherwise stated. The resulting conditional treatment-bridge estimator is

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{(\varphi)}(a,a^{\prime})=f^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}(a;\theta_{3}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}).

(80)

In our implementation, the third-stage dataset is constructed from the second-stage split, although one may alternatively use a separate third-stage split.

Thus, for each fixed reference treatment
a^{\prime}
, the conditional TreatmentNet differs from the population-level TreatmentNet only through the second-stage density-ratio target and the resulting anchor-specific third-stage regression. The first-stage conditional mean embedding of the treatment proxy is unchanged and may be shared across anchors.

The procedure is summarized in Algorithm
10
.

Input:
Datasets
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(\varphi)}=\{(\bar{a}_{i},\bar{x}_{i},\bar{w}_{i},\bar{z}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\varphi}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}=\{(\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{x}_{i},\tilde{w}_{i},\tilde{z}_{i},\tilde{\hat{r}}_{i}^{(a^{\prime})})\}_{i=1}^{m_{\varphi}}
for a fixed reference treatment
a^{\prime}
.

Design choices:
Second-stage regression loss
\ell^{(\varphi,\mathrm{ATT})}_{2}
; third-stage regression loss
\ell^{(\varphi,\mathrm{ATT})}_{3}
.

Output:
Conditional treatment-bridge estimator
\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{(\varphi)}(a,a^{\prime})
.

1:

Train the first stage exactly as in Algorithm
2
.

2:

Train the second stage exactly as in Algorithm
2
, replacing the stage-2 regression target
\hat{r}(a,x,w)
by the anchor-specific target
\hat{r}^{(a^{\prime})}(a,x,w)
.

3:

Construct the third-stage pseudo-outcome dataset

\mathcal{D}_{3}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}=\{(a_{i},y_{i}^{\mathrm{pseudo},(a^{\prime})})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\varphi,3}},\hskip 17.00024pty_{i}^{\mathrm{pseudo},(a^{\prime})}=y_{i}\,\hat{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})}(a_{i},x_{i},z_{i}).

4:

Train the regression network
f^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}(\cdot;\theta_{3}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})})
on
\mathcal{D}_{3}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}
to approximate
\mathbb{E}[Y\hat{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a]
.

5:

Return

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{(\varphi)}(a,a^{\prime})=f^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}(a;\theta_{3}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}).

Algorithm 10

Conditional TreatmentNet

G.3
Conditional dose-response estimation: doubly robust method

We now combine the conditional outcome- and treatment-bridge estimators into a doubly robust estimator of the conditional dose-response function. Recall that, for a fixed reference treatment level
a^{\prime}
, the identifying representation is

f_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{\mathrm{(DR)}}(a,a^{\prime};h_{0},\varphi_{0})=\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)\{Y-h_{0}(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a]+\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)\mid A=a^{\prime}].

Thus, given estimators
\hat{h}
and
\hat{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})}
, the remaining task is to estimate the conditional expectation terms appearing in this expression as functions of the intervention level
a
. As in the population-level and heterogeneous settings, we describe two implementations of this final stage. Let

\mathcal{D}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})}=\{(a_{i},x_{i},w_{i},z_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}}

denote a sample on which both
\hat{h}
and
\hat{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})}
can be evaluated. In our implementation, we reuse the second-stage split for this purpose, although one may alternatively use a separate split.

G.3.0.0.1
Version 1: direct residual regression.

For fixed
a^{\prime}
, the most direct implementation is to regress the bridge-weighted residual

\hat{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})}(a,X,Z)\{Y-\hat{h}(a,X,W)\}

on the treatment level
A
. We therefore construct the pseudo-outcome dataset

\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})}=\{(a_{i},y_{i}^{(\kappa,1,a^{\prime})})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}},\quad\text{where}\quad y_{i}^{(\kappa,1,a^{\prime})}=\hat{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})}(a_{i},x_{i},z_{i})\bigl(y_{i}-\hat{h}(a_{i},x_{i},w_{i})\bigr).

We then fit a regression network
k^{(\kappa,1,a^{\prime})}(\cdot;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})})
to approximate

k^{(\kappa,1,a^{\prime})}(a;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})})\approx\mathbb{E}[\hat{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})}(a,X,Z)\{Y-\hat{h}(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a].

More generally, this network may be trained with any differentiable regression loss. Writing
\ell^{(\kappa,\mathrm{ATT})}_{1}
for this loss, the third-stage objective is

\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\kappa,1}^{\mathrm{ATT}}(\theta_{1}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})})=\frac{1}{n_{\kappa}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}}\ell^{(\kappa,\mathrm{ATT})}_{1}\!\left(y_{i}^{(\kappa,1,a^{\prime})},k^{(\kappa,1,a^{\prime})}(a_{i};\theta_{1}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})})\right).

(81)

Combining this residual regression with the conditional OutcomeNet estimator yields

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{\mathrm{(DR1)}}(a,a^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{(h)}(a,a^{\prime})+k^{(\kappa,1,a^{\prime})}(a;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})})

\displaystyle=\hat{{\bm{h}}}^{\top}\left(\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(a)\otimes g^{(h)}(a^{\prime};\theta_{3}^{(h)})\right)+k^{(\kappa,1,a^{\prime})}(a;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})}).

(82)

This is the most direct neural implementation of the conditional doubly robust formula, since the additional regression network learns the full bridge-weighted residual correction in one step.

G.3.0.0.2
Version 2: decoupled decomposition.

Alternatively, for fixed
a^{\prime}
, we may expand the doubly robust formula as

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{\mathrm{(DR)}}(a,a^{\prime};h_{0},\varphi_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)\mid A=a^{\prime}]+\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi_{0}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)\mid A=a]

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,a^{\prime},X,Z)h_{0}(a,X,W)\mid A=a].

The first two terms are already estimated by the conditional OutcomeNet and conditional TreatmentNet procedures. It therefore remains only to estimate the interaction term

\mathbb{E}[\hat{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})}(a,X,Z)\hat{h}(a,X,W)\mid A=a].

To this end, we construct the pseudo-outcome dataset

\mathcal{D}_{2}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})}=\{(a_{i},y_{i}^{(\kappa,2,a^{\prime})})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}},\quad\text{where}\quad y_{i}^{(\kappa,2,a^{\prime})}=\hat{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})}(a_{i},x_{i},z_{i})\hat{h}(a_{i},x_{i},w_{i}).

We then fit a correction network
k^{(\kappa,2,a^{\prime})}(\cdot;\theta_{2}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})})
such that

k^{(\kappa,2,a^{\prime})}(a;\theta_{2}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})})\approx\mathbb{E}[\hat{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})}(a,X,Z)\hat{h}(a,X,W)\mid A=a].

As above, this network may be trained with any differentiable regression loss. Denoting this loss by
\ell^{(\kappa,\mathrm{ATT})}_{2}
, we minimize

\hat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\kappa,2}^{\mathrm{ATT}}(\theta_{2}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})})=\frac{1}{n_{\kappa}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}}\ell^{(\kappa,\mathrm{ATT})}_{2}\!\left(y_{i}^{(\kappa,2,a^{\prime})},k^{(\kappa,2,a^{\prime})}(a_{i};\theta_{2}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})})\right).

(83)

The resulting decoupled conditional doubly robust estimator is

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{\mathrm{(DR2)}}(a,a^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{(h)}(a,a^{\prime})+\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{(\varphi)}(a,a^{\prime})-k^{(\kappa,2,a^{\prime})}(a;\theta_{2}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})})

\displaystyle=\hat{{\bm{h}}}^{\top}\left(\phi_{A,2}^{(h)}(a)\otimes g^{(h)}(a^{\prime};\theta_{3}^{(h)})\right)+f^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})}(a;\theta_{3}^{(\varphi,a^{\prime})})-k^{(\kappa,2,a^{\prime})}(a;\theta_{2}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})}).

(84)

The procedures are summarized in Algorithms
11
and
12
.

Input:
A dataset
\mathcal{D}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})}=\{(a_{i},x_{i},w_{i},z_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}}
on which both
\hat{h}
and
\hat{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})}
can be evaluated, together with a fixed reference treatment level
a^{\prime}
.

Subroutines:
Algorithms
9
and
10
.

Design choice:
Third-stage regression loss
\ell^{(\kappa,\mathrm{ATT})}_{1}
.

Hyperparameters:
Hyperparameters required by Algorithms
9
and
10
, together with the optimization hyperparameters for the correction network
k^{(\kappa,1,a^{\prime})}(\cdot;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})})
.

Output:
Conditional doubly robust estimator
\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{\mathrm{(DR1)}}(a,a^{\prime})
.

1:

Train the conditional OutcomeNet via Algorithm
9
to obtain
\hat{h}(a,x,w)
and
\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{(h)}(a,a^{\prime})
.

2:

Train the conditional TreatmentNet via Algorithm
10
to obtain
\hat{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})}(a,x,z)
.

3:

Construct the pseudo-outcome dataset

\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})}=\{(a_{i},y_{i}^{(\kappa,1,a^{\prime})})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\kappa}},\hskip 17.00024pty_{i}^{(\kappa,1,a^{\prime})}=\hat{\varphi}^{(a^{\prime})}(a_{i},x_{i},z_{i})\bigl(y_{i}-\hat{h}(a_{i},x_{i},w_{i})\bigr).

4:

Train the correction network
k^{(\kappa,1,a^{\prime})}(\cdot;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})})
on
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})}
using the loss in Equation
81
.

5:

Define

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{\mathrm{(DR1)}}(a,a^{\prime})=\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{(h)}(a,a^{\prime})+k^{(\kappa,1,a^{\prime})}(a;\theta_{1}^{(\kappa,a^{\prime})}).

6:

Return

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATT}}^{\mathrm{(DR1)}}(a,a^{\prime})
.

Algorithm 12

Conditional DRPCLNET, Version 2

Appendix H
Consistency of the proposed algorithms

In this section, we prove the consistency results of our proposed algorithms. For simplicity of the analysis, we assume independent splits of the dataset for each regression stage.

H.1
Outcome bridge consistency for dose-response estimation

We derive weak norm convergence rates for the outcome bridge in this subsection. Our proof relies on uniform deviation inequalities and controlling the Rademacher complexities of relevant function classes.

Let

Q:=(A,X,Z),\qquad R:=(A,X),

and write

m_{0}(q):=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid Q=q].

For a measurable function
u(a,x,w)
, define the outcome-bridge conditional
expectation operator

(T_{h}u)(a,x,z):=\mathbb{E}[u(a,x,W)\mid A=a,X=x,Z=z].

The induced projected seminorm/weak-norm is

\|u\|_{T_{h}}:=\|T_{h}u\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}.

Let the first-stage sample be

D_{1}^{(h)}=\{(\bar{q}_{i},\bar{w}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{h}},

and the second-stage sample be

D_{2}^{(h)}=\{(\tilde{y}_{i},\tilde{q}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m_{h}}.

We assume
D_{1}^{(h)}
is an i.i.d. sample from
{\mathbb{P}}_{Q,W}
,

D_{2}^{(h)}
is an i.i.d. sample from
{\mathbb{P}}_{Y,Q}
, and the two samples are independent.

We consider a learned-feature bridge class. Let
\nu_{h}=(n_{h},m_{h})
. Let

\Phi_{\nu_{h}}\subset\{\phi:\mathcal{W}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\nu_{h}}}\},

be a class of learned outcome-proxy feature maps,

\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{h}}\subset\{g:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\nu_{h}}}\},

be a class of first-stage conditional-mean regressors, and

\Theta_{\nu_{h}}\subset\{\theta:\mathcal{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\nu_{h}}}\}

be a class of second-stage heads. The bridge class is

\mathcal{H}_{\nu_{h}}:=\left\{h_{\theta,\phi}(a,x,w)=\langle\theta(a,x),\phi(w)\rangle:\theta\in\Theta_{\nu_{h}},\ \phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{h}}\right\}.

The usual tensor-product architecture is subsumed in this notation. Indeed, the
final linear layer in

\langle h,\phi_{AX,2}^{(h)}(a,x)\otimes\phi_{W,2}^{(h)}(w)\rangle_{F}=\phi_{AX,2}^{(h)}(a,x)^{\top}h\varphi_{W,2}^{(h)}(w)

is absorbed into the map

\theta(a,x)=h^{\top}\varphi_{AX,2}^{(h)}(a,x).

Similarly, the first-stage linear layer is absorbed into the class

\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{h}}
.

For every
\phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{h}}
, define the population conditional feature mean

\mu_{\phi}(q):=\mathbb{E}[\phi(W)\mid Q=q].

Then

(T_{h}h_{\theta,\phi})(q)=\langle\theta(r),\mu_{\phi}(q)\rangle.

For each candidate feature map
\phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{h}}
, define the first-stage
empirical risk

\widehat{R}_{h,1}(\phi,g):=\frac{1}{n_{h}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{h}}\|\phi(\bar{w}_{i})-g(\bar{q}_{i})\|_{2}^{2},\qquad g\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{h}}.

The profiled first-stage estimator is

\hat{g}_{\phi}\in\argmin_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{h}}}\widehat{R}_{h,1}(\phi,g).

Thus the first stage is defined for every candidate learned feature map

\phi
, not only for a fixed oracle feature.

The second-stage profiled ERM is

(\hat{\theta},\hat{\phi})\in\argmin_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in\Theta_{\nu_{h}}\\
\phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{h}}\end{subarray}}\widehat{R}_{h,2}(\theta,\phi),

where

\widehat{R}_{h,2}(\theta,\phi):=\frac{1}{m_{h}}\sum_{i=1}^{m_{h}}\left\{\tilde{y}_{i}-\langle\theta(\tilde{r}_{i}),\hat{g}_{\phi}(\tilde{q}_{i})\rangle\right\}^{2}.

The learned outcome bridge is

\hat{h}(a,x,w):=\langle\hat{\theta}(a,x),\hat{\phi}(w)\rangle.

H.1.0.0.1
Boundedness assumptions.

Assume there exist finite constants
B_{Y},B_{\phi},B_{g},B_{\theta}
such that

|Y|\leq B_{Y}\quad\text{a.s.},

and

\sup_{\phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{h}}}\sup_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\|\phi(w)\|_{2}\leq B_{\phi},

\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{h}}}\sup_{q\in\mathcal{Q}}\|g(q)\|_{2}\leq B_{g},

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\nu_{h}}}\sup_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\|\theta(r)\|_{2}\leq B_{\theta}.

Define

M_{1}:=(B_{\phi}+B_{g})^{2},\qquad M_{2}:=(B_{Y}+B_{\theta}B_{g})^{2}.

H.1.0.0.2
Approximation errors.

Define the first-stage conditional-mean approximation error

\kappa_{1,\nu_{h}}:=\sup_{\phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{h}}}\inf_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{h}}}\|g-\mu_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}.

Define the projected second-stage approximation error

\kappa_{2,\nu_{h}}:=\inf_{\theta\in\Theta_{\nu_{h}},\ \phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{h}}}\left\|m_{0}-\langle\theta,\mu_{\phi}\rangle\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2},

where

\langle\theta,\mu_{\phi}\rangle(q):=\langle\theta(r),\mu_{\phi}(q)\rangle.

H.1.0.0.3
Loss classes.

Let

\mathcal{L}_{1,\nu_{h}}:=\left\{(q,w)\mapsto\|\phi(w)-g(q)\|_{2}^{2}:\phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{h}},\ g\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{h}}\right\},

and

\mathcal{L}_{2,\nu_{h}}:=\left\{(y,q)\mapsto\left(y-\langle\theta(r),g(q)\rangle\right)^{2}:\theta\in\Theta_{\nu_{h}},\ g\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{h}}\right\}.

For a sample
S=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{N})
, define the empirical Rademacher
complexity

\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{S}(\mathcal{F}):=\mathbb{E}_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{i}f(s_{i})\right],

where
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{N}
are independent Rademacher random variables.

We will use the following standard uniform deviation bound: if

\mathcal{F}
is a class of functions taking values in
[0,M]
, then with
probability at least
1-\delta
,

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|{\mathbb{P}}f-{\mathbb{P}}_{N}f\right|\leq 2\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{S}(\mathcal{F})+3M\sqrt{\frac{\log(2/\delta)}{2N}}.

Lemma H.1

(Uniform first-stage error)
.

Define

\epsilon_{1,\nu_{h}}:=\sup_{\phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{h}}}\|\hat{g}_{\phi}-\mu_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}.

Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\epsilon_{1,\nu_{h}}\leq\kappa_{1,\nu_{h}}+2\Delta_{1,\nu_{h}}(\delta),

where

\Delta_{1,\nu_{h}}(\delta):=2\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{D_{1}^{(h)}}(\mathcal{L}_{1,\nu_{h}})+3M_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log(2/\delta)}{2n_{h}}}.

Proof.

For fixed
\phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{h}}
, define the population first-stage risk

R_{h,1}(\phi,g):=\mathbb{E}\left[\|\phi(W)-g(Q)\|_{2}^{2}\right].

Since

\mu_{\phi}(Q)=\mathbb{E}[\phi(W)\mid Q],

the usual least-squares projection identity gives, for every measurable

g:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\nu_{h}}}
,

R_{h,1}(\phi,g)-R_{h,1}(\phi,\mu_{\phi})=\|g-\mu_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}.

Indeed,

\displaystyle R_{h,1}(\phi,g)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\|\phi(W)-\mu_{\phi}(Q)+\mu_{\phi}(Q)-g(Q)\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=R_{h,1}(\phi,\mu_{\phi})+\|g-\mu_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}

\displaystyle+2\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\phi(W)-\mu_{\phi}(Q),\mu_{\phi}(Q)-g(Q)\right\rangle\right],

and the cross term is zero by conditional expectation.

Let

\Delta_{1,\nu_{h}}:=\sup_{\phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{h}},\,g\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{h}}}\left|R_{h,1}(\phi,g)-\widehat{R}_{h,1}(\phi,g)\right|.

By empirical risk minimization,

\widehat{R}_{h,1}(\phi,\hat{g}_{\phi})\leq\widehat{R}_{h,1}(\phi,g)\qquad\forall g\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{h}}.

Therefore, for every
g\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{h}}
,

\displaystyle R_{h,1}(\phi,\hat{g}_{\phi})-R_{h,1}(\phi,\mu_{\phi})

\displaystyle\leq\widehat{R}_{h,1}(\phi,\hat{g}_{\phi})-R_{h,1}(\phi,\mu_{\phi})+\Delta_{1,\nu_{h}}

\displaystyle\leq\widehat{R}_{h,1}(\phi,g)-R_{h,1}(\phi,\mu_{\phi})+\Delta_{1,\nu_{h}}

\displaystyle\leq R_{h,1}(\phi,g)-R_{h,1}(\phi,\mu_{\phi})+2\Delta_{1,\nu_{h}}.

Using the projection identity,

\|\hat{g}_{\phi}-\mu_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}\leq\|g-\mu_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}+2\Delta_{1,\nu_{h}}.

Taking the infimum over
g\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{h}}
and then the supremum over

\phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{h}}
yields

\epsilon_{1,\nu_{h}}\leq\kappa_{1,\nu_{h}}+2\Delta_{1,\nu_{h}}.

Finally, applying the uniform Rademacher deviation bound to the bounded loss
class
\mathcal{L}_{1,\nu_{h}}
gives

\Delta_{1,\nu_{h}}\leq\Delta_{1,\nu_{h}}(\delta)

with probability at least
1-\delta
.
∎

Theorem H.2

(Projected outcome-bridge consistency for learned OutcomeNet)
.

Assume the boundedness conditions above, and assume the two stages are solved
by exact measurable ERM. Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\|T_{h}\hat{h}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}\leq 4\kappa_{2,\nu_{h}}+6B_{\theta}^{2}\kappa_{1,\nu_{h}}+12B_{\theta}^{2}\Delta_{1,\nu_{h}}(\delta/2)+4\Delta_{2,\nu_{h}}(\delta/2),

where

\Delta_{2,\nu_{h}}(\delta):=2\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{D_{2}^{(h)}}(\mathcal{L}_{2,\nu_{h}})+3M_{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(2/\delta)}{2m_{h}}}.

Equivalently,

\displaystyle\|T_{h}\hat{h}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle 4\kappa_{2,\nu_{h}}+6B_{\theta}^{2}\kappa_{1,\nu_{h}}

\displaystyle+24B_{\theta}^{2}\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{D_{1}^{(h)}}(\mathcal{L}_{1,\nu_{h}})+8\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{D_{2}^{(h)}}(\mathcal{L}_{2,\nu_{h}})

\displaystyle+36B_{\theta}^{2}M_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log(4/\delta)}{2n_{h}}}+12M_{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(4/\delta)}{2m_{h}}}.

If there exists a square-integrable outcome bridge
h_{0}
satisfying

T_{h}h_{0}=m_{0},

then the same bound controls the projected bridge error:

\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|_{T_{h}}^{2}=\|T_{h}\hat{h}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}.

Proof.

For every
(\theta,\phi)
, define the population projected prediction

p_{\theta,\phi}(q):=\langle\theta(r),\mu_{\phi}(q)\rangle,

and the empirical plug-in prediction

\hat{p}_{\theta,\phi}(q):=\langle\theta(r),\hat{g}_{\phi}(q)\rangle.

By construction,

T_{h}h_{\theta,\phi}=p_{\theta,\phi}.

In particular,

T_{h}\hat{h}=p_{\hat{\theta},\hat{\phi}}.

Conditional on
D_{1}^{(h)}
, the maps
\hat{g}_{\phi}
are fixed elements of

\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{h}}
. Define the population second-stage risk

R_{h,2}^{\mathrm{plug}}(\theta,\phi):=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\{Y-\hat{p}_{\theta,\phi}(Q)\right\}^{2}\right],

and its empirical version

\widehat{R}_{h,2}^{\mathrm{plug}}(\theta,\phi):=\frac{1}{m_{h}}\sum_{i=1}^{m_{h}}\left\{\tilde{y}_{i}-\hat{p}_{\theta,\phi}(\tilde{q}_{i})\right\}^{2}.

Since

m_{0}(Q)=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid Q],

the least-squares identity gives

R_{h,2}^{\mathrm{plug}}(\theta,\phi)=\mathbb{E}[(Y-m_{0}(Q))^{2}]+\|\hat{p}_{\theta,\phi}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}.

Let

\Delta_{2,\nu_{h}}:=\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\nu_{h}},\,g\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{h}}}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\{Y-\langle\theta(R),g(Q)\rangle\}^{2}\right]-\frac{1}{m_{h}}\sum_{i=1}^{m_{h}}\{\tilde{y}_{i}-\langle\theta(\tilde{R}_{i}),g(\tilde{Q}_{i})\rangle\}^{2}\right|.

Because
\hat{g}_{\phi}\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{h}}
, this uniform deviation controls the
plug-in class:

\sup_{\theta,\phi}\left|R_{h,2}^{\mathrm{plug}}(\theta,\phi)-\widehat{R}_{h,2}^{\mathrm{plug}}(\theta,\phi)\right|\leq\Delta_{2,\nu_{h}}.

By empirical risk minimization,

\widehat{R}_{h,2}^{\mathrm{plug}}(\hat{\theta},\hat{\phi})\leq\widehat{R}_{h,2}^{\mathrm{plug}}(\theta,\phi)\qquad\forall(\theta,\phi)\in\Theta_{\nu_{h}}\times\Phi_{\nu_{h}}.

Therefore,

\|\hat{p}_{\hat{\theta},\hat{\phi}}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}\leq\inf_{\theta,\phi}\|\hat{p}_{\theta,\phi}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}+2\Delta_{2,\nu_{h}}.

Next compare the plug-in prediction
\hat{p}_{\theta,\phi}
with its population
counterpart
p_{\theta,\phi}
. For every
(\theta,\phi)
,

\displaystyle\|\hat{p}_{\theta,\phi}-p_{\theta,\phi}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\{\left\langle\theta(R),\hat{g}_{\phi}(Q)-\mu_{\phi}(Q)\right\rangle\right\}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq B_{\theta}^{2}\|\hat{g}_{\phi}-\mu_{\phi}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq B_{\theta}^{2}\epsilon_{1,\nu_{h}}.

Hence

\inf_{\theta,\phi}\|\hat{p}_{\theta,\phi}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}\leq 2\inf_{\theta,\phi}\|p_{\theta,\phi}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}+2B_{\theta}^{2}\epsilon_{1,\nu_{h}}.

By definition of
\kappa_{2,\nu_{h}}
,

\inf_{\theta,\phi}\|\hat{p}_{\theta,\phi}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}\leq 2\kappa_{2,\nu_{h}}+2B_{\theta}^{2}\epsilon_{1,\nu_{h}}.

Therefore,

\|\hat{p}_{\hat{\theta},\hat{\phi}}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}\leq 2\kappa_{2,\nu_{h}}+2B_{\theta}^{2}\epsilon_{1,\nu_{h}}+2\Delta_{2,\nu_{h}}.

We now pass from the plug-in prediction

\hat{p}_{\hat{\theta},\hat{\phi}}
to the true projected bridge

p_{\hat{\theta},\hat{\phi}}=T_{h}\hat{h}
. Using

\|a+b\|^{2}\leq 2\|a\|^{2}+2\|b\|^{2},

we obtain

\displaystyle\|p_{\hat{\theta},\hat{\phi}}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\hat{p}_{\hat{\theta},\hat{\phi}}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}+2\|p_{\hat{\theta},\hat{\phi}}-\hat{p}_{\hat{\theta},\hat{\phi}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\hat{p}_{\hat{\theta},\hat{\phi}}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}+2B_{\theta}^{2}\epsilon_{1,\nu_{h}}.

Combining the previous displays gives

\|p_{\hat{\theta},\hat{\phi}}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}\leq 4\kappa_{2,\nu_{h}}+6B_{\theta}^{2}\epsilon_{1,\nu_{h}}+4\Delta_{2,\nu_{h}}.

Since

p_{\hat{\theta},\hat{\phi}}=T_{h}\hat{h},

we have

\|T_{h}\hat{h}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}\leq 4\kappa_{2,\nu_{h}}+6B_{\theta}^{2}\epsilon_{1,\nu_{h}}+4\Delta_{2,\nu_{h}}.

By the first-stage lemma, with probability at least
1-\delta/2
,

\epsilon_{1,\nu_{h}}\leq\kappa_{1,\nu_{h}}+2\Delta_{1,\nu_{h}}(\delta/2).

By the uniform deviation inequality applied to

\mathcal{L}_{2,\nu_{h}}
, with probability at least
1-\delta/2
,

\Delta_{2,\nu_{h}}\leq\Delta_{2,\nu_{h}}(\delta/2).

A union bound gives the stated result with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Finally, if
h_{0}
is a valid outcome bridge, then

T_{h}h_{0}=m_{0}.

Therefore

\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|_{T_{h}}^{2}=\|T_{h}(\hat{h}-h_{0})\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}=\|T_{h}\hat{h}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}.

∎

Corollary H.3

(Weak-norm consistency)
.

Suppose

\kappa_{1,\nu_{h}}\to 0,\qquad\kappa_{2,\nu_{h}}\to 0,

and

\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{D_{1}^{(h)}}(\mathcal{L}_{1,\nu_{h}})\to 0,\qquad\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{D_{2}^{(h)}}(\mathcal{L}_{2,\nu_{h}})\to 0

in probability, with
n_{h},m_{h}\to\infty
. Then

\|T_{h}\hat{h}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q})}^{2}\to 0

in probability. If an outcome bridge
h_{0}
exists with
T_{h}h_{0}=m_{0}
, then

\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|_{T_{h}}^{2}\to 0

in probability.

Remark H.4

(What this result does and does not prove)
.

This theorem establishes convergence rates for the outcome bridge function in the
weak norm
induced by the operator
T_{h}

\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|_{T_{h}}=\left\|\mathbb{E}[\hat{h}(A,X,W)-h_{0}(A,X,W)\mid A,X,Z]\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,Z})}.

It does
not
imply

\|\hat{h}-h_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,W})}\to 0,

in general. Indeed, since
h_{0}
is the solution to an ill-posed inverse problem, obtaining strong norm convergence for
\hat{h}
requires controlling sieve measure of ill-posedness, which generally diverges as sieve dimension grows
[
6
,
10
,
9
,
35
,
44
,
28
]
.
In the severely ill-posed case, strong-norm convergence can be arbitrarily slow. The result below avoids this difficulty for the outcome-bridge plug-in estimator: using the dual treatment bridge, the dose-response functional is well-posed with respect to the weak residual
T_{h}\hat{h}-m_{0}
, so projected convergence is sufficient for consistency
[
16
]
. This weak-norm control is then used later in the doubly robust analysis.

Remark H.5

(Approximate optimization)
.

If the first-stage and second-stage empirical minimizations are solved only up to
optimization errors
\eta_{1,\nu_{h}}
and
\eta_{2,\nu_{h}}
, respectively, the same proof
goes through with additional additive terms of order

B_{\theta}^{2}\eta_{1,\nu_{h}}+\eta_{2,\nu_{h}}.

The theorem above sets these errors to zero because it analyzes the exact
profile-ERM idealization, not the stochastic nonconvex optimizer.

H.2
From projected outcome-bridge consistency to dose-response consistency

We now bound the
L^{2}
error for the dose response curve by the weak norm error for the estimated outcome bridge. Our argument adapts
16
, which informally says that learning dose response curve via proximal g-formula is well-posed. In the previous section, we showed

\|T_{h}\hat{h}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,Z})}^{2}=O_{p}(\rho_{h,\nu_{h}})

for some sequence
\rho_{h,\nu_{h}}\to 0
.

For a candidate bridge
h
, define the population plug-in dose-response curve

\bar{f}_{h}(a):=\mathbb{E}[h(a,X,W)].

Our goal is to control

\|\bar{f}_{\hat{h}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}.

Assume that there exists an ATE treatment bridge

\varphi^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)

satisfying

\mathbb{E}[\varphi^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a,X,W]=\frac{p_{A}(a)}{p_{A\mid X,W}(a\mid X,W)}.

Assume moreover that

C_{\varphi}^{2}:=\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{a\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{A}}\mathbb{E}[\{\varphi^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)\}^{2}\mid A=a]<\infty.

Lemma H.6

(Weak residual controls dose-response error)
.

For any square-integrable candidate
h(a,X,W)
,

\left|f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)-\bar{f}_{h}(a)\right|\leq C_{\varphi}(a)\left\|\mathbb{E}[Y-h(a,X,W)\mid A=a,X,Z]\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{X,Z\mid A=a})},

where

C_{\varphi}(a)^{2}:=\mathbb{E}[\{\varphi^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)\}^{2}\mid A=a].

Proof.

By treatment-bridge identification,

f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a].

Also, for any
h
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[h(a,X,W)\varphi^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[h(a,X,W)\mathbb{E}[\varphi^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a,X,W]\mid A=a\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[h(a,X,W)\frac{p_{A}(a)}{p_{A\mid X,W}(a\mid X,W)}\mid A=a\right].

Using Bayes’ rule,

p_{X,W\mid A=a}(x,w)=\frac{p_{A\mid X,W}(a\mid x,w)p_{X,W}(x,w)}{p_{A}(a)}.

Hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[h(a,X,W)\frac{p_{A}(a)}{p_{A\mid X,W}(a\mid X,W)}\mid A=a\right]

\displaystyle=\int h(a,x,w)\frac{p_{A}(a)}{p_{A\mid X,W}(a\mid x,w)}p_{X,W\mid A=a}(x,w)\,dx\,dw

\displaystyle=\int h(a,x,w)p_{X,W}(x,w)\,dx\,dw=\mathbb{E}[h(a,X,W)]=\bar{f}_{h}(a).

Therefore,

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)-\bar{f}_{h}(a)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\varphi^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)\{Y-h(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a].

Conditioning on
(X,Z)
,

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)-\bar{f}_{h}(a)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\varphi^{\mathrm{ATE}}_{0}(a,X,Z)\mathbb{E}[Y-h(a,X,W)\mid A=a,X,Z]\mid A=a\right].

By Cauchy–Schwarz,

\left|f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)-\bar{f}_{h}(a)\right|\leq C_{\varphi}(a)\left\|\mathbb{E}[Y-h(a,X,W)\mid A=a,X,Z]\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{X,Z\mid A=a})}.

∎

Theorem H.7

(Outcome-bridge dose-response consistency)
.

Suppose

\displaystyle\|T_{h}\hat{h}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,Z})}^{2}=O_{p}(\rho_{h,\nu_{h}}),

(85)

and suppose the treatment bridge above exists with

C_{\varphi}:=\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{a\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{A}}C_{\varphi}(a)<\infty.

Then

\|\bar{f}_{\hat{h}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq C_{\varphi}^{2}\|T_{h}\hat{h}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,Z})}^{2}.

Consequently,

\|\bar{f}_{\hat{h}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}=O_{p}(C_{\varphi}^{2}\rho_{h,\nu_{h}}).

In particular, if
\rho_{h,\nu_{h}}\to 0
, then

\bar{f}_{\hat{h}}\to f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\quad\text{in }L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})

in probability.

Proof.

For
h=\hat{h}
, the previous lemma gives, for every
a
,

\left|f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)-\bar{f}_{\hat{h}}(a)\right|^{2}\leq C_{\varphi}(a)^{2}\left\|\mathbb{E}[Y-\hat{h}(a,X,W)\mid A=a,X,Z]\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{X,Z\mid A=a})}^{2}.

Integrating over
a\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{A}
,

\displaystyle\|\bar{f}_{\hat{h}}-

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}=\int\left|\bar{f}_{\hat{h}}(a)-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)\right|^{2}\,d{\mathbb{P}}_{A}(a)

\displaystyle\leq\int C_{\varphi}(a)^{2}\left[\int\left\{\mathbb{E}[Y-\hat{h}(a,X,W)\mid A=a,X=x,Z=z]\right\}^{2}dP_{X,Z\mid A=a}(x,z)\right]d{\mathbb{P}}_{A}(a)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varphi}^{2}\int\int\left\{\mathbb{E}[Y-\hat{h}(a,X,W)\mid A=a,X=x,Z=z]\right\}^{2}d{\mathbb{P}}_{X,Z\mid A=a}(x,z)d{\mathbb{P}}_{A}(a)

\displaystyle=C_{\varphi}^{2}\int\left\{\mathbb{E}[Y-\hat{h}(A,X,W)\mid A,X,Z]\right\}^{2}dP_{A,X,Z}(a,x,z).

Since

m_{0}(A,X,Z)=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid A,X,Z],

we have

\mathbb{E}[Y-\hat{h}(A,X,W)\mid A,X,Z]=m_{0}(A,X,Z)-T_{h}\hat{h}(A,X,Z).

Therefore,

\|\bar{f}_{\hat{h}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq C_{\varphi}^{2}\|T_{h}\hat{h}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,Z})}^{2}.

The theorem follows immediately from Eq.
85
in the theorem statement.
∎

Theorem H.8

(Empirical outcome-bridge averaging error)
.

Let
\hat{h}
be trained on a sample independent of an evaluation sample

\mathcal{D}_{3}^{(h)}=\{(x_{i}^{\circ},w_{i}^{\circ})\}_{i=1}^{t_{h}}
, where

(x_{i}^{\circ},w_{i}^{\circ})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}{\mathbb{P}}_{X,W}
. Define

\mu_{\hat{h}}(a):=\mathbb{E}[\hat{h}(a,X,W)\mid\hat{h}],\qquad\hat{\mu}_{h}(a):=\frac{1}{t_{h}}\sum_{i=1}^{t_{h}}\hat{h}(a,x_{i}^{\circ},w_{i}^{\circ}).

Assume that the learned outcome-bridge class is uniformly bounded, so that

|\hat{h}(a,x,w)|\leq B_{h}
almost surely. Then

\mathcal{E}_{\mu,h}:=\|\hat{\mu}_{h}-\mu_{\hat{h}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}=O_{p}(t_{h}^{-1}).

Consequently, if

\|\mu_{\hat{h}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}=O_{p}(\rho_{h,\nu_{h}})
, then

\|\hat{\mu}_{h}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}=O_{p}(\rho_{h,\nu_{h}}+t_{h}^{-1}).

Proof.

Condition on the trained bridge
\hat{h}
. For every fixed
a
, the empirical average

\hat{\mu}_{h}(a)
is unbiased for
\mu_{\hat{h}}(a)
, since

\mathbb{E}[\hat{\mu}_{h}(a)\mid\hat{h}]=\mathbb{E}[\hat{h}(a,X,W)\mid\hat{h}]=\mu_{\hat{h}}(a).

Moreover, because
(x_{i}^{\circ},w_{i}^{\circ})
are conditionally i.i.d.,

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\{\hat{\mu}_{h}(a)-\mu_{\hat{h}}(a)\}^{2}\mid\hat{h}\right]=\frac{1}{t_{h}}\mathrm{Var}\!\left(\hat{h}(a,X,W)\mid\hat{h}\right)\leq\frac{B_{h}^{2}}{t_{h}}.

Integrating over
a\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{A}
gives

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mu,h}\mid\hat{h}\right]=\int\mathbb{E}\!\left[\{\hat{\mu}_{h}(a)-\mu_{\hat{h}}(a)\}^{2}\mid\hat{h}\right]d{\mathbb{P}}_{A}(a)\leq\frac{B_{h}^{2}}{t_{h}}.

Therefore, by Markov’s inequality, for every
\delta>0
,

\Pr\!\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mu,h}>\frac{B_{h}^{2}}{\delta t_{h}}\;\middle|\;\hat{h}\right)\leq\delta,

and hence
\mathcal{E}_{\mu,h}=O_{p}(t_{h}^{-1})
.

Finally,

\hat{\mu}_{h}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}=(\hat{\mu}_{h}-\mu_{\hat{h}})+(\mu_{\hat{h}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}),

so

\|\hat{\mu}_{h}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq 2\|\hat{\mu}_{h}-\mu_{\hat{h}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}+2\|\mu_{\hat{h}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}.

Combining the empirical averaging bound with

\|\mu_{\hat{h}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}=O_{p}(\rho_{h,\nu_{h}})

gives the stated rate.
∎

H.3
Treatment bridge consistency for dose-response estimation

We obtain convergence rates for
TreatmentNet
by controlling the Rademacher complexity of relevant function classes. Our proof is the counterpart of the corresponding analysis for
OutcomeNet
in Appendix
H.1
. For simplicity, we profile out first stage nuisances, and assume each nuisance is estimated on a separate split of the sample.

Let

Q_{\varphi}:=(A,X,W),\qquad B_{\varphi}:=(A,X),

and define the ATE treatment-bridge target

r_{0}(q_{\varphi}):=r_{0}(a,x,w)=\frac{p_{A}(a)}{p_{A\mid X,W}(a\mid x,w)}.

For a measurable function
u(a,x,z)
, define the treatment-bridge conditional
expectation operator

(T_{\varphi}u)(a,x,w):=\mathbb{E}[u(a,x,Z)\mid A=a,X=x,W=w],

and the projected seminorm

\|u\|_{T_{\varphi}}:=\|T_{\varphi}u\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}.

H.3.0.0.1
Density-ratio plug-in.

In the implementation, TreatmentNet is trained with an estimated density ratio rather than the true density ratio
r_{0}
. We refer to the latter problem as the oracle learning problem, and
r_{0}
as an oracle target. Let

\mathcal{D}_{r}^{(\varphi)}

be an independent sample used to construct a density-ratio estimator

\hat{r}:\mathcal{Q}_{\varphi}\to\mathbb{R}.

Throughout the treatment-side analysis, all empirical second-stage regressions use the plug-in target
\hat{r}(Q_{\varphi})
. We separate its error as

\mathcal{E}_{r}:=\|\hat{r}-r_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}.

All statements below are conditional on
\mathcal{D}_{r}^{(\varphi)}
, except when the final oracle-target bound explicitly adds
\mathcal{E}_{r}
.

The first-stage sample is

D_{1}^{(\varphi)}=\{(\bar{q}_{i},\bar{z}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\varphi}},

and the second-stage sample is

D_{2}^{(\varphi)}=\{\tilde{q}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m_{\varphi}}.

The second-stage labels are generated as
\hat{r}(\tilde{q}_{i})
. We assume that

D_{1}^{(\varphi)}
,
D_{2}^{(\varphi)}
, and
\mathcal{D}_{r}^{(\varphi)}
are mutually independent.

Let
\nu_{\varphi}=(n_{\varphi},m_{\varphi})
denote the sample split sizes. Also, let

\Phi_{\nu_{\varphi}}\subset\{\phi:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\nu_{\varphi}}}\}

be a class of learned treatment-proxy feature maps,

\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{\varphi}}\subset\{g:\mathcal{Q}_{\varphi}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\nu_{\varphi}}}\}

be a class of first-stage conditional-mean regressors, and

\Theta_{\nu_{\varphi}}\subset\{\theta:\mathcal{B}_{\varphi}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\nu_{\varphi}}}\}

be a class of second-stage heads. The treatment-bridge class is

\mathcal{H}_{\nu_{\varphi}}:=\left\{\varphi_{\theta,\phi}(a,x,z)=\langle\theta(a,x),\phi(z)\rangle:\theta\in\Theta_{\nu_{\varphi}},\ \phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{\varphi}}\right\}.

This notation includes the tensor-product implementation

\varphi(a,x,z)=\left\langle\bm{\varphi},\phi_{AX,2}^{(\varphi)}(a,x)\otimes\phi_{Z,2}^{(\varphi)}(z)\right\rangle,

where
\bm{\varphi}
is the final treatment-network head. Equivalently, the final head may be absorbed into the vector-valued map
\theta(a,x)
.

For every
\phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{\varphi}}
, define the population conditional feature mean

\mu_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}(q_{\varphi}):=\mathbb{E}[\phi(Z)\mid Q_{\varphi}=q_{\varphi}].

Then

(T_{\varphi}\varphi_{\theta,\phi})(q_{\varphi})=\langle\theta(b_{\varphi}),\mu_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}(q_{\varphi})\rangle.

For each candidate feature map
\phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{\varphi}}
, define the first-stage empirical risk

\widehat{R}_{\varphi,1}(\phi,g):=\frac{1}{n_{\varphi}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\varphi}}\|\phi(\bar{z}_{i})-g(\bar{q}_{i})\|_{2}^{2},\qquad g\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{\varphi}}.

The profiled first-stage estimator is

\hat{g}_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{\varphi}}}\widehat{R}_{\varphi,1}(\phi,g).

The second-stage profiled ERM is

(\hat{\theta}^{(\varphi)},\hat{\phi}^{(\varphi)})\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\theta\in\Theta_{\nu_{\varphi}},\ \phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{\varphi}}}\widehat{R}_{\varphi,2}(\theta,\phi;\hat{r}),

where

\widehat{R}_{\varphi,2}(\theta,\phi;\hat{r}):=\frac{1}{m_{\varphi}}\sum_{i=1}^{m_{\varphi}}\left\{\hat{r}(\tilde{q}_{i})-\left\langle\theta(\tilde{b}_{i}),\hat{g}_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}(\tilde{q}_{i})\right\rangle\right\}^{2}.

The learned treatment bridge is

\hat{\varphi}(a,x,z):=\left\langle\hat{\theta}^{(\varphi)}(a,x),\hat{\phi}^{(\varphi)}(z)\right\rangle.

Assumption H.9

(Treatment-side boundedness)
.

There exist finite constants

B_{r},\quad B_{\hat{r}},\quad B_{\phi},\quad B_{g},\quad B_{\theta}

such that:

•

The oracle density ratio is bounded:

|r_{0}(Q_{\varphi})|\leq B_{r}\quad\text{almost surely}.

•

The plug-in density-ratio estimator is bounded conditional on

\mathcal{D}_{r}^{(\varphi)}
:

|\hat{r}(Q_{\varphi})|\leq B_{\hat{r}}\quad\text{almost surely}.

•

The treatment-proxy feature maps are uniformly bounded:

\sup_{\phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{\varphi}}}\sup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\|\phi(z)\|_{2}\leq B_{\phi}.

•

The first-stage conditional-mean class is uniformly bounded:

\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{\varphi}}}\sup_{q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\varphi}}\|g(q)\|_{2}\leq B_{g}.

•

The second-stage head class is uniformly bounded:

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\nu_{\varphi}}}\sup_{b\in\mathcal{B}_{\varphi}}\|\theta(b)\|_{2}\leq B_{\theta}.

Define

M_{\varphi,1}:=(B_{\phi}+B_{g})^{2},\qquad M_{\varphi,2}:=(B_{\hat{r}}+B_{\theta}B_{g})^{2}.

H.3.0.0.2
Approximation errors.

Define the first-stage conditional-mean approximation error

\kappa_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}:=\sup_{\phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{\varphi}}}\inf_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{\varphi}}}\|g-\mu_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}.

For the plug-in second-stage target, define

\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}:=\inf_{\theta\in\Theta_{\nu_{\varphi}},\ \phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{\varphi}}}\left\|\hat{r}-\langle\theta,\mu_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}\rangle\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2},

where

\langle\theta,\mu_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}\rangle(q_{\varphi}):=\langle\theta(b_{\varphi}),\mu_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}(q_{\varphi})\rangle.

For reference, the corresponding oracle approximation error is

\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{0}:=\inf_{\theta\in\Theta_{\nu_{\varphi}},\ \phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{\varphi}}}\left\|r_{0}-\langle\theta,\mu_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}\rangle\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}.

By the triangle inequality,

\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}\leq 2\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{0}+2\mathcal{E}_{r}.

H.3.0.0.3
Loss classes.

Let

\mathcal{L}_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}:=\left\{(q,z)\mapsto\|\phi(z)-g(q)\|_{2}^{2}:\phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{\varphi}},\ g\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{\varphi}}\right\},

and define the plug-in second-stage loss class

\mathcal{L}_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}(\hat{r}):=\left\{q\mapsto\left(\hat{r}(q)-\langle\theta(b),g(q)\rangle\right)^{2}:\theta\in\Theta_{\nu_{\varphi}},\ g\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{\varphi}}\right\}.

For a sample
S=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{N})
, we define the empirical Rademacher complexity of the function class
\mathcal{F}

\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{S}(\mathcal{F}):=\mathbb{E}_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{i}f(s_{i})\right],

where we take expectation with respect to independent Rademacher random variables
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{N}
.

We apply the following uniform deviation inequality
[
50
]
. If
\mathcal{F}
takes values in

[0,M]
, then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}|{\mathbb{P}}f-{\mathbb{P}}_{N}f|\leq 2\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{S}(\mathcal{F})+3M\sqrt{\frac{\log(2/\delta)}{2N}}.

Define

\Delta_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}(\delta):=2\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{D_{1}^{(\varphi)}}(\mathcal{L}_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}})+3M_{\varphi,1}\sqrt{\frac{\log(2/\delta)}{2n_{\varphi}}},

and

\Delta_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}(\delta):=2\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{D_{2}^{(\varphi)}}(\mathcal{L}_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}(\hat{r}))+3M_{\varphi,2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(2/\delta)}{2m_{\varphi}}}.

Lemma H.10

(Uniform treatment Stage-1 error)
.

Define

\epsilon_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}:=\sup_{\phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{\varphi}}}\|\hat{g}_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}-\mu_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}.

Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\epsilon_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}\leq\kappa_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}+2\Delta_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}(\delta).

Proof.

Fix
\phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{\varphi}}
. For any measurable

g:\mathcal{Q}_{\varphi}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\nu_{\varphi}}}
,

\mathbb{E}\|\phi(Z)-g(Q_{\varphi})\|_{2}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\|\phi(Z)-\mu_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}(Q_{\varphi})\|_{2}^{2}+\|g-\mu_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2},

where the cross term vanishes because

\mu_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}(Q_{\varphi})=\mathbb{E}[\phi(Z)\mid Q_{\varphi}].

The uniform deviation bound for

\mathcal{L}_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}
, together with empirical optimality of

\hat{g}_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}
, gives uniformly over
\phi

\|\hat{g}_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}-\mu_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}\leq\inf_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu_{\varphi}}}\|g-\mu_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}+2\Delta_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}(\delta).

Taking the supremum over
\phi\in\Phi_{\nu_{\varphi}}
gives the result.
∎

Theorem H.11

(Projected plug-in treatment-bridge consistency)
.

Assume Assumption
H.9
and exact measurable ERM in both stages. Conditional on
\mathcal{D}_{r}^{(\varphi)}
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-\hat{r}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}\leq\;

\displaystyle 4\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}+6B_{\theta}^{2}\kappa_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}+12B_{\theta}^{2}\Delta_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}(\delta/2)+4\Delta_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}(\delta/2).

Proof.

For every
(\theta,\phi)
, define the population projected prediction

p_{\theta,\phi}^{(\varphi)}(q):=\langle\theta(b),\mu_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}(q)\rangle,

and the empirical plug-in prediction

\hat{p}_{\theta,\phi}^{(\varphi)}(q):=\langle\theta(b),\hat{g}_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}(q)\rangle.

By construction,

T_{\varphi}\varphi_{\theta,\phi}=p_{\theta,\phi}^{(\varphi)}.

In particular,

T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}=p_{\hat{\theta}^{(\varphi)},\hat{\phi}^{(\varphi)}}^{(\varphi)}.

Conditional on
D_{1}^{(\varphi)}
and
\mathcal{D}_{r}^{(\varphi)}
, the maps

\hat{g}_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}
and the plug-in target
\hat{r}
are fixed. Define

R_{\varphi,2}^{\mathrm{plug}}(\theta,\phi;\hat{r}):=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\{\hat{r}(Q_{\varphi})-\hat{p}_{\theta,\phi}^{(\varphi)}(Q_{\varphi})\right\}^{2}\right],

and

\widehat{R}_{\varphi,2}^{\mathrm{plug}}(\theta,\phi;\hat{r}):=\frac{1}{m_{\varphi}}\sum_{i=1}^{m_{\varphi}}\left\{\hat{r}(\tilde{q}_{i})-\hat{p}_{\theta,\phi}^{(\varphi)}(\tilde{q}_{i})\right\}^{2}.

The second-stage ERM minimizes

\widehat{R}_{\varphi,2}^{\mathrm{plug}}(\theta,\phi;\hat{r})
.
By the uniform deviation inequality applied to

\mathcal{L}_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}(\hat{r})
, with probability at least

1-\delta/2
,

\sup_{\theta,\phi}\left|R_{\varphi,2}^{\mathrm{plug}}(\theta,\phi;\hat{r})-\widehat{R}_{\varphi,2}^{\mathrm{plug}}(\theta,\phi;\hat{r})\right|\leq\Delta_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}(\delta/2).

Therefore,

\|\hat{p}_{\hat{\theta},\hat{\phi}}^{(\varphi)}-\hat{r}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}\leq\inf_{\theta,\phi}\|\hat{p}_{\theta,\phi}^{(\varphi)}-\hat{r}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}+2\Delta_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}(\delta/2).

For every
(\theta,\phi)
,

\|\hat{p}_{\theta,\phi}^{(\varphi)}-p_{\theta,\phi}^{(\varphi)}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}\leq B_{\theta}^{2}\|\hat{g}_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}-\mu_{\phi}^{(\varphi)}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}\leq B_{\theta}^{2}\epsilon_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}.

Hence

\inf_{\theta,\phi}\|\hat{p}_{\theta,\phi}^{(\varphi)}-\hat{r}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}\leq 2\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}+2B_{\theta}^{2}\epsilon_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}.

Thus

\|\hat{p}_{\hat{\theta},\hat{\phi}}^{(\varphi)}-\hat{r}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}\leq 2\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}+2B_{\theta}^{2}\epsilon_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}+2\Delta_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}(\delta/2).

Finally, pass from the empirical plug-in prediction

\hat{p}_{\hat{\theta},\hat{\phi}}^{(\varphi)}
to the true projected bridge

p_{\hat{\theta},\hat{\phi}}^{(\varphi)}=T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}
. Using

\|a+b\|^{2}\leq 2\|a\|^{2}+2\|b\|^{2}
,

\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-\hat{r}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}\leq 2\|\hat{p}_{\hat{\theta},\hat{\phi}}^{(\varphi)}-\hat{r}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}+2B_{\theta}^{2}\epsilon_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}.

Combining all of the above yields

\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-\hat{r}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}\leq 4\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}+6B_{\theta}^{2}\epsilon_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}+4\Delta_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}(\delta/2).

By Lemma
H.10
, with probability at least

1-\delta/2
,

\epsilon_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}\leq\kappa_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}+2\Delta_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}(\delta/2).

We complete the proof via a union bound with respect to the high probability events above.
∎

Corollary H.12

(Projected treatment-bridge consistency for the oracle target)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
H.11
,
conditional on
\mathcal{D}_{r}^{(\varphi)}
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-r_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}\leq\;

\displaystyle 8\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}+12B_{\theta}^{2}\kappa_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}

\displaystyle+24B_{\theta}^{2}\Delta_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}(\delta/2)+8\Delta_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}(\delta/2)+2\mathcal{E}_{r}.

Equivalently, using

\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}\leq 2\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{0}+2\mathcal{E}_{r},

we also have

\displaystyle\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-r_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}\leq\;

\displaystyle 16\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{0}+12B_{\theta}^{2}\kappa_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}

\displaystyle+24B_{\theta}^{2}\Delta_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}(\delta/2)+8\Delta_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}(\delta/2)+18\mathcal{E}_{r}.

Proof.

By the triangle inequality,

\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-r_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}\leq 2\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-\hat{r}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}+2\|\hat{r}-r_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}.

The first display follows from Theorem
H.11
. The second display follows from

\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}\leq 2\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{0}+2\mathcal{E}_{r}
.
∎

Corollary H.13

(Projected TreatmentNet consistency)
.

Suppose

\kappa_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}\to 0,\qquad\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{0}\to 0,\qquad\mathcal{E}_{r}\to 0,

and

\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{D_{1}^{(\varphi)}}(\mathcal{L}_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}})\to 0,\qquad\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{D_{2}^{(\varphi)}}(\mathcal{L}_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}(\hat{r}))\to 0

in probability, with
n_{\varphi},m_{\varphi}\to\infty
. Then

\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-r_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}\to 0

in probability.

Remark H.14

(What this result does and does not prove)
.

This theorem proves convergence only in the projected treatment-bridge seminorm:

\|\hat{\varphi}-\varphi_{0}\|_{T_{\varphi}}=\left\|\mathbb{E}[\hat{\varphi}(A,X,Z)-\varphi_{0}(A,X,Z)\mid A,X,W]\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,W})}.

It does not imply

\|\hat{\varphi}-\varphi_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,Z})}\to 0

without an additional inverse-stability assumption. No strong bridge convergence is used in the dose-response result below.

Remark H.15

(Role of density-ratio estimation)
.

The density-ratio nuisance enters only through

\mathcal{E}_{r}=\|\hat{r}-r_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}.

The treatment bridge is trained against the plug-in target
\hat{r}
, while the causal target is defined by
r_{0}
. Corollary
H.12
separates these two errors explicitly. Establishing rates for
\mathcal{E}_{r}
depends on the chosen density-ratio estimator and is treated as an external nuisance problem.

H.4
Dose-response consistency of TreatmentNet

We now convert the projected treatment-bridge result into consistency of the
ATE dose-response curve. This step does not require strong convergence of

\hat{\varphi}
in
L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,Z})
. Instead, it uses the outcome bridge as the
dual object and controls the population dose-response error through the
projected treatment-bridge residual.

Recall that

Q_{\varphi}:=(A,X,W),\qquad r_{0}(a,x,w)=\frac{p_{A}(a)}{p_{A\mid X,W}(a\mid x,w)}.

The treatment-bridge representation of the ATE dose-response curve is

f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[Y\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a],

where

T_{\varphi}\varphi_{0}=r_{0}.

Given the learned treatment bridge
\hat{\varphi}
, define the population plug-in treatment-side curve

\bar{f}_{\hat{\varphi}}^{(\varphi)}(a):=\mathbb{E}[Y\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a].

Assumption H.16

(Dual outcome bridge and boundedness)
.

Assume that:

•

There exists an outcome bridge
h_{0}
satisfying

\mathbb{E}[Y-h_{0}(a,X,W)\mid A=a,X,Z]=0.

•

The outcome bridge has uniformly bounded conditional second moment:

C_{h}^{2}:=\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{a\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{A}}\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)^{2}\mid A=a]<\infty.

Lemma H.17

(Projected treatment residual controls the population curve)
.

For any square-integrable candidate treatment bridge
\varphi(a,X,Z)
,

\left|\bar{f}_{\varphi}^{(\varphi)}(a)-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)\right|\leq C_{h}(a)\left\|T_{\varphi}\varphi(a,X,W)-r_{0}(a,X,W)\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{X,W\mid A=a})},

where

C_{h}(a)^{2}:=\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)^{2}\mid A=a].

Consequently,

\|\bar{f}_{\varphi}^{(\varphi)}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq C_{h}^{2}\|T_{\varphi}\varphi-r_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}.

Proof.

For any
\varphi
,

\bar{f}_{\varphi}^{(\varphi)}(a)-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[Y\{\varphi(a,X,Z)-\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)\}\mid A=a].

Since
\varphi(a,X,Z)-\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)
is a function of
(X,Z)
, the outcome-bridge equation gives

\mathbb{E}[\{Y-h_{0}(a,X,W)\}\{\varphi(a,X,Z)-\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)\}\mid A=a]=0.

Therefore,

\bar{f}_{\varphi}^{(\varphi)}(a)-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)\{\varphi(a,X,Z)-\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)\}\mid A=a].

Conditioning on
(X,W)
,

\bar{f}_{\varphi}^{(\varphi)}(a)-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)=\mathbb{E}\!\left[h_{0}(a,X,W)\mathbb{E}[\varphi(a,X,Z)-\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a,X,W]\mid A=a\right].

Since
T_{\varphi}\varphi_{0}=r_{0}
, the inner conditional expectation equals

T_{\varphi}\varphi(a,X,W)-r_{0}(a,X,W).

Cauchy–Schwarz gives

\left|\bar{f}_{\varphi}^{(\varphi)}(a)-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)\right|^{2}\leq C_{h}(a)^{2}\left\|T_{\varphi}\varphi(a,X,W)-r_{0}(a,X,W)\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{X,W\mid A=a})}^{2}.

Integrating over
a\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{A}
and using

C_{h}(a)^{2}\leq C_{h}^{2}

almost surely proves the final display.
∎

Theorem H.18

(Population dose-response error of TreatmentNet)
.

Let

\mathcal{E}_{r}:=\|\hat{r}-r_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}

be the density-ratio estimation error from Appendix
H.3
.
Then

\|\bar{f}_{\hat{\varphi}}^{(\varphi)}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq 2C_{h}^{2}\left\{\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-\hat{r}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}+\mathcal{E}_{r}\right\}.

Consequently, on the event of
Theorem
H.11
,

\displaystyle\|\bar{f}_{\hat{\varphi}}^{(\varphi)}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq\;

\displaystyle 2C_{h}^{2}\Big[4\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}+6B_{\theta}^{2}\kappa_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}

\displaystyle\quad+12B_{\theta}^{2}\Delta_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}(\delta/2)+4\Delta_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}(\delta/2)+\mathcal{E}_{r}\Big].

Proof.

By Lemma
H.17
,

\|\bar{f}_{\hat{\varphi}}^{(\varphi)}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq C_{h}^{2}\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-r_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}.

Using

T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-r_{0}=(T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-\hat{r})+(\hat{r}-r_{0})

and
\|u+v\|^{2}\leq 2\|u\|^{2}+2\|v\|^{2}
, we obtain

\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-r_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}\leq 2\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-\hat{r}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{Q_{\varphi}})}^{2}+2\mathcal{E}_{r}.

This proves the first display. The second display follows by substituting the projected plug-in treatment-bridge bound from
Theorem
H.11
.
∎

H.4.0.0.1
Third-stage regression.

The empirical TreatmentNet dose-response estimator is obtained by a third-stage regression of

Y\hat{\varphi}(A,X,Z)

on
A
. Let

D_{3}^{(\varphi)}=\{(a_{i},y_{i},x_{i},z_{i})\}_{i=1}^{t_{\varphi}}

be an independent third-stage sample with
t_{\varphi}
number of observations

\mathcal{F}_{t_{\varphi}}^{(\varphi)}

be a scalar regression class
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}
. Define

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{(\varphi)}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{t_{\varphi}}^{(\varphi)}}\frac{1}{t_{\varphi}}\sum_{i=1}^{t_{\varphi}}\{y_{i}\hat{\varphi}(a_{i},x_{i},z_{i})-f(a_{i})\}^{2}.

The population third-stage regression target is

\bar{f}_{\hat{\varphi}}^{(\varphi)}(a)=\mathbb{E}[Y\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a].

Define the third-stage approximation error

\kappa_{\varphi,3,t_{\varphi}}:=\inf_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{t_{\varphi}}^{(\varphi)}}\|f-\bar{f}_{\hat{\varphi}}^{(\varphi)}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}.

Conditional on
\hat{\varphi}
, define the third-stage loss class

\mathcal{L}_{\varphi,3,t_{\varphi}}(\hat{\varphi}):=\left\{(y,a,x,z)\mapsto\left(y\hat{\varphi}(a,x,z)-f(a)\right)^{2}:f\in\mathcal{F}_{t_{\varphi}}^{(\varphi)}\right\}.

Assumption H.19

(Third-stage boundedness)
.

Conditional on the trained treatment bridge
\hat{\varphi}
, assume that:

•

The third-stage loss class is uniformly bounded:

\sup_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\varphi,3,t_{\varphi}}(\hat{\varphi})}|\ell|\leq M_{\varphi,3}

for some finite constant
M_{\varphi,3}
.

•

The third-stage sample
D_{3}^{(\varphi)}
is independent of the samples used to train
\hat{r}
and
\hat{\varphi}
.

Define the third-stage empirical process error

\Delta_{\varphi,3,t_{\varphi}}(\delta):=2\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{D_{3}^{(\varphi)}}(\mathcal{L}_{\varphi,3,t_{\varphi}}(\hat{\varphi}))+3M_{\varphi,3}\sqrt{\frac{\log(2/\delta)}{2t_{\varphi}}}.

Lemma H.20

(Third-stage Rademacher bound)
.

Under Assumption
H.19
, conditional on the trained treatment bridge
\hat{\varphi}
, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\|\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{(\varphi)}-\bar{f}_{\hat{\varphi}}^{(\varphi)}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq\kappa_{\varphi,3,t_{\varphi}}+2\Delta_{\varphi,3,t_{\varphi}}(\delta).

Proof.

Conditional on
\hat{\varphi}
, this is ordinary least-squares regression with response

\Gamma_{\varphi}:=Y\hat{\varphi}(A,X,Z)

and covariate
A
. Since

\bar{f}_{\hat{\varphi}}^{(\varphi)}(A)=\mathbb{E}[\Gamma_{\varphi}\mid A,\hat{\varphi}],

the least-squares projection identity gives

\mathbb{E}[\{\Gamma_{\varphi}-f(A)\}^{2}\mid\hat{\varphi}]-\mathbb{E}[\{\Gamma_{\varphi}-\bar{f}_{\hat{\varphi}}^{(\varphi)}(A)\}^{2}\mid\hat{\varphi}]=\|f-\bar{f}_{\hat{\varphi}}^{(\varphi)}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}.

Applying the uniform deviation inequality to

\mathcal{L}_{\varphi,3,t_{\varphi}}(\hat{\varphi})
and using empirical optimality of

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{(\varphi)}
proves the claim.
∎

Theorem H.21

(TreatmentNet dose-response consistency)
.

Suppose Assumptions
H.16
and

H.19
hold. On the intersection of the high-probability events from
Theorem
H.11
and
Lemma
H.20
,

\displaystyle\|\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{(\varphi)}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq\;

\displaystyle 2\kappa_{\varphi,3,t_{\varphi}}+4\Delta_{\varphi,3,t_{\varphi}}(\delta)

\displaystyle+4C_{h}^{2}\Big[4\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}+6B_{\theta}^{2}\kappa_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+12B_{\theta}^{2}\Delta_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}(\delta/2)+4\Delta_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}(\delta/2)+\mathcal{E}_{r}\Big].

Proof.

Decompose

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{(\varphi)}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}=(\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{(\varphi)}-\bar{f}_{\hat{\varphi}}^{(\varphi)})+(\bar{f}_{\hat{\varphi}}^{(\varphi)}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}).

Using
(u+v)^{2}\leq 2u^{2}+2v^{2}
,

\|\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{(\varphi)}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq 2\|\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{(\varphi)}-\bar{f}_{\hat{\varphi}}^{(\varphi)}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}+2\|\bar{f}_{\hat{\varphi}}^{(\varphi)}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}.

The first term is controlled by Lemma
H.20
. The second term is controlled by Theorem
H.18
. Substituting both bounds gives the result.
∎

Corollary H.22

(TreatmentNet dose-response consistency)
.

Suppose

\kappa_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}\to 0,\qquad\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{0}\to 0,\qquad\kappa_{\varphi,3,t_{\varphi}}\to 0,\qquad\mathcal{E}_{r}\to 0,

and

\Delta_{\varphi,1,\nu_{\varphi}}(\delta)\to 0,\qquad\Delta_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}(\delta)\to 0,\qquad\Delta_{\varphi,3,t_{\varphi}}(\delta)\to 0

in probability, with
n_{\varphi},m_{\varphi},t_{\varphi}\to\infty
. If
C_{h}<\infty
, then

\|\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{(\varphi)}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}\to 0

in probability.

Proof.

Use

\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{\hat{r}}\leq 2\kappa_{\varphi,2,\nu_{\varphi}}^{0}+2\mathcal{E}_{r}

inside Theorem
H.21
. The stated assumptions force every term in the bound to vanish.
∎

H.5
Doubly robust dose-response consistency

We now analyze DRPCLNET-V1 for the ATE dose-response curve. The bridge estimators

\hat{h}
and
\hat{\varphi}
, as well as the plug-in density-ratio estimator

\hat{r}
, are trained on samples independent of the final-stage residual-regression
sample and are treated as fixed in the final-stage analysis. The result uses only
projected bridge residuals. It does not require strong
L^{2}
-convergence of either
bridge.

Let

(T_{h}h)(a,x,z):=\mathbb{E}[h(a,x,W)\mid A=a,X=x,Z=z],

and

(T_{\varphi}\varphi)(a,x,w):=\mathbb{E}[\varphi(a,x,Z)\mid A=a,X=x,W=w].

Write

m_{0}(a,x,z):=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid A=a,X=x,Z=z],\qquad r_{0}(a,x,w):=\frac{p_{A}(a)}{p_{A\mid X,W}(a\mid x,w)}.

The oracle outcome and treatment bridge equations are

T_{h}h_{0}=m_{0},\qquad T_{\varphi}\varphi_{0}=r_{0}.

The density-ratio nuisance error is

\mathcal{E}_{r}:=\|\hat{r}-r_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,W})}^{2}.

H.5.0.0.1
Population plug-in DR functional.

Define

\mu_{\hat{h}}(a):=\mathbb{E}[\hat{h}(a,X,W)]

and

\kappa_{\hat{h},\hat{\varphi}}(a):=\mathbb{E}[\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\{Y-\hat{h}(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a].

The population plug-in DRPCLNET-V1 functional is

\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}(a):=\mu_{\hat{h}}(a)+\kappa_{\hat{h},\hat{\varphi}}(a).

Lemma H.23

(ATE doubly robust identity)
.

For every
a
,

f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)-\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[\{\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)-\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\}\{h_{0}(a,X,W)-\hat{h}(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a].

Proof.

By the outcome bridge equation,

\mathbb{E}[Y-h_{0}(a,X,W)\mid A=a,X,Z]=0.

Since
\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)
is a function of
(X,Z)
,

\mathbb{E}[\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\{Y-h_{0}(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a]=0.

Therefore,

\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[\hat{h}(a,X,W)]+\mathbb{E}[\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\{h_{0}(a,X,W)-\hat{h}(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a].

By the outcome bridge representation,

f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)].

Hence

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}(a)-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\hat{h}(a,X,W)-h_{0}(a,X,W)]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}[\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\{h_{0}(a,X,W)-\hat{h}(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a].

The treatment bridge reweighting identity gives, for any square-integrable

q(a,X,W)
,

\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)q(a,X,W)\mid A=a]=\mathbb{E}[q(a,X,W)].

Applying this identity with

q(a,X,W)=\hat{h}(a,X,W)-h_{0}(a,X,W)

yields

\mathbb{E}[\hat{h}(a,X,W)-h_{0}(a,X,W)]=\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)\{\hat{h}(a,X,W)-h_{0}(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a].

Substitution gives

\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}(a)-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[\{\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)-\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)\}\{h_{0}(a,X,W)-\hat{h}(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a].

Multiplying both sides by
-1
proves the claim.
∎

H.5.0.0.2
Projected residual bounds for the population DR remainder.

Define the conditional bridge-error constants

B_{e,\varphi}^{2}:=\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{a\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{A}}\mathbb{E}[\{\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)-\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)\}^{2}\mid A=a],

and

B_{e,h}^{2}:=\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{a\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{A}}\mathbb{E}[\{h_{0}(a,X,W)-\hat{h}(a,X,W)\}^{2}\mid A=a].

Under the bounded bridge-class assumptions used in the outcome- and treatment-side
consistency analyses, these constants are finite.

Define the projected residuals

\mathcal{R}_{h}^{\mathrm{weak}}:=\|T_{h}\hat{h}-m_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,Z})}^{2},

\mathcal{R}_{\varphi}^{\mathrm{weak},\hat{r}}:=\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-\hat{r}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,W})}^{2},

and

\mathcal{R}_{\varphi}^{\mathrm{weak},0}:=\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-r_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,W})}^{2}.

Then

\mathcal{R}_{\varphi}^{\mathrm{weak},0}\leq 2\mathcal{R}_{\varphi}^{\mathrm{weak},\hat{r}}+2\mathcal{E}_{r}.

Lemma H.24

(Population DR remainder controlled by projected residuals)
.

Assume
B_{e,\varphi}<\infty
and
B_{e,h}<\infty
. Then

\|\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq B_{e,\varphi}^{2}\mathcal{R}_{h}^{\mathrm{weak}},

and

\|\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq B_{e,h}^{2}\mathcal{R}_{\varphi}^{\mathrm{weak},0}.

Consequently,

\|\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq\min\left\{B_{e,\varphi}^{2}\mathcal{R}_{h}^{\mathrm{weak}},\;B_{e,h}^{2}\mathcal{R}_{\varphi}^{\mathrm{weak},0}\right\},

and also

\|\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq\min\left\{B_{e,\varphi}^{2}\mathcal{R}_{h}^{\mathrm{weak}},\;2B_{e,h}^{2}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\varphi}^{\mathrm{weak},\hat{r}}+\mathcal{E}_{r}\right)\right\}.

Proof.

By Lemma
H.23
,

f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)-\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[\{\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)-\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\}\{h_{0}(a,X,W)-\hat{h}(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a].

First condition on
(X,Z)
. Since

\mathbb{E}[h_{0}(a,X,W)-\hat{h}(a,X,W)\mid A=a,X,Z]=m_{0}(a,X,Z)-T_{h}\hat{h}(a,X,Z),

we obtain

f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)-\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[\{\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)-\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\}\{m_{0}(a,X,Z)-T_{h}\hat{h}(a,X,Z)\}\mid A=a].

By Cauchy–Schwarz,

\displaystyle|f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)-\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}(a)|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\mathbb{E}[\{\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)-\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\}^{2}\mid A=a]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\mathbb{E}[\{m_{0}(a,X,Z)-T_{h}\hat{h}(a,X,Z)\}^{2}\mid A=a].

Using the definition of
B_{e,\varphi}
and integrating over
a\sim P_{A}
gives

\|\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq B_{e,\varphi}^{2}\mathcal{R}_{h}^{\mathrm{weak}}.

Second condition on
(X,W)
. Since

\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{0}(a,X,Z)-\hat{\varphi}(a,X,Z)\mid A=a,X,W]=r_{0}(a,X,W)-T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}(a,X,W),

we similarly obtain

f_{\mathrm{ATE}}(a)-\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[\{h_{0}(a,X,W)-\hat{h}(a,X,W)\}\{r_{0}(a,X,W)-T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}(a,X,W)\}\mid A=a].

Cauchy–Schwarz and integration over
a\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{A}
give

\|\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq B_{e,h}^{2}\mathcal{R}_{\varphi}^{\mathrm{weak},0}.

Finally,

\mathcal{R}_{\varphi}^{\mathrm{weak},0}=\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-r_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,W})}^{2}\leq 2\|T_{\varphi}\hat{\varphi}-\hat{r}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,W})}^{2}+2\|\hat{r}-r_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A,X,W})}^{2},

which gives the last display.
∎

H.5.0.0.3
Final-stage residual regression.

Define the residual pseudo-outcome

\Gamma_{\mathrm{DR}}:=\hat{\varphi}(A,X,Z)\{Y-\hat{h}(A,X,W)\}.

Then

\kappa_{\hat{h},\hat{\varphi}}(a)=\mathbb{E}[\Gamma_{\mathrm{DR}}\mid A=a].

Let

D_{\mathrm{DR}}=\{(a_{i},\Gamma_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n_{\mathrm{DR}}}

be an independent final-stage sample. Let
\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}
denote the residual-regression sieve index, and let
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}}
be a scalar regression class. Define

\hat{\kappa}_{\mathrm{DR}}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}}}\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{DR}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\mathrm{DR}}}\{\Gamma_{i}-k(a_{i})\}^{2}.

Define the approximation error

\kappa_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}}:=\inf_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}}}\|k-\kappa_{\hat{h},\hat{\varphi}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}.

Define the residual-regression loss class

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}}:=\left\{(\gamma,a)\mapsto(\gamma-k(a))^{2}:k\in\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}}\right\}.

Assumption H.25

(DR final-stage boundedness)
.

Conditional on the trained bridge estimators
(\hat{h},\hat{\varphi})
, assume:

•

The residual-regression loss class is uniformly bounded:

\sup_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}}}|\ell|\leq M_{\mathrm{DR}}

for some finite constant
M_{\mathrm{DR}}
.

•

The final-stage sample
D_{\mathrm{DR}}
is independent of the samples used to train

\hat{h}
,
\hat{r}
, and
\hat{\varphi}
.

Define

\Delta_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}}(\delta):=2\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{D_{\mathrm{DR}}}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}})+3M_{\mathrm{DR}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(2/\delta)}{2n_{\mathrm{DR}}}}.

Lemma H.26

(Final-stage residual-regression bound)
.

Under Assumption
H.25
, conditional on the trained bridge estimators, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\|\hat{\kappa}_{\mathrm{DR}}-\kappa_{\hat{h},\hat{\varphi}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq\kappa_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}}+2\Delta_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}}(\delta).

Proof.

Conditional on
(\hat{h},\hat{\varphi})
, this is ordinary least-squares regression with response

\Gamma_{\mathrm{DR}}
and covariate
A
. Since

\kappa_{\hat{h},\hat{\varphi}}(A)=\mathbb{E}[\Gamma_{\mathrm{DR}}\mid A,\hat{h},\hat{\varphi}],

the least-squares projection identity gives

\mathbb{E}[\{\Gamma_{\mathrm{DR}}-k(A)\}^{2}\mid\hat{h},\hat{\varphi}]-\mathbb{E}[\{\Gamma_{\mathrm{DR}}-\kappa_{\hat{h},\hat{\varphi}}(A)\}^{2}\mid\hat{h},\hat{\varphi}]=\|k-\kappa_{\hat{h},\hat{\varphi}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}.

Applying the uniform deviation inequality to

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}}
and using empirical optimality of

\hat{\kappa}_{\mathrm{DR}}
yields the result.
∎

The DRPCLNET-V1 estimator is

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}(a):=\hat{\mu}_{h}(a)+\hat{\kappa}_{\mathrm{DR}}(a).

Theorem H.27

(DRPCLNET-V1 ATE consistency)
.

Under Assumption
H.25
, on the event of
Lemma
H.26
,

\displaystyle\|\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq\;

\displaystyle 4\mathcal{E}_{\mu,h}+4\kappa_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}}+8\Delta_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}}(\delta)

\displaystyle+2\min\left\{B_{e,\varphi}^{2}\mathcal{R}_{h}^{\mathrm{weak}},\;2B_{e,h}^{2}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\varphi}^{\mathrm{weak},\hat{r}}+\mathcal{E}_{r}\right)\right\}.

Equivalently, using the oracle projected treatment residual,

\|\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq 4\mathcal{E}_{\mu,h}+4\kappa_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}}+8\Delta_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}}(\delta)+2\min\left\{B_{e,\varphi}^{2}\mathcal{R}_{h}^{\mathrm{weak}},\;B_{e,h}^{2}\mathcal{R}_{\varphi}^{\mathrm{weak},0}\right\}.

Consequently, if

\mathcal{E}_{\mu,h}\to 0,\qquad\kappa_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}}\to 0,\qquad\Delta_{\mathrm{DR},\nu_{\mathrm{DR}}}(\delta)\to 0,

and either

\mathcal{R}_{h}^{\mathrm{weak}}\to 0

or

\mathcal{R}_{\varphi}^{\mathrm{weak},\hat{r}}\to 0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{E}_{r}\to 0

in probability, then

\|\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}\to 0.

Proof.

Decompose

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}=(\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}-\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}})+(\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}).

Using
(u+v)^{2}\leq 2u^{2}+2v^{2}
,

\|\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}\leq 2\|\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}-\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}+2\|\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}-f_{\mathrm{ATE}}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{A})}^{2}.

Moreover,

\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}-\bar{f}_{\mathrm{ATE}}^{\mathrm{DR1}}=(\hat{\mu}_{h}-\mu_{\hat{h}})+(\hat{\kappa}_{\mathrm{DR}}-\kappa_{\hat{h},\hat{\varphi}}).

Therefore,
